Imaginary Schur-Weyl duality for quiver Hecke superalgebras Thanks: The author was supported by the NSF grant DMS-2346684.
Alexander Kleshchev

Abstract
. Quiver Hecke super algebras R θ R_{\theta} were defined by Kang-Kashiwara-Tsuchioka and used by Hill-Wang and Kang-Kashiwara-Oh to categorify quantum Kac-Moody superalgebras and by Kang-Kashiwara-Oh to categorufy highest weight modules over quantum Kac-Moody superalgebras. In affine type A p − 1 ( 2 ) A_{p-1}^{(2)} , a Morita equivalence established by Kang, Kashiwara and Tsuchioka connects quiver Hecke superalgebras to blocks of double covers of symmetric and alternating groups in (odd) characteristic p p . These results motivate further study of representation theory of quiver Hecke superalgebras. The irreducible (graded super)modules over quiver Hecke superalgebras can be classified in terms of cuspidal modules, as shown by the author and Livesey. To every indivisible positive root α \alpha of type A p − 1 ( 2 ) A_{p-1}^{(2)} and a non-negative integer d d , one associates an explicit quotient R ¯ d ​ α \bar{R}_{d\alpha} of R d ​ α R_{d\alpha} called the cuspidal algebra. If the root α \alpha is real, the cuspidal algebra R ¯ d ​ α \bar{R}_{d\alpha} is well-understood and has a unique irreducible module called a (real) irreducible cuspidal module. But if α = δ \alpha=\delta , the imaginary null-root, the corresponding imaginary cuspidal algebra R ¯ d ​ δ \bar{R}_{d\delta} is still rather mysterious. For example, it has only been known that the number of the isomorphism classes of the irreducible R ¯ d ​ δ \bar{R}_{d\delta} -modules equals the number of the ℓ \ell -multipartitions of d d , but there has been no way to canonically associate an irreducible R ¯ d ​ δ \bar{R}_{d\delta} -module to such a multipartiton. The imaginary cuspidal algebra is especially important because of its connections to the RoCK blocks of the double covers of symmetric and alternating groups established by the author and Livesey. In this paper we undertake a detailed study of the imaginary cuspidal algebra R ¯ d ​ δ \bar{R}_{d\delta} and its representation theory. We use the so-called Gelfand-Graev idempotents and subtle degree and parity shifts to construct a (graded) Morita (super)equivalent algebra 𝖢 ⁡ ( n , d ) {\mathsf{C}}(n,d) (for any n ≥ d n\geq d ). The advantage of the algebra 𝖢 ⁡ ( n , d ) {\mathsf{C}}(n,d) is that, unlike R ¯ d ​ δ \bar{R}_{d\delta} , it is non-negatively graded. Moreover, the degree zero component 𝖢 ​ ( n , d ) 0 {\mathsf{C}}(n,d)^{0} is shown to be isomorphic to the direct sum of tensor products of ℓ \ell copies of the classical Schur algebras. Since the irreducible modules over a non-negatively graded algebra are lifts of the irreducible modules over its degree zero component, this immediately gives the classification (and the description of dimensions/characters, etc.) of the irreducible 𝖢 ⁡ ( n , d ) {\mathsf{C}}(n,d) -modules, and hence of the irreducible R ¯ d ​ δ \bar{R}_{d\delta} -modules, in terms of the classical Schur algebras. In particular, this allows us to canonically label these by the ℓ \ell -multipartitions of d d . The results of this paper will be used crucially in our future work on RoCK blocks of the double covers of symmetric and alternating groups. In particular, this will lead to a ‘local’ description, up to Morita equivalence, of arbitrary defect RoCK blocks of these groups in terms of generalized Schur algebras corresponding to an explicit Brauer tree algebra. In view of the recent results on Broué’s Conjecture for these groups, this will provide a ‘local’ description of an arbitrary block of the double covers of symmetric and alternating groups up to derived equivalence.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
20C20, 20C25, 20C30, 18N25

Chapter 1
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation and general description of main results

1.1a.
Motivation

Quiver Hecke
super
algebras were defined by Kang, Kashiwara and Tsuchioka
[
26
]
and used by Hill and Wang
[
21
]
and Kang, Kashiwara and Oh
[
27
,
28
]
to categorify quantum Kac-Moody superalgebras, and by Kang, Kashiwara and Oh
[
27
,
28
]
to categorify highest weight modules over quantum Kac-Moody superalgebras.

In affine type
A_{p-1}^{(2)}
, a Morita equivalence
established by Kang, Kashiwara and Tsuchioka
[
26
]
connects cyclotomic quotients of quiver Hecke superalgebras to blocks of double covers of symmetric and alternating groups in (odd) characteristic
p
and related Hecke superalgebras. This is analogous to the connection between cyclotomic quotients of quiver Hecke algebras (Khovanov-Lauda-Rouquier algebras) and blocks of symmetric groups and Hecke algebras established in
[
6
,
40
]
.

Further tight connections between the imaginary cuspidal representations of quiver Hecke superalgebras and RoCK blocks of the double covers of symmetric and alternating groups were established in
[
33
]
, leading eventually to the proof of Broué’s Abelian Defect Conjecture for these groups
[
14
,
7
]
.

So it is important to further study representation theory of quiver Hecke superalgebras.

1.1b.
Quiver Hecke superalgebras

We fix a ground field
\mathbb{F}
. In the main body of the paper we often work over more general ground rings
\Bbbk
, but in this introduction we concentrate on the case
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
.
We will consider the quiver Hecke superalgebras of Lie type
A_{2\ell}^{(2)}
over
\mathbb{F}
. Let
Q_{+}
be the non-negative part of the root lattice in type
A_{2\ell}^{(2)}
and
\Phi_{+}
be the system of positive roots, see §
2.1d
for details. Of particular importance is the imaginary null-root
\delta\in\Phi_{+}
.

To every
\theta\in Q_{+}
, following
[
26
]
, we associate a graded
\mathbb{F}
-superalgebra
R_{\theta}
called the quiver Hecke superalgebra, see §
3.3
. We will be working in the category
R_{\theta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
of finitely generated graded supermodules over
R_{\theta}
. (It is known that the irreducible graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodules are finite-dimensional and irreducible as usual
R_{\theta}
-modules, so one might expect that the grading and the superstructure should not be essential when we consider the irreducible modules but this is not the case.)

1.1c.
Cuspidal modules and cuspidal algebras

The irreducible graded supermodules over quiver Hecke superalgebras were classified in terms of cuspidal irreducible modules in
[
33
]
. The theory is reviewed in §
4.1a
.
To every indivisible positive root
\alpha\in\Phi_{+}
and
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, one associates an explicit quotient
\bar{R}_{d\alpha}
of
R_{d\alpha}
called the cuspidal algebra.
The modules over
\bar{R}_{d\alpha}
inflated to
R_{d\alpha}
(as
\alpha
and
d
vary) are called the cuspidal modules. Arbitrary irreducible
R_{\theta}
-modules can be classified in terms of the cuspidal irreducible modules, see Theorem
4.1.5
.

If the positive root
\alpha
is real, the corresponding
real cuspidal algebra

\bar{R}_{d\alpha}
is well-understood and has a unique irreducible module. In this way, as
\alpha
varies over the real positive roots and
d
varies over positive integers, we obtain the (well-understood)
real cuspidal irreducible modules
over quiver Hecke superalgebras.

But if
\alpha=\delta
, the corresponding
imaginary cuspidal algebras

\bar{R}_{d\delta}
are still rather mysterious. The modules over quiver Hecke superalgebras arising by inflation from the imaginary cuspidal algebras are referred to as the
imaginary cuspidal modules
. As a consequence of the Kang-Kashawara-Oh categorification theorem, it was proved in
[
33
]
that
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
has exactly
|{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)|
irreducible modules (up to isomorphism), where

J\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{0,1,\dots,\ell-1\}

and
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
is the set of
J
-multipartitions of
d
.
However, in
[
33
]
, we were unable to canonically label the irreducible
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
-modules by such multipartitons.

1.1d.
Imaginary cuspidal algebras and classical Schur algebras

In this paper we undertake a detailed study of the imaginary cuspidal algebra
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
and its representation theory.
For any
n\geq d
, we use the so-called Gelfand-Graev idempotents and subtle
degree and parity shifts
to construct graded superalgebras
{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)
which are (graded) Morita (super)equivalent to
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
. The advantage of the algebras
{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)
is that, unlike
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
, they are
non-negatively graded
. Moreover, for the degree zero component
{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)^{0}
we obtain the algebra isomorphism

{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)^{0}\cong\bigoplus_{d_{0}+\dots+d_{\ell-1}=d}S(n,d_{0})\otimes\dots\otimes S(n,d_{\ell-1}),

where
S(n,d)
denotes the
classical Schur algebra
as in
[
19
]
.
Since the irreducible modules over the non-negatively graded algebra
{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)
are lifts of the irreducible modules over its degree zero component, this immediately gives the classification (and the description of dimensions/characters, etc.) of the irreducible
{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)
-modules, and hence, by Morita equivalence, of the irreducible
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
-modules, in terms of the classical Schur algebras. In particular, this allows us to canonically label these by the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
.

1.1e.
Connection to RoCK blocks of double covers of symmetric groups

The imaginary cuspidal algebras
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
are especially important because of their connections to the
RoCK blocks
of the double covers of symmetric and alternating groups established by the author and Livesey
[
33
]
. In fact, the results of this paper will be used crucially in our forthcoming work
[
32
]
which gives a ‘local’ description, up to Morita equivalence, of
arbitrary defect
RoCK blocks of the double covers in terms of generalized Schur algebras corresponding to an explicit Brauer tree algebra. In view of the recent results on Broué’s Abelian Defect Conjecture for these groups
[
14
,
7
]
, this will provide a ‘local’ description of an arbitrary block of the double covers of symmetric and alternating groups up to derived equivalence. This will also allow us to describe the decomposition numbers of arbitrary defect RoCK blocks in terms of Littlewood-Richardson coefficients, inverse Kostka polynomials and decomposition numbers of the classical Schur algebras, generalizing
[
18
]
where the abelian defect case was considered.

1.2.
Contents of the paper and statements of the main results

Let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We work with the imaginary cuspidal algebra
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
. Note that in the main body of the paper we have used the notation
\hat{C}_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bar{R}_{d\delta}
.

1.2a.
Combinatorial sets

The following combinatorial sets will be used:

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda(n,d)
of all
compositions of
d
with
n
parts
; the elements of
\Uplambda(n,d)
are the tuples
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})
of non-negative integers such that
|\mu|\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mu_{1}+\dots+\mu_{n}=d
;

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda(d)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\Uplambda(n,d)
of all
compositions of
d
; note that the union is not disjoint since we identify the compositions
\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d)
and
\nu\in\Uplambda(m,d)
for
m>n
if
\nu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n},0,\dots,0)
; we have the usual
dominance order

\unlhd
on the set
\Uplambda(d)
;

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)
of
J
-multicompositions of
d
with
n
parts
; the elements of
\Uplambda^{J}(d)
are the tuples
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}=(\mu^{(0)},\dots,\mu^{(\ell-1)})
of compositions with
n
parts such that
|\mu^{(0)}|+\dots+|\mu^{(\ell-1)}|=d
.

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)\subseteq\Uplambda(n,d)
of all
essential compositions of
d
with
n
parts
, i.e. those

\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d)
with
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}\neq 0
;

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda_{0}(d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)=\bigsqcup_{n=1}^{d}\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)
of all
essential compositions of
d
;

\bullet

the set
{\mathcal{P}}(d)
of all
partitions of
d
, i.e. compositions of
d
with weakly decreasing parts;

\bullet

the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
of
J
-multipartitions of
d
; the elements of
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
are the tuples
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}=(\mu^{(0)},\dots,\mu^{(\ell-1)})
of partitions with
|\mu^{(0)}|+\dots+|\mu^{(\ell-1)}|=d
.

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
of all
colored compositions of
d
with
n
parts
, i.e. all pairs
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})
with
\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d)
and
\text{\boldmath$j$}=(j_{1},\cdots,j_{n})\in J^{n}
an
n
-tuple of elements of
J
usually written as
\text{\boldmath$j$}=j_{1}\cdots j_{n}
;

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
of all
colored compositions of
d
;

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)\mid\mu\in\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)\}
of
essential colored compositions
of
d
with
n
parts;

\bullet

the set
\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)=\bigsqcup_{n=1}^{d}\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
of
essential colored compositions of
d
.

1.2b.
Cuspidal parabolic subalgebras

For
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\Uplambda(d)
, we have an idempotent
1_{\lambda\delta}\in\bar{R}_{d\delta}
and the cuspidal parabolic subalgebra

\bar{R}_{\lambda_{1}\delta}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bar{R}_{\lambda_{n}\delta}\cong\bar{R}_{\lambda\delta}\subseteq 1_{\lambda\delta}\bar{R}_{d\delta}1_{\lambda\delta},

see (
4.1.12
). This yields the functors of parabolic induction and restriction

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\bar{R}_{\lambda\delta}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to\bar{R}_{d\delta}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ V\mapsto\bar{R}_{d\delta}1_{\lambda\delta}\otimes_{\bar{R}_{\lambda\delta}}V,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\bar{R}_{d\delta}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to\bar{R}_{\lambda\delta}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ W\mapsto 1_{\lambda\delta}\bar{R}_{d\delta}\otimes_{\bar{R}_{d\delta}}W,

cf. Lemmas
4.1.8
,
4.1.9
.

1.2c.
Gelfand-Graev idempotents

In this paper we introduce certain remarkable idempotents in
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
which we call
Gelfand-Graev idempotents
(generalizing the class of Gelfand-Graev idempotents used in
[
33
]
).

To every colored composition
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(d)
we associate the explicit Gelfand-Graev idempotent

{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\in\bar{R}_{d\delta},

see (
4.2.9
). Then the idempotents
\{{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}
are distinct and orthogonal to each other, hence

(1.2.1)

{f}_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)}{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\in\bar{R}_{d\delta}

is also an idempotent. So we can consider the
Gelfand-Graev idempotent truncation

C_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}_{d}\bar{R}_{d\delta}{f}_{d}.

Theorem A.

The graded superalgebras
C_{d}
and
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
are graded Morita superequivalent.

In other words,
C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}
and
\bar{R}_{d\delta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
are equivalent as graded supercategories, see §
2.2d
. Theorem A is proved in Corollary
6.2.9
.

For a finite dimensional graded
C_{d}
-supermodule
V
, in view of (
1.2.1
), we have

V=\bigoplus_{(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)}{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}V.

We introduce the
Gelfand-Graev formal character
of a finite dimensional graded
C_{d}
-supermodule
V
:

\operatorname{GGCh}(V)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)}(\dim{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}V)\cdot(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\mathbb{Z}\cdot\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d).

where
\mathbb{Z}\cdot\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)
is the free
\mathbb{Z}
-module on the basis
\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)
.

1.2d.
Parabolic subalgebras and Gelfand-Graev induction

For a composition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\Uplambda(d)
, we also have the Gelfand-Graev idempotent in the parabolic subalgebra
\bar{R}_{\lambda\delta}

{f}_{\lambda}={f}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes{f}_{\lambda_{n}}\in\bar{R}_{\lambda_{1}\delta}\otimes\cdots\otimes\bar{R}_{\lambda_{n}\delta}=\bar{R}_{\lambda\delta}\subseteq 1_{\lambda\delta}\bar{R}_{d\delta}1_{\lambda\delta},

which yields the Gelfand-Graev parabolic subalgebra

C_{\lambda}=C_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes C_{\lambda_{n}}\subseteq{f}_{\lambda}C_{d}{f}_{\lambda}

and the functors of Gelfand-Graev parabolic induction and restriction

\displaystyle{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ V\mapsto C_{d}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{d}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ W\mapsto{f}_{\lambda}C_{d}\otimes_{C_{d}}W.

Under the Morita equivalence of Theorem A, these correspond to the functors
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}
and
{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}
, respectively, see (
4.2.25
).

For the special composition
\omega_{d}=(1^{d})\in\Uplambda(d)
, the idempotent truncation
{f}_{\omega_{d}}C_{d}{f}_{\omega_{d}}
was studied in
[
33
]
. We have constructed an explicit isomorphism

(1.2.2)

{f}_{\omega_{d}}C_{d}{f}_{\omega_{d}}\cong H_{d}(A_{\ell}),

where
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
is the
affine Brauer tree superalgebra
corresponding to the
graded Brauer tree superalgebra
A_{\ell}
, see §
3.2b
and Theorem
4.2.47
. Moreover, it was even proved that
{f}_{\omega_{d}}\bar{R}_{d\delta}{f}_{\omega_{d}}={f}_{\omega_{d}}C_{d}{f}_{\omega_{d}}
is graded Morita superequivalent to
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
provided
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{F}=0
or
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{F}>p
. However, in the case
0<\operatorname{char}\mathbb{F}\leq p
, which is crucial for modular representation theory, the algebras
{f}_{\omega_{d}}\bar{R}_{d\delta}{f}_{\omega_{d}}
and
\bar{R}_{d\delta}
are
not
Morita equivalent. This explains the necessity of using the more general Gelfand-Graev idempotent
{f}_{d}
, see Theorem A.

As a special case of (
1.2.2
), we get the following explicit description of the Gelfand-Graev truncated imaginary cuspidal algebra in rank
1
:

C_{1}\cong H_{1}(A_{\ell})\cong A_{\ell}[z],

where
A_{\ell}[z]
is a twisted polynomial superalgebra in the even indeterminate
z
of degree
4
with coefficients in
A_{\ell}
, see §
3.2b
.
This immediately yields the explicit construction of the irreducible modules over
C_{1}\cong A_{\ell}[z]
:

{\mathrm{Irr}}(C_{1})=\{L_{j}\mid j\in J\},

where each
L_{j}
is
1
-dimensional spanned by a vector
v_{j}
and the action of the generators of
A_{\ell}[z]
on
v_{j}
is described explicitly, see §
4.2h
.

1.2e.
Imaginary tensor spaces

To understand the irreducible modules over
C_{d}
we introduce the
colored imaginary tensor spaces

M_{d,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathrm{GGI}}_{\omega_{d}}^{d}L_{j}^{\boxtimes d}=C_{d}{f}_{\omega_{d}}\otimes_{C_{\omega_{d}}}L_{j}^{\boxtimes d}\qquad(j\in J).

with generator
v_{d,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}_{\omega_{d}}\otimes v_{j}^{\otimes d}
.

Fix
j\in J
. For the special essential colored composition
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
with
j_{1}=\dots=j_{n}=j
, we denote

(1.2.3)

(\mu,j)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}).

We then have

(1.2.4)

M_{d,j}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Uplambda_{0}(d)}{f}^{\mu,j}M_{d,j},

see Lemma
6.1.19
.

Theorem B.

Let
j\in J
. For every
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(d)
there exists a unique up to isomorphism irreducible graded
C_{d}
-supermodule
L_{j}(\mu)
such that
L_{j}(\mu)
is a composition factor of
M_{d,j}
and

\operatorname{GGCh}(L_{j}(\mu))=(\mu,j)+\sum_{\nu\in\Uplambda_{0}(d),\,\nu\lhd\mu}b_{\nu,j}\cdot(\nu,j)

for some
b_{\nu,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Moreover,
\{L_{j}(\mu)\mid\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}(d)\}
is a complete and non-redundant set of the composition factors of
M_{d,j}
up to isomorphism.

For
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}=(\mu^{(0)},\dots,\mu^{(\ell-1)})\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
with
d_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=|\mu^{(j)}|
for all
j\in J
, we define

(1.2.5)

L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathrm{GGI}}^{d}_{d_{0},\dots,d_{\ell-1}}\big(L_{0}(\mu^{(0)})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes L_{\ell-1}(\mu^{(\ell-1)})\big).

Theorem C.

We have that
\{L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mid\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)\}
is a complete and non-redundant set of the irreducible graded
C_{d}
-supermodules up to isomorphism.

Theorems B and C are proved in Theorem
6.2.7
.

1.2f.
Imaginary Schur-Weyl and Howe dualities

To prove Theorems B and C and to further study representation theory of
C_{d}
, we use a Schur-Weyl type duality between
C_{d}
and the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
on
d
letters. We then upgrade it to a Howe type duality between
C_{d}
and the classical Schur algebra.

Fix
j\in J
.
The isomorphism (
1.2.2
) allows us to define the explicit right action of
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
on
M_{d,j}
. Let

{\bar{C}}_{d,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{d}/{\mathrm{Ann}}_{C_{d}}(M_{d,j}).

The following double centralizer property is proved in Theorem
8.2.17
(ii) (‘
{\mathrm{sop}}
’ stands for the opposite superalgebra, and we always consider
\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
as a graded superalgebra in the trivial way):

Theorem D (Imaginary Schur-Weyl Duality).

Let
j\in J
. Then we have
\operatorname{End}_{{\bar{C}}_{d,j}}(M_{d,j})^{\mathrm{sop}}\cong\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
and
\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}}(M_{d,j})\cong{\bar{C}}_{d,j}
.

We prove in Theorem
7.1.6
that
M_{d,j}
is projective as a graded supermodule over
{\bar{C}}_{d,j}
; however, it is not in general a projective generator. To define a projective generator we use the action of
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
on
M_{d,j}
to define the graded
{\bar{C}}_{d,j}
-supermodules

\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{m\in M_{d,j}\mid mg=m\ \text{for all}\ g\in\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\}\qquad(\lambda\in\Uplambda(d))

(the analogues of tensor products of divided powers of the natural module over the general linear group).
Here
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
denotes the standard parabolic subgroup
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda_{1}}\times\dots\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda_{n}}\leq\mathfrak{S}_{d}
for
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\Uplambda(d)
.
Recall that
S(n,d)
denotes the classical Schur algebra.

Theorem E (Imaginary Howe Duality).

Let
j\in J
. Then:

(i)

for any
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
we have
\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}\cong{\bar{C}}_{d,j}{f}^{\lambda,j}
;

(ii)

there is an explicit algebra isomorphism

\textstyle S(n,d)\cong\operatorname{End}_{{\bar{C}}_{d,j}}\big(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)}\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}\big);

(iii)

if
n\geq d
then
\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)}\Gamma_{j}^{\lambda}
is a projective generator for
{\bar{C}}_{d,j}
.

Part (i) is proved in Corollary
8.1.25
, part (ii) in Theorem
7.3.4
, and part (iii) in Theorem
8.1.21
(iii).

If
n\geq d
, Theorem E yields an equivalence

{\mathpzc{b}}_{n,d,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S(n,d)\!\operatorname{-mod}\to{\bar{C}}_{d,j}\!\operatorname{-mod}.

It is well-known
[
19
]
that the irreducible modules over
S(n,d)
are canonically labeled by their highest weights which are elements of
{\mathcal{P}}(d)
:

\{L_{n,d}(\lambda)\mid\lambda\in{\mathcal{P}}(d)\},

Then
L_{j}(\lambda)\cong{\mathpzc{b}}_{n,d,j}(L(\lambda))
, see Lemma
8.2.3
.

There is an even more direct connection between
C_{d}
and the classical Schur algebra. To explain this, we need to introduce an important regrading of
C_{d}
.

1.2g.
Regrading truncated cuspidal algebras

For
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
, set

\displaystyle t_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=d(2\ell+1)+\sum_{s=1}^{n}\mu_{s}^{2}(2j_{s}-4\ell)\in\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle{\varepsilon}_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{s=1}^{n}\mu_{s}j_{s}\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }2)\,\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

and consider the new graded superalgebra

(1.2.6)

{\mathsf{C}}_{d}=\bigoplus_{(\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$}),(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{t_{\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$}}-t_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}_{\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$}}-{\varepsilon}_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}}{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}C_{d}{f}^{\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$}}.

Here
{\mathpzc{Q}}
is the degree shift and
\Uppi
is the parity shift; thus,
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
is the same algebra as
C_{d}
but with a different grading (
\mathbb{Z}
-grading) and a different superstructure (
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading), see §
5.1a
for details.

We denote by
{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
the element corresponding to an element
c\in C_{d}
; for example, we have the idempotents
\mathsf{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
and
\mathsf{f}_{\lambda}
in
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
corresponding to the idempotents
{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
and
{f}_{\lambda}
in
C_{d}
, respectively. We also have the parabolic
{\mathsf{C}}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathsf{f}_{\lambda}{\mathsf{C}}_{d}\mathsf{f}_{\lambda}
.

The graded superalgebras
C_{d}
and
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
are graded Morita superequivalent.
in fact, given a graded
C_{d}
-supermodule
V
, the
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
-module corresponding to
V
under the graded Morita superequivalence of
C_{d}
and
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
is

{\mathsf{V}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{(\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)}^{n}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{-t_{\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$}}}\Uppi^{-{\varepsilon}_{\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$}}}{f}^{\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$}}V;

thus
{\mathsf{V}}=V
as a vector space but has a different grading and superstructure and
{\mathsf{c}}
acts on
{\mathsf{V}}
in the same way as
c
acts on
V
for any
c\in C_{d}
. The vector of
{\mathsf{V}}
corresponding to
v\in V
is denoted by
{\mathsf{v}}
. In particular, we have the imaginary tensor spaces
{\mathsf{M}}_{d,j}
over
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
with generator
{\mathsf{v}}_{d,j}
, obtained by regrading of
M_{d,j}
. The right
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
-module structure is then inherited from that on
M_{d,j}
, see §
1.2f
.
The decomposition (
1.2.4
) becomes

(1.2.7)

{\mathsf{M}}_{d,j}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\Uplambda_{0}(d)}\mathsf{f}^{\mu,j}{\mathsf{M}}_{d,j},

While the graded superalgebras
C_{d}
and
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
are graded Morita superequivalent, this subtle ‘regrading’ happens to be very useful. The corresponding regrading is also crucial when dealing with RoCK blocks of double covers of symmetric groups, see
[
32
]
. The major advantage of
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
over
C_{d}
is that it is non-negatively graded:

Theorem F.

The algebra
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
is non-negatively graded.

Theorem F is proved in Theorem
5.2.23
.

1.2h.
The elements
\mathsf{g}_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
and
g_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}

A key role in this paper is played by certain explicit elements
g_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\in C_{d}
and the corresponding elements
\mathsf{g}_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
defined in §
5.2b
for any
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(d)
. Recalling the notation (
1.2.3
), in the special case where
j_{1}=\dots=j_{d}=j
we have the elements
g_{\mu,j}\in C_{d}
and
\mathsf{g}_{\mu,j}\in{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
.
The introduction of the elements
\mathsf{g}_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
can be motivated by the following remarkable properties:

Theorem G.

Let
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
and
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
.

(i)

The element
\mathsf{g}_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
is even and of degree
0
.

(ii)

The space
\mathsf{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}{\mathsf{C}}_{\mu}^{0}\mathsf{f}^{\omega_{d},j_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots j_{n}^{\mu_{n}}}
is
1
-dimensional and spanned by
\mathsf{g}_{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
.

(iii)

There is an even degree
0
isomorphism of right
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
-modules

\mathsf{f}^{\lambda,j}{\mathsf{M}}_{d,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Bbbk_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\otimes_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}

which maps
\mathsf{g}_{\lambda,j}{\mathsf{v}}_{d,j}
to
1\otimes 1_{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}
, where the right permutation module
\Bbbk_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\otimes_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
is purely even and concentrated in degree
0
.

Note that part (ii) of the theorem, which is proved in Lemma
5.2.34
, already determines the elements
\mathsf{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
uniquely up to a scalar.
Part (i) is proved in Lemma
5.2.33
.
Part (iii) is proved in Proposition
9.1.9
.

We now consider the graded superalgebra

{\mathsf{C}}_{j}(n,d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{d}}\Big(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)}{\mathsf{C}}_{d}\mathsf{f}^{\lambda,j}\Big)^{\mathrm{sop}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d)}\mathsf{f}^{\mu,j}{\mathsf{C}}_{d}\mathsf{f}^{\lambda,j}.

The action of
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
on
{\mathsf{M}}_{d,j}
yields the action of
{\mathsf{C}}_{j}(n,d)
on

{\mathsf{M}}_{j}(n,d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)}\mathsf{f}^{\lambda,j}{\mathsf{M}}_{d,j}.

By Theorem G, as graded right
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
-supermodules,

{\mathsf{M}}_{j}(n,d)\simeq\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)}\Bbbk_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\otimes_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}.

So by definition of the classical Schur algebra,

\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}}({\mathsf{M}}_{j}(n,d))=S(n,d).

On the other hand, the action of
{\mathsf{C}}_{j}(n,d)
on
{\mathsf{M}}_{j}(n,d)
gives the graded superalgebra homomorphism

{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\mathsf{C}}_{j}(n,d)\to\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}}({\mathsf{M}}_{j}(n,d)).

Theorem H.

Let
j\in J
and
n\geq d
. Then the restriction of
{\varphi}
to the degree zero composnent is an algebra isomorphism

{\mathsf{C}}_{j}(n,d)^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}S(n,d)
.

Theorem H is proved in Theorem
9.1.11
.

1.2i.
The algebra
{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)

For a
J
-multicomposition
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)
we denote

{\underline{d}}(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})=(|\mu^{(0)}|,\dots,|\mu^{(\ell-1)}|);

this is an element of the set
\Uplambda(J,d)
of all tuples
{\underline{d}}=(d_{0},\dots,d_{\ell-1})
of non-negative integers with
d_{0}+\dots+d_{\ell-1}=d
.

There is an embedding

{\mathtt{b}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)\to\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n\ell,d),\ \text{\boldmath$\mu$}\mapsto({\mathtt{a}}(\text{\boldmath$\mu$}),\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(n)})

where

{\mathtt{a}}(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})=(\mu^{(0)}_{1},\dots,\mu^{(0)}_{n},\dots,\mu^{(\ell-1)}_{1},\dots,\mu^{(\ell-1)}_{n})\quad\text{and}\quad\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=0^{n}1^{n}\cdots(\ell-1)^{n}\in J^{n\ell}

(informally, read the parts of the compositions
\mu^{(0)},\dots,\mu^{(\ell-1)}
and record the corresponding colors), see (
2.1.16
).
We will consider the multicompositions in
\Lambda^{J}(n,d)
as the colored compositions in
\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n\ell,d)
via this embedding. In particular, we now have the idempotents
\mathsf{f}^{\text{\boldmath$\mu$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathsf{f}^{{\mathtt{b}}(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})}
for any
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)
.

We consider the
graded superalgebra

{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{d}}\Big(\bigoplus_{\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}\in\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)}{\mathsf{C}}_{d}\mathsf{f}^{\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}}\Big)^{\mathrm{sop}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\text{\boldmath$\lambda$},\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)}\mathsf{f}^{\text{\boldmath$\mu$}}{\mathsf{C}}_{d}\mathsf{f}^{\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}}.

If
n\geq d
,
this
is just a Morita equivalent version of
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
, see Lemma
9.1.12
:

Theorem I.

If
n\geq d
,
then
{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)
and
{\mathsf{C}}_{d}
are graded Morita superequivalent.

For
{\underline{d}}\in\Uplambda(J,d)
, we also have the graded superalgebra

{\mathsf{C}}(n,{\underline{d}})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{End}_{{\mathsf{C}}_{d}}\Big(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}\in\Uplambda^{J}(n,d),\\
{\underline{d}}(\text{\boldmath$\lambda$})={\underline{d}}\end{subarray}}{\mathsf{C}}_{d}\mathsf{f}^{\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}}\Big)^{\mathrm{sop}}\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\boldmath$\lambda$},\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in\Uplambda^{J}(n,d),\\
{\underline{d}}(\text{\boldmath$\lambda$})={\underline{d}}(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})={\underline{d}}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{f}^{\text{\boldmath$\mu$}}{\mathsf{C}}_{d}\mathsf{f}^{\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}}.

Theorem J.

We have for the degree zero components:

(i)

{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)^{0}=\bigoplus_{{\underline{d}}\in\Uplambda(J,d)}{\mathsf{C}}(n,{\underline{d}})^{0}
;

(ii)

for each
{\underline{d}}\in\Uplambda(J,d)
, we have

{\mathsf{C}}(n,{\underline{d}})^{0}\cong{\mathsf{C}}_{0}(n,d_{0})^{0}\otimes\dots\otimes{\mathsf{C}}_{\ell-1}(n,d_{\ell-1})^{0}
;

In particular, taking into account Theorem H,

{\mathsf{C}}(n,d)^{0}\cong\bigoplus_{{\underline{d}}=(d_{0},\dots,d_{\ell-1})\in\Uplambda(J,d)}S(n,d_{0})\otimes\dots\otimes S(n,d_{\ell-1}).

Theorem J is proved in Lemmas
9.1.13
,
9.1.14
and Corollary
9.1.15

Chapter 2
Background and generalities

2.1.
Combinatorial and Lie theoretic notation

2.1a.
General conventions

We will work either over a principal ideal domain
\mathcal{O}
of characteristic
0
or over a field
\mathbb{F}
of arbitrary characteristic. We denote by
\Bbbk
either
\mathcal{O}
or
\mathbb{F}
.
We denote
\mathbb{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathbb{N}_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and write
\mathbb{Z}/2=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\{{\bar{0}},{\bar{1}}\}
.

Throughout the paper we fix

\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and denote

(2.1.1)

\displaystyle p\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=2\ell+1,\quad I\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{0,1,\dots,\ell\}\quad\text{and}\quad J\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{0,1,\dots,\ell-1\}.

We identify
I
with the set of vertices of a certain Dynkin diagram, see §
2.1d
.
For
i\in I
, we define
\|i\|\in\mathbb{Z}/2
as follows

(2.1.2)

\|i\|\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\bar{1}}&\hbox{if $i=0$,}\\
{\bar{0}}&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Throughout the paper,
q
is an indeterminate and
\pi
is an indeterminate satisfying
\pi^{2}=1
. To be more precise, we will work with the ring

\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]/(\pi^{2}-1),

as well as the rings

\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}]
of Laurent polynomials in
q
and

\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))
of Laurent series in
q
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}
.
We have the
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}
-linear involution

(2.1.3)

\bar{}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}]\to\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}],\ q^{n}\mapsto q^{-n}

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we will use the following elements of
\mathbb{Z}[q,q^{-1}]\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}]
:

(2.1.4)

[n]_{q^{k}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\frac{q^{kn}-q^{-kn}}{q^{k}-q^{-k}},\quad[n]_{q^{k}}^{!}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[1]_{q^{k}}[2]_{q^{k}}\cdots[n]_{q^{k}}.

For
i\in I
, we define

(2.1.5)

q_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}q&\hbox{if $i=0$,}\\
q^{2}&\hbox{if $0<i<\ell$,}\\
q^{4}&\hbox{if $i=\ell$.}\end{array}\right.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the following elements of
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}]

(2.1.6)

\begin{split}[n]_{i}&\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}[n]_{q_{i}}&\hbox{if $i\neq 0$,}\\
\frac{(q\pi)^{n}-q^{-n}}{q\pi-q^{-1}}&\hbox{if $i=0$,}\end{array}\right.\\
[n]_{i}^{!}&\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[1]_{i}[2]_{i}\cdots[n]_{i}.\end{split}

Note that

(2.1.7)

\overline{[n]_{i}}=[n]_{i}\qquad(\text{for}\ i>0)

but in general
\overline{[n]_{0}}\neq[n]_{0}
. In fact,
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(2.1.8)

\overline{[2n]_{0}^{!}}=\pi^{n}[2n]_{0}^{!}.

For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we write elements of
I^{d}
as
\text{\boldmath$i$}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})
or
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}\cdots i_{d}
, with
i_{1},\dots,i_{d}\in I
, and refer to them as
words with entries in
I
. Given words
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{d}
and
\text{\boldmath$j$}\in I^{c}
, we have the concatenation word
\text{\boldmath$i$}\text{\boldmath$j$}\in I^{d+c}
. Similarly, we have the set of words
J^{d}
with entries in
J
.

For for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, we will consider the
integral segments

(2.1.9)

[a,b]\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}.

Given integral segments
{\tt X}
and
{\tt X}_{1},\dots,{\tt X}_{k}
, we say that
{\tt X}
is an
increasing disjoint union
of
{\tt X}_{1},\dots,{\tt X}_{k}
if
{\tt X}={\tt X}_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup{\tt X}_{k}
and
a<b
whenever
a\in{\tt X}_{r}
,
b\in{\tt X}_{s}
and
r<s
.

2.1b.
Partitions and compositions

A
composition
is an infinite sequence
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)
of non-negative integers which are eventually zero. We write
\varnothing
for the composition
(0,0,\dots)
.
When writing compositions, we may collect consecutive equal parts together with a superscript, and omit an infinite tail of
0
’s.
For example, for
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we have the composition

(2.1.10)

\omega_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(1^{d}).

Given compositions
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
and
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m})
, we say that
\lambda
is a
refinement
of
\mu
if there exist

0=k_{0}\leq k_{1}\leq\dots\leq k_{m-1}\leq k_{m}=n
such that

\mu_{s}=\sum_{r=k_{s-1}+1}^{k_{s}}\lambda_{r}
for all
s=1,\dots,m
.

Any composition
\lambda
has finite sum
|\lambda|\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\dots
, and we say that
\lambda
is a composition of
|\lambda|
. For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
\Uplambda(d)
for the set of all compositions of
d
.
The standard
dominance order
on
\Uplambda(d)
is denoted
\unlhd
, see
[
23
, (1.4.6)]
. For
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)\in\Uplambda(d)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
we denote

(2.1.11)

k\lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(k\lambda_{1},k\lambda_{2},\dots)\in\Uplambda(kd).

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

\displaystyle\Uplambda(n,d)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)\in\Uplambda(d)\mid\lambda_{k}=0\ \text{for all $k>n$}\},

If
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
we often write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
.

For
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
we have segments

(2.1.12)

{\tt P}^{\lambda}_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{r-1}+1,\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{r}],\qquad(1\leq r\leq n).

Then
[1,d]
is an increasing disjoint union of
{\tt P}^{\lambda}_{1},\dots,{\tt P}^{\lambda}_{n}
.

A composition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)
is called
essential
if
\lambda_{k}=0
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
implies that
\lambda_{l}=0
for all
l\geq k
. We denote by
\Uplambda_{0}(d)
the set of all essential compositions of
d
. When we write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
for
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{0}(d)
, we always suppose that
\lambda_{n}>0
, in which case we also write
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)\subseteq\Uplambda(n,d)
.
Note that
\Uplambda_{0}(d)=\bigsqcup_{n=1}^{d}\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)
.
Given
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)
and
\mu\in\Uplambda_{0}(m,c)
we have the
concatenation

\lambda\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n},\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m})\in\Uplambda_{0}(n+m,c+d).

Occasionally we will also use the set of compositions

(2.1.13)

\Uplambda(J,d)=\{{\underline{d}}=(d_{0},\dots,d_{\ell-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\mid d_{0}+\dots+d_{\ell-1}=d\}.

A
partition
is a composition whose parts are weakly decreasing. We write

{\mathcal{P}}(d)
for the set of partitions of
d
. We set

\displaystyle\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Uplambda(n,d)\cap{\mathcal{P}}.

We denote by
\lambda^{\prime}
the
transposed partition
of a partition
\lambda
, see
[
22
, 3.5]
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
denote the set of all
J
-multipartitions
of
d
. So the elements of
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
are tuples

\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}=(\lambda^{(0)},\dots,\lambda^{(\ell-1)})
of partitions satisfying
\sum_{j\in J}|\lambda^{(j)}|=d
.
We denote
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigsqcup_{d\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)
.
Similarly, we have
J
-multicompositions

\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)=\{(\lambda^{(0)},\dots,\lambda^{(\ell-1)})\mid\lambda^{(0)},\dots,\lambda^{(\ell-1)}\in\Uplambda(n)\ \,\text{and\, $\textstyle\sum_{j\in J}|\lambda^{(j)}|=d$}.\}

We have a bijection

(2.1.14)

{\mathtt{a}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Uplambda(n\ell,d),\ \text{\boldmath$\lambda$}\mapsto(\lambda^{(0)}_{1},\dots,\lambda^{(0)}_{n},\dots,\lambda^{(\ell-1)}_{1},\dots,\lambda^{(\ell-1)}_{n}).

We denote
\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Uplambda(n,d)\times J^{n}
. Thus the elements of
\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
are pairs
(\lambda,\text{\boldmath$j$})
with
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
and
\text{\boldmath$j$}\in J^{n}
. We refer to such pairs as
colored compositions (of
d
of length
n
)
. We set

\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d).

We also have the sets of
colored essential compositions
:

\displaystyle\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)=\{(\lambda,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)\mid\lambda\in\Uplambda_{0}(n,d)\},\quad\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)=\bigsqcup_{n=0}^{d}\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d).

For
(\lambda,\text{\boldmath$i$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)
and
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(c)
, we have the concatenation

(2.1.15)

(\lambda\mu,\text{\boldmath$i$}\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(c+d).

We denote

\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=0^{n}1^{n}\cdots(\ell-1)^{n}\in J^{n\ell}.

We now have the injective map

(2.1.16)

{\mathtt{b}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Uplambda^{J}(n,d)\to\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n\ell,d),\ \text{\boldmath$\lambda$}\mapsto({\mathtt{a}}(\text{\boldmath$\lambda$}),\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(n)}).

We sometimes consider multicompositions in
\Lambda^{J}(n,d)
as colored compositions in
\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n\ell,d)
via this injection.

2.1c.
Symmetric group

We denote by
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
the symmetric group on
d
letters
and set

s_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(r,r+1)\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
for
r=1,\dots,d-1
to be the elementary transpositions in
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
.
We denote by
{\mathtt{l}}(w)
the corresponding
length
of
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}

and set
\mathtt{sgn}(w)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(-1)^{{\mathtt{l}}(w)}
.
We denote by
w_{d}
the longest element of
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
.

We denote by
\leq
the Bruhat order on the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, i.e. for
u,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, we have
u\leq w
if and only if
u=s_{r_{a_{1}}}\cdots s_{r_{a_{b}}}
for some
1\leq a_{1}<\cdots<a_{b}\leq l
, where
w=s_{r_{1}}\cdots s_{r_{l}}
is a reduced decomposition for
w
.

For any
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})\in\Uplambda(d)
, we have the
standard parabolic subgroup

\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda_{k}}\leq\mathfrak{S}_{n}.

We denote by
w_{\lambda}
the longest element of
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
.
For the composition
\lambda=(a^{b})
we abbreviate
\mathfrak{S}_{a^{b}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathfrak{S}_{(a^{b})}\leq\mathfrak{S}_{ab}
.

For
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
, we denote by
{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}
(resp.
{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}
, resp.
{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
) the set of the minimal length coset representatives for
\mathfrak{S}_{d}/\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
(resp.
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\backslash\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, resp.
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\backslash\mathfrak{S}_{d}/\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
). It is well-known that
{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}={\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}\cap{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}
. For
\lambda=(a^{b})
we abbreviate
{\mathscr{D}}^{a^{b}}_{ab}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathscr{D}}^{(a^{b})}_{ab}
,
{}^{a^{b}}{\mathscr{D}}_{ab}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={}^{(a^{b})}{\mathscr{D}}_{ab}
, etc.

Let
\nu
be yet another composition of
d
. We denote

{\mathscr{D}}^{\lambda}_{\nu}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\nu}\quad\text{and}\quad{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{\nu}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\nu}.

These will only be used when
\lambda
is a refinement of
\nu
, in which case these are the sets of the shortest coset representatives for
\mathfrak{S}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\backslash\mathfrak{S}_{\nu}
respectively.

If
{\tt X}\subseteq[1,d]
and
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, we say that
w
is
increasing on
{\tt X}
if
w(a)<w(b)
for all
a,b\in{\tt X}
with
a<b
. In this case, if
{\tt Y}=w({\tt X})
, we say that
w
maps
{\tt X}
increasingly onto
{\tt Y}
and write

(2.1.17)

w\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\tt X}\nearrow{\tt Y}.

Note that for
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
, we have
w\in{\mathscr{D}}^{\lambda}
if and only if
w
is increasing on the segments
{\tt P}^{\lambda}_{r}
for all
r=1,\dots,n
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
. If
w\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}w^{-1}=\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
for some
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, we write
\mu=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}w\lambda
and say that
w

permutes the parts of
\lambda
; in that case we have

w\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}w^{-1}=\mathfrak{S}_{w\mu}.

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Set
\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\lambda_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,\lambda_{\sigma^{-1}(n)})\in\Uplambda(n,d)
. Let
w_{\lambda,\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
be the permutation such that

(2.1.18)

w_{\lambda,\sigma}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\tt P}^{\lambda}_{r}\nearrow{\tt P}^{\mu}_{\sigma(r)}.

Note that

w_{\lambda,\sigma}\in{}^{\mu}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}
and
w_{\lambda,\sigma}\lambda=\mu
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
.
If
w\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
, then
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap w\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}w^{-1}
is a standard parabolic in
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
. This standard parabolic corresponds to a certain composition of
n
, which we denote
\lambda\cap w\mu
. Similarly,
w^{-1}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}w\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}
is the standard parabolic corresponding to a composotion
w^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu
. Thus:

(2.1.19)

\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap w\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}w^{-1}=\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\cap w\mu},\quad w^{-1}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}w\cap\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}=\mathfrak{S}_{w^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu}\qquad(w\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}).

Moreover,
w
permutes the parts of
w^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu
, and
w(w^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu)=\lambda\cap w\mu
, so

w\mathfrak{S}_{w^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu}w^{-1}=\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda\cap w\mu}.

This can be made more explicit as follows. Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(m,d)
and
\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d)
. Let
M(\lambda,\mu)
be the set of all matrices
A=(a_{r,s})\in M_{m\times n}(\mathbb{N})
such that
\sum_{s=1}^{n}a_{r,s}=\lambda_{r}
for
r=1,\dots,m
and
\sum_{r=1}^{m}a_{r,s}=\mu_{s}
for
s=1,\dots,n
. Given
A\in M(\lambda,\mu)
, the segment
[1,d]
is an increasing disjoint union of the segments

{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{1,1},\dots,{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{m,1},{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{1,2},\dots,{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{m,2},\dots,{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{1,n},\dots,{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{m,n},

of sizes
|{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{r,s}|=a_{r,s}
as well as
an increasing disjoint union of the segments

{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{1,1},\dots,{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{1,n},{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{2,1},\dots,{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{2,n},\dots,{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{m,1},\dots,{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{m,n},

of sizes
|{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{r,s}|=a_{r,s}
. Let
w_{A}\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
be such that

w_{A}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{r,s}\nearrow{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{r,s}
for all
1\leq r\leq m
and
1\leq s\leq n
. Then
w_{A}\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu}_{d}
and

(2.1.20)

M(\lambda,\mu)\to{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu}_{d},\ A\mapsto w_{A}

is a bijection. Moreover,

\displaystyle\lambda\cap w_{A}\mu=(a_{1,1},\dots,a_{1,n},a_{2,1},\dots,a_{2,n},\dots,a_{m,1},\dots,a_{m,n}),

\displaystyle w_{A}^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu=(a_{1,1},\dots,a_{m,1},a_{1,2},\dots,a_{m,2},\dots,a_{1,n},\dots,a_{m,n}).

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
.
We define the elements
{\mathtt{x}}_{d},{\mathtt{y}}_{d}
of the group algebra
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
and the elements
{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda},{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}
of the group algebra
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
as follows

(2.1.21)

{\mathtt{x}}_{d}=\sum_{g\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}}g,\quad{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda}=\sum_{g\in\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}g,\quad{\mathtt{y}}_{d}=\sum_{g\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\mathtt{sgn}(g)g,\quad{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}=\sum_{g\in\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\mathtt{sgn}(g)g.

Lemma 2.1.22
.

[
10
, Lemma 4.1]

Let
\lambda\in{\mathcal{P}}(d)
. There exists a unique element
u_{\lambda}\in{}^{\lambda^{\prime}}{\mathscr{D}}^{\lambda}
such that
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\cap u_{\lambda}\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}u_{\lambda}^{-1}=\{1\}
. Moreover,
{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}
is a free
\Bbbk
-module of rank
1
generated by the element
{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda^{\prime}}u_{\lambda}{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}
. In particular,

{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda^{\prime}}u_{\lambda}{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}\neq 0
.

The group
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
acts on
I^{d}
by place permutations:
g\cdot\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{g^{-1}(1)}\cdots i_{g^{-1}(d)}
for
g\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}\cdots i_{d}\in I^{d}
.
If
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})\in\Uplambda(d)
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\in I^{\lambda_{1}},\dots,\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}\in I^{\lambda_{k}}
, the words of the form
g\cdot(\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)})
with
g\in{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}
are called
shuffles
of
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)},\dots,\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}
.

2.1d.
Lie theory

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be the Kac-Moody Lie algebra of type
A_{2\ell}^{(2)}
(over
{\mathbb{C}})
,
see
[
25
, Chapter 4]
. The Dynkin diagram of
{\mathfrak{g}}
has vertices labeled by
I
:

We denote by
P
the
weight lattice
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
We have the set
\{\alpha_{i}\>|\>i\in I\}\subset P
of
simple roots
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and the set
\{h_{i}\>|\>i\in I\}\subset P^{*}
of the
simple coroots
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. The
Cartan matrix

\left(\langle h_{i},\alpha_{j}\rangle\right)_{0\leq i,j\leq\ell}
is:

\left(\begin{matrix}2&-2&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-1&2&-1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&-1&2&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&&&\ddots&&&\\
0&0&0&\dots&2&-1&0\\
0&0&0&\dots&-1&2&-2\\
0&0&0&\dots&0&-1&2\\
\end{matrix}\right)\quad\text{if\ \ $\ell\geq 2$,\ \ and}\quad\left(\begin{matrix}2&-4\\
-1&2\end{matrix}\right)\quad\text{if\ \ $\ell=1$.}

We denote by
Q
the sublattice of
P
generated by the simple roots and set

Q_{+}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\big\{\sum_{i\in I}m_{i}\alpha_{i}\mid m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\ \text{for all $i\in I$}\big\}\subset Q.

For
\theta=\sum_{i\in I}m_{i}\alpha_{i}\in Q_{+}
, its
height
is

{\operatorname{ht}}(\theta)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{i\in I}m_{i}

and its

parity
is

(2.1.23)

\|\theta\|\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=m_{0}\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }2)\,.

We denote

\delta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}2\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{\ell}\in Q_{+}.

Note that
{\operatorname{ht}}(\delta)=2\ell+1=p
.

We denote by
(.|.)
a normalized invariant form on
P
whose Gram matrix with respect to the linearly independent set
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}
is:

\left(\begin{matrix}2&-2&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
-2&4&-2&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&-2&4&\cdots&0&0&0\\
&&&\ddots&&&\\
0&0&0&\dots&4&-2&0\\
0&0&0&\dots&-2&4&-4\\
0&0&0&\dots&0&-4&8\\
\end{matrix}\right)\quad\text{if $\ell\geq 2$, and}\quad\left(\begin{matrix}2&-4\\
-4&8\end{matrix}\right)\quad\text{if $\ell=1$.}

In particular,
recalling the notation (
2.1.5
), we have
q_{i}=q^{(\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i})/2}
.

For
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}\cdots i_{n}\in I^{n}
, we denote

(2.1.24)

{\operatorname{wt}}(\text{\boldmath$i$})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\alpha_{i_{1}}+\dots+\alpha_{i_{n}}\in Q_{+}.

For
\theta\in Q_{+}
of height
n
, we set

I^{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}\cdots i_{n}\in I^{n}\mid{\operatorname{wt}}(\text{\boldmath$i$})=\theta\}.

We define
I^{\theta}_{{\operatorname{div}}}
to be the set of all expressions of the form

i_{1}^{(m_{1})}\cdots i_{r}^{(m_{r})}
with

m_{1},\dots,m_{r}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in I

and
m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}}+\cdots+m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}=\theta
(note we do not insists that
i_{k}\neq i_{k+1}
). We refer to such expressions as
divided power words
. We identify
I^{\theta}
with the subset of
I^{\theta}_{\operatorname{div}}
which consists of all expressions as above with all
m_{k}=1
. We use the same notation for concatenation of divided power words as for concatenation of words.
For
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}^{(m_{1})}\cdots i_{r}^{(m_{r})}\in I^{\theta}_{{\operatorname{div}}}
, we denote

(2.1.25)

\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=i_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots i_{r}^{m_{r}}\in I^{\theta}.

As in
[
25
, §5]
, the set
\Phi
of
roots
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a disjoint union of the set
\Phi^{\mathrm{im}\,}=\{n\delta\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
of
imaginary roots
and the set
\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}
of
real roots
. Also
\Phi=\Phi_{+}\sqcup-\Phi_{+}
, where
\Phi_{+}
is the set of
positive roots
. We denote
\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{im}\,}=\Phi^{\mathrm{im}\,}\cap\Phi_{+}
and
\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}=\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}\cap\Phi_{+}
.

Following
[
2
]
, we define a
convex preorder
on
\Phi_{+}
as a preorder
\preceq
on
\Phi_{+}
such that the following three conditions hold for all
\beta,\gamma\in\Phi_{+}
:

(2.1.26)

\displaystyle\beta\preceq\gamma\ \text{or}\ \gamma\preceq\beta;

(2.1.27)

\displaystyle\text{if $\beta\preceq\gamma$ and $\beta+\gamma\in\Phi_{+}$, then $\beta\preceq\beta+\gamma\preceq\gamma$};

(2.1.28)

\displaystyle\text{$\beta\preceq\gamma$ and $\gamma\preceq\beta$ if and only if $\beta$ and $\gamma$ are proportional}.

We write
\beta\prec\gamma
if
\beta\preceq\gamma
but
\gamma\not\preceq\beta
.
By (
2.1.28
),
\beta\preceq\gamma
and
\gamma\preceq\beta
happens for
\beta\neq\gamma
if and only if both
\beta
and
\gamma
are imaginary.
In particular, the set
\Psi
from (
2.1.35
) is totally ordered with respect to
\preceq
.
The set of real roots splits into two disjoint infinite sets

\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\succ\delta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}\mid\beta\succ\delta\}
and
\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\prec\delta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}\mid\beta\prec\delta\}.
The following is noted in
[
33
, §3.3a, (Con3)]
:

Lemma 2.1.29
.

Let
\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{im}\,}
, and
\beta=\sum_{a=1}^{b}\gamma_{a}
for some positive roots
\gamma_{a}
. If either
\gamma_{a}\preceq\beta
for all
a=1,\dots,b
or
\gamma_{a}\succeq\beta
for all
a=1,\dots,b
, then all
\gamma_{a}
are imaginary.

Let
\Phi^{\prime}
be the root system of type
C_{\ell}
whose Dynkin diagram is obtained by dropping the simple root
\alpha_{0}
from our type
A_{2\ell}^{(2)}
Dynkin diagram. Then
\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi^{\prime}_{\text{s}}\sqcup\Phi^{\prime}_{\text{l}}
where
\Phi^{\prime}_{\text{s}}=\{\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}\mid(\alpha|\alpha)=4\}
and
\Phi^{\prime}_{\text{l}}=\{\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}\mid(\alpha|\alpha)=8\}
.
By
[
25
, §6]
, we have
\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}=\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\text{s}}\sqcup\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\text{m}}\sqcup\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\text{l}}
for

(2.1.30)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\text{s}}

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha+(2n-1)\delta)/2\mid\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}_{l},\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\},

(2.1.31)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\text{m}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha+n\delta\mid\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}_{s},\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\},

(2.1.32)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\text{l}}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha+2n\delta\mid\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}_{l},\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

The set of positive roots is then

\Phi_{+}=\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{im}\,}\sqcup\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}},

where
\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{im}\,}=\{n\delta\mid n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\},
while
\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}
consists of the roots in
\Phi^{\prime}_{+}
together with the roots in (
2.1.30
)–(
2.1.32
) with
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, cf.
[
25
, Proposition 6.3]
.

Example 2.1.33
.

Following
[
39
, Example 3.5]
, consider
{\mathbb{R}}
as a vector space over
{\mathbb{Q}}
and let
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\mathbb{Q}}\Phi\to{\mathbb{R}}
be an injective linear map. Then it is easy to see that the following defines a convex preorder on
\Phi_{+}
:

\beta\preceq\gamma\quad\iff\quad\chi(\beta)/{\operatorname{ht}}(\beta)\leq\chi(\gamma)/{\operatorname{ht}}(\gamma).

Let
\tilde{\alpha}=2\alpha_{1}+\dots+2\alpha_{l-1}+\alpha_{\ell}\in\Phi^{\prime}
be the highest root in
\Phi^{\prime}
. Denote by
\Phi^{\prime}_{\sharp}
the set of all roots in

\Phi^{\prime}
which are non-negative linear combinations of
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell-1},-\tilde{\alpha}
(this is a non-standard choice of a positive root system in
\Phi^{\prime}
). The linear function
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\mathbb{Q}}\Phi\to{\mathbb{R}}
is determined by a choice of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linearly independent real numbers
\chi(\alpha_{0}),\dots,\chi(\alpha_{\ell-1}),\chi(\alpha_{\ell})
. These can be chosen so that
\chi(\alpha_{1}),\dots,\chi(\alpha_{\ell-1}),\chi(-\tilde{\alpha})
are positive and
\chi(\delta)
is very close to zero.
For the corresponding convex preorder
\preceq
we have

(2.1.34)

\begin{split}\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\succ\delta}=\{\beta\in\Phi_{+}\mid\text{$\beta$ is of the form $r\alpha+s\delta$ with $r\in\{1/2,1\}$, $\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}_{\sharp}$}\},\\
\Phi^{\mathrm{re}}_{\prec\delta}=\{\beta\in\Phi_{+}\mid\text{$\beta$ is of the form $-r\alpha+s\delta$ with $r\in\{1/2,1\}$, $\alpha\in\Phi^{\prime}_{\sharp}$}\}.\end{split}

These convex preorders are especially important since they are related to RoCK blocks of spin double covers of symmetric groups, see
[
33
]
. Starting with Section
4.2
, we will always work with such a convex preorder.

The set of
indivisible positive roots
is:

(2.1.35)

\Psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}\cup\{\delta\}.

Let
\theta\in Q_{+}
. A
root partition of
\theta
is a pair
({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
, where
{\underline{m}}=(m_{\beta})_{\beta\in\Psi}
is a tuple of non-negative integers such that
\sum_{\beta\in\Psi}m_{\beta}\beta=\theta
, and
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(m_{\delta})
is a
J
-multipartition of
m_{\delta}
, see §
2.1b
. Denote by
{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)
the set of all root partitions of
\theta
.

2.2.
Graded superalgebras

2.2a.
Graded superspaces and superalgebras

By a
graded superspace
we understand a
\Bbbk
-module with
\Bbbk
-module decomposition
V=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z},\,{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2}V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
.
We refer to the elements of
V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
as (homogeneous) elements of
bidegree

(n,{\varepsilon})
. We also refer to
n
as
degree
and
{\varepsilon}
as
parity
and write for
v\in V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
:

\operatorname{bideg}(v)=(n,{\varepsilon}),\ \deg(v)=n,\ |v|={\varepsilon}.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2
, we use the notation
V^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=V^{n}_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus V^{n}_{{\bar{1}}}
,
V_{\varepsilon}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
, and

(2.2.1)

\quad V^{>n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{m>n}V^{m},\quad V^{\geq n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{m\geq n}V^{m},\quad V^{<n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{m<n}V^{m}.

A
subsuperspace

W\subseteq V
is a
\Bbbk
-submodule such that
W=\bigoplus_{n,{\varepsilon}}(W\cap V_{n,{\varepsilon}})
.

If each
V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
is a free
\Bbbk
-module of finite rank and
V^{n}=0
for
n\ll 0
, the
graded superdimension of
V
is defined to be

\dim_{q,\pi}V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},\,{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}\nolimits_{\Bbbk}V^{n}_{\varepsilon})q^{n}\pi^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q)).

The
graded dual superspace
is
V^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z},\,{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2}(V^{n}_{\varepsilon})^{*}
where the elements of
(V^{n}_{\varepsilon})^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(V^{n}_{\varepsilon},\Bbbk)
are considered as elements of bidegree
(-n,{\varepsilon})
. Recalling the bar-involution from (
2.1.3
), we have that if
V
is a free
\Bbbk
-module of finite rank then

(2.2.2)

\dim_{q,\pi}V^{*}=\overline{\dim_{q,\pi}V}.

Let
V,W
be graded superspaces. The tensor product
V\otimes W
is considered as a graded superspace via
\operatorname{bideg}(v\otimes w)=(\deg(v)+\deg(w),|v|+|w|)
for all homogeneous
v\in V
and
w\in W
.
For
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\delta\in\mathbb{Z}/2
, a bidegree
(m,\delta)
(homogeneous) linear map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to W
is a
\Bbbk
-linear map satisfying
f(V^{n}_{\varepsilon})\subseteq W^{n+m}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta}
for all
n,{\varepsilon}
. We denote the
\Bbbk
-module of all bidegree
(m,\delta)
linear maps from
V
to
W
by
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(V,W)^{m}_{\delta}
, and set

\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(V,W)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z},\,\delta\in\mathbb{Z}/2}\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(V,W)^{m}_{\delta}.

Let
V
be a graded superspace and
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
The symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
acts on
V^{\otimes d}
via

(2.2.3)

{}^{w}(v_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{d})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(-1)^{[w;v_{1},\dots,v_{d}]}v_{w^{-1}(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes v_{w^{-1}(d)},

where

[w;v_{1},\dots,v_{d}]\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{1\leq a<c\leq d,\,w^{-1}(a)>w^{-1}(c)}|v_{a}||v_{c}|.

A graded (
\Bbbk
-)
superalgebra
is a graded superspace
A=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z},\,{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2}A^{n}_{\varepsilon}
which is a unital algebra such that
A^{n}_{\varepsilon}A_{m}^{\delta}\subseteq A_{n+m}^{{\varepsilon}+\delta}
. The
opposite superalgebra

A^{\mathrm{sop}}
is the same as
A
as a
\Bbbk
-module but has multiplication
a*b=(-1)^{|a||b|}ba
.

Let
A,B
be graded superalgebras. An
isomorphism

f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\to B
is an algebra isomorphism which is homogeneous of bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
. We write
A\cong B
if
A
and
B
are isomorphic in this sense. The tensor product
A\otimes B
is considered as a graded superalgebra via
(a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=(-1)^{|b||a^{\prime}|}aa^{\prime}\otimes bb^{\prime}

for all homogeneous
a,a^{\prime}\in A
and
b,b^{\prime}\in B
.

Let
A
be a superalgebra and
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
For
1\leq t\leq d
,
1\leq r<s\leq d
, we have maps

\displaystyle\iota^{(d)}_{t}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\to A^{\otimes d},\ a\mapsto 1_{A}^{\otimes(t-1)}\otimes a\otimes 1_{A}^{\otimes(d-t)},

\displaystyle\iota^{(d)}_{r,s}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\otimes A\to A^{\otimes d},\ a\otimes b\mapsto 1_{A}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes a\otimes 1_{A}^{\otimes(s-r-1)}\otimes b\otimes 1_{A}^{\otimes(d-s)}.

For
x\in A
and
y\in A\otimes A
, we sometimes denote

(2.2.4)

x_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\iota^{(d)}_{r}(x)\in A^{\otimes d},\quad y_{r,s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\iota^{(d)}_{r,s}(y)\in A^{\otimes d}.

Given a graded superalgebra
A
, we consider the
wreath superproduct

A\wr_{\mathop{\tt s}\nolimits}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, where
A\wr_{\mathop{\tt s}\nolimits}\mathfrak{S}_{d}=A^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
as graded superspaces, with the group algebra
\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
always considered as a graded superalgebra in the trivial way, i.e. concentrated in bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
. To define the algebra structure, we identify
A^{\otimes d}
and
\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
with subspaces of
A^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
in the obvious way, and postulate that
A^{\otimes d}
,
\mathbb{F}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
are subalgebras of
A\wr_{\mathop{\tt s}\nolimits}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
; then recalling (
2.2.3
), we also postulate that

\displaystyle w\,(a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{d})={}^{w}(a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{d})\,w\qquad(w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d},\ a_{1},\dots,a_{d}\in A).

2.2b.
Graded supermodules

Let
A
be a graded superalgebra.
A
graded
A
-supermodule

V
is an
A
-module which is also a graded superspace such that
A^{n}_{\varepsilon}V^{m}_{\delta}\subseteq V^{n+m}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta}
for all
n,m,{\varepsilon},\delta
.
A graded subsupermodule
W\subseteq V
is a graded subsuperspace which is also an
A
-submodule.

Let
V,W
be graded
A
-supermodules. A
bidegree
(m,\delta)
(homogeneous) graded
A
-supermodule homomorphism from
V
to
W
is
\Bbbk
-linear map
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to W
of a bidegree
(m,\delta)
satisfying
f(av)=(-1)^{\delta|a|}af(v)
for all (homogeneous)
a\in A,v\in V
. We denote by
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(V,W)^{m}_{\delta}
the
\Bbbk
-module of all bidegree
(m,\delta)
graded
A
-supermodule homomorphism from
V
to
W
, and set

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(V,W)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z},\,\delta\in\mathbb{Z}/2}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(V,W)^{m}_{\delta}.

We refer to the elements of
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(V,W)
as
graded
A
-supermodule homomorphisms from
V
to
W
.

For a graded superalgebra
A
, we denote by

A\!\operatorname{-Mod}
the category of all
graded
A
-supermodules and all graded
A
-supermodule homomorphisms.
An isomorphism in
A\!\operatorname{-Mod}
will be denoted
\cong
. On the other hand, a homogeneous bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
isomorphism in
A\!\operatorname{-Mod}
will be denoted
\simeq
.
In this paper, we assume that all graded superalgebras
A
are noetherian (as graded superalgebras).
For such
A
, we usually work in the full subcategory

A\!\operatorname{-mod}
of
A\!\operatorname{-Mod}
which consists of all

finitely generated
graded
A
-supermodules.

In fact,
A\!\operatorname{-mod}
is a graded
({\mathpzc{Q}},\Uppi)
-supercategory in the sense of
[
3
, Definition 6.4]
, with
\Uppi
the parity change functor and
{\mathpzc{Q}}
the degree shift functor. To be more precise, if
V=\bigoplus_{n,{\varepsilon}}V^{n}_{\varepsilon}\in A\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we have
(\Uppi V)^{n}_{\varepsilon}=V^{n}_{{\varepsilon}+{\bar{1}}}
with the new action
a\cdot v=(-1)^{|a|}av
, and
({\mathpzc{Q}}V)^{n}_{\varepsilon}=V^{n-1}_{\varepsilon}
(with the old action). The following notation will be used. Suppose that
f=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2}a_{n,{\varepsilon}}q^{n}\pi^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}]
be a Laurent polynomial with the coefficients
a_{n,{\varepsilon}}\in\mathbb{N}
. Given
V\in A\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we interpret
V^{\oplus f}
as follows:

(2.2.5)

V^{\oplus f}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z},{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{n}\Uppi^{\varepsilon}V^{\oplus a_{n,{\varepsilon}}}.

If
g
is another such Laurent polynomial then

(2.2.6)

(V^{\oplus f})^{\oplus g}\simeq V^{\oplus fg}.

A graded
A
-supermodule
V
is
irreducible
if it has exactly two graded subsupermodules:
0
and
V
. In general, an irreducible graded
A
-supermodule
V
may or may not be irreducible when considered as a usual
A
-module. If it is,
V
is called
absolutely irreducible
. (The irreducible graded supermodules considered in this paper will all be absolutely irreducible, see Lemma
3.3.19
.)
We denote by
{\mathrm{Irr}}(A)
a complete and non-redundant set of irreducible graded
A
-supermodules, i.e. every irreducible graded
A
-supermodule is isomorphic to some member of
{\mathrm{Irr}}(A)
and
V\not\cong W
for any any two distinct
V,W\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(A)
.

Let
A,B
be graded superalgebras. Given a graded
A
-supermodule
V
and a graded
B
-supermodule
W
, we have the graded
(A\otimes B)
-supermodule
V\boxtimes W
with the action

(a\otimes b)(v\otimes w)=(-1)^{|b||v|}(av\otimes bw)\qquad(a\in A,\,b\in B,\,v\in V,\,w\in W).

A graded
(A,B)
-bisupermodule

V
is an
(A,B)
-bimodule which is also a graded superspace such that
A^{n}_{\varepsilon}V^{m}_{\delta}\subseteq V^{n+m}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta}
and
V^{m}_{\delta}B^{n}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq V^{n+m}_{{\varepsilon}+\delta}
for all
n,m,{\varepsilon},\delta
.
A homomorphism of graded
(A,B)
-bisupermodules is defined similarly to a homomorphism of graded
A
-supermodules. In particular, a bidegree
(m,\delta)
homomorphism of graded
(A,B)
-bisupermodules
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to W
satisfies
f(avb)=(-1)^{\delta|a|}af(v)b
for all (homogeneous)
a\in A,\,v\in V,\,b\in B
. In fact, we can identify the notions of a graded
(A,B)
-bisupermodule and a graded
(A\otimes B^{{\mathrm{sop}}})
-supermodule and then a homomorphism of graded
(A,B)
-bisupermodules is the same thing as a homomorphism of graded
(A\otimes B^{\mathrm{sop}})
-supermodules, see e.g.
[
33
, §2.2b]
.
Just like for graded supermodules, we use the notation
\cong
to indicate an isomorphism of graded bisupermodules, and
\simeq
to indicate a homogeneous bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
isomorphism of graded bisupermodules.

Example 2.2.7
.

Given a graded
A
-supermodule
M
, we have the graded superalgebra
\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)^{\mathrm{sop}}
, and
M
is naturally a graded
(A,\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)^{\mathrm{sop}})
-bisupermodule with
f\in\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)^{\mathrm{sop}}=\operatorname{End}_{A}(M)
acting via

m\cdot f=(-1)^{|f||m|}f(m)
.

2.2c.
Induction and restriction for graded supermodules

Let
A
be a graded
\Bbbk
-superalgebra with a bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
idempotent
e
. Suppose that
B
is a graded subsuperalgebra of
eAe
. Then
eA
is a graded
(B,A)
-bisupermodule and
Ae
is a graded
(A,B)
-bisupermodule, so we have the usual functors of restriction, induction and coinduction

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Res}}^{A}_{B}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\!\operatorname{-Mod}\to B\!\operatorname{-Mod},\ V\mapsto eA\otimes_{A}V,

(2.2.9)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ind}}^{A}_{B}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}B\!\operatorname{-Mod}\to A\!\operatorname{-Mod},\ W\mapsto Ae\otimes_{B}W,

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Coind}}^{A}_{B}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}B\!\operatorname{-Mod}\to A\!\operatorname{-Mod},\ W\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(eA,W).

The functor
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{B}^{A}
is left adjoint to
{\mathrm{Res}}_{B}^{A}
and the functor
{\mathrm{Coind}}_{B}^{A}
is right adjoint to
{\mathrm{Res}}_{B}^{A}
. Note that the definition of the structure of a graded
A
-supermodule on
{\mathrm{Coind}}_{B}^{A}W=\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(eA,W)
involves sign:
(a\cdot f)(ea^{\prime})=(-1)^{|a|(|f|+|a^{\prime}|)}(ea^{\prime}a)
for
a,a^{\prime}\in A
and
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(eA,W)
.

We will need a criterion for an isomorphism
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{B}^{A}V\cong{\mathrm{Coind}}_{B}^{A}V
.

Lemma 2.2.11
.

Let
e,f
be bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
idempotents in a graded
\Bbbk
-superalgebra
A
and
B\subseteq eAe,\,C\subseteq fAf
be graded subsuperalgebras. Let
V\in B\!\operatorname{-Mod}
and
W\in C\!\operatorname{-Mod}
.
We make the following assumptions:

(1)

The right
B
-module
Ae
has basis
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}
and the left
C
-module
fA
has basis
\{c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\}
.

(2)

There is an isomorphism of graded
\Bbbk
-superalgebras
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}C
and a degree
(0,{\bar{0}})
-isomorphism
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to W
of graded
\Bbbk
-supermodules such that
j(bv)=i(b)j(v)
for all
b\in B
and
v\in V
.

(3)

The graded
(C,B)
-bisupermodule
fAe
has a graded subsuperbimodule
Y
and a homogeneous element
\xi
such that
\xi b\equiv(-1)^{|b||\xi|}i(b)\xi\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }Y)\,
and the map
C\to(fAe)/Y,\ c\mapsto c\xi+Y
is an isomorphism of graded left
C
-supermodules.

(4)

There is a permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that for
1\leq r,s\leq n
, we have
c_{s}b_{r}\equiv\delta_{\sigma(r),s}\xi\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }Y)\,
.

Then there is an isomorphism of graded
A
-supermodules

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{B}^{A}V\simeq{\mathpzc{Q}}^{\deg(\xi)}\Uppi^{|\xi|}{\mathrm{Coind}}_{C}^{A}W.

Proof.

We have to construct a graded
A
-supermodule isomorphism
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{B}^{A}V\to{\mathrm{Coind}}_{C}^{A}W
of bidegree
(-\deg(\xi),|\xi|)
. This will come from a bidegree
(-\deg(\xi),|\xi|)
homomorphism of graded
B
-supermodules
V\to{\mathrm{Res}}^{A}_{B}{\mathrm{Coind}}_{C}^{A}W
using adjointness of
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{B}^{A}
and
{\mathrm{Res}}_{B}^{A}
. Note that
{\mathrm{Res}}^{A}_{B}{\mathrm{Coind}}_{C}^{A}W\simeq e\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(fA,W)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(fAe,W).

By the assumption (3), every element of
fAe
can written uniquely in the form
c\xi+y
for
c\in C
and
y\in Y
. So for every
v\in V
, we have a graded
C
-supermodule homomorphism

F_{v}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}fAe\to W,\ c\xi+y\mapsto(-1)^{|c||v|+|c||\xi|}c\,j(v).

Since
\operatorname{bideg}(F_{v})=(\deg(v)-\deg(\xi),|v|+|\xi|)
, we have a bidegree
(-\deg(\xi),|\xi|)
homomorphism of graded
\Bbbk
-supermodules

\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(fAe,W),\ v\mapsto(-1)^{|v||\xi|}F_{v}.

We claim that
\alpha
is a homomorphism of graded
B
-supermodules. This means
\alpha(bv)=(-1)^{|b||\xi|}b\alpha(v)
for
b\in B
and
v\in V
, or
F_{bv}=bF_{v}
. Now

\displaystyle(bF_{v})(c\xi+y

\displaystyle)=(-1)^{|b|(|\xi|+|v|+|c|+|\xi|)}F_{v}((c\xi+y)b)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|b|(|v|+|c|)}F_{v}(c\xi b+yb)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|b|(|v|+|c|)+|\xi||b|}F_{v}(ci(b)\xi+yb)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|b|(|v|+|c|)+|\xi||b|+(|c|+|b|)|v|+(|c|+|b|)|\xi|}ci(b)j(v)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|b||c|+|c||v|+|c||\xi|}ci(b)j(v)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|c||bv|+|c||\xi|}cj(bv)

\displaystyle=F_{bv}(c\xi+y).

Now, adjointness of
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{B}^{A}
and
{\mathrm{Res}}_{B}^{A}
yields a a bidegree
(-\deg(\xi),|\xi|)
homomorphism of graded
A
-supermodules

F\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Ae\otimes_{B}V\to\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(fA,W),\ ae\otimes v\mapsto F_{ae,v},

where
F_{ae,v}
is defined from

F_{ae,v}(fa^{\prime})=(-1)^{|a|(|\xi|+|v|+|a^{\prime}|)}F_{v}(fa^{\prime}ae).

By the assumption (1), we have an isomorphism of graded
\Bbbk
-supermodules

(2.2.12)

Ae\otimes_{B}V\simeq\bigoplus_{r=1}^{n}(b_{r}\otimes V)\cong\bigoplus_{r=1}^{n}V,

where the second isomorphism comes from the isomorphisms

V\to b_{r}\otimes V,\ v\mapsto b_{r}\otimes v
. Similarly, we have an isomorphism of graded
\Bbbk
-supermodules

(2.2.13)

\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(fA,W)\simeq\bigoplus_{s=1}^{n}\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(Cc_{s},W)\cong\bigoplus_{s=1}^{n}W,

with the second isomorphism coming from the isomorphisms

{\varphi}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W\to\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(Cc_{s},W),\ w\mapsto(cc_{s}\mapsto(-1)^{|c||c_{s}|+|c||v|+|c_{s}||v|}cw).

Now, by the assumption (4), we have

F_{b_{r},v}(c_{s})=\pm F_{v}(c_{s}b_{r})=\pm\delta_{\sigma(r),s}j(v).

Thus
F
maps the vector
b_{r}\otimes v
from the
r
th summand of (
2.2.12
) onto
\pm{\varphi}_{\sigma(r)}(j(v))
from the
\sigma(r)
th summand of (
2.2.13
). Hence
F
is an isomorphism.
∎

Example 2.2.14
.

Suppose that the graded superalgebra
B
in Lemma
2.2.11
is of the form
X\otimes Y
for two graded superalgebras
X
and
Y
, and
C=Y\otimes X
. Moreover, suppose that
V
is of the form
M\boxtimes N
for a graded
X
-supermodule
M
and a graded
Y
-supermodule
N
, and
W=N\boxtimes M
. Then we can take
i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X\otimes Y\to Y\otimes X,\ x\otimes y\mapsto(-1)^{|x||y|}y\otimes x
and
j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M\otimes N\to N\otimes M,\ m\otimes n\mapsto(-1)^{|m||n|}n\otimes m
. If all the other assumptions of the lemma are satisfied, we will get an isomorphism

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{X\otimes Y}^{A}M\boxtimes N\simeq{\mathrm{Coind}}_{Y\otimes X}^{A}N\boxtimes M.

Checking the explicit construction of this isomorphism, one can see that the isomorphism is natural in
M
and
N
.

2.2d.
Graded Morita superequivalence

For graded
\Bbbk
-superalgebras
A
and
B
, a
graded Morita superequivalence
between
A
and
B
is a Morita equivalence between
A
and
B
induced by a graded
(A,B)
-bisupermodule
M
and a graded
(B,A)
-bisupermodule
N
such that
M\otimes_{B}N\simeq A
and
N\otimes_{A}M\simeq B
. (with both bimodule isomorphisms homogeneous of bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
).
A standard argument shows that
A
and
B
are graded Morita superequivalent if and only if the graded supercategories
A\!\operatorname{-mod}
and
B\!\operatorname{-mod}
are graded superequivalent, i.e. there are functors
{\mathpzc F}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\!\operatorname{-mod}\to B\!\operatorname{-mod}
and
{\mathpzc G}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}B\!\operatorname{-mod}\to A\!\operatorname{-mod}
such that
{\mathpzc F}\circ{\mathpzc G}
and
{\mathpzc G}\circ{\mathpzc F}
are isomorphic to identities via graded supernatural transformations of bidegrees
(0,{\bar{0}})
, cf.
[
3
, Deinition 1.1(iv)]
.

Lemma 2.2.15
.

[
33
, Lemma 2.2.14]

Let
A
be a graded
\mathbb{F}
-superalgebra and
e\in A^{0}_{{\bar{0}}}
an idempotent such that
eL\neq 0
for every irreducible graded
A
-supermodule
L
(equivalently
AeA=A
). Then the functors
eA\otimes_{A}-
and
Ae\otimes_{eAe}-
induce a graded Morita superequivalence between
A
and
eAe
.

Lemma 2.2.16
.

[
33
, Corollary 2.2.15]

Let
A
be a graded
\mathbb{F}
-superalgebra and
e\in A^{0}_{{\bar{0}}}
an idempotent. If
|{\mathrm{Irr}}(eAe)|\geq|{\mathrm{Irr}}(A)|<\infty
then the functors
eA\otimes_{A}-
and
Ae\otimes_{eAe}-
induce a graded Morita superequivalence between
A
and
eAe
.

From now on until the end of the subsection, we assume that
A
is a graded
\mathcal{O}
-superalgebra with
A^{n}_{\varepsilon}
finitely generated as an
\mathcal{O}
-module for all
n
and
{\varepsilon}
. Suppose that
\mathbb{F}=\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{m}
for a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
in
\mathcal{O}
. We can extend scalars to get a graded
\mathbb{F}
-superalgebra
A_{\mathbb{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}A
. Given
a\in A
, we denote
a_{\mathbb{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1\otimes a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
. If
V
is a graded
A
-supermodule then
V_{\mathbb{F}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathbb{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}V
is a graded
A_{\mathbb{F}}
-supermodule (similarly for graded bisupermodules).

If
e\in A^{0}_{{\bar{0}}}
is an idempotent, we have the functors

(2.2.17)

\begin{split}Ae\otimes_{eAe}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}eAe\!\operatorname{-mod}\to A\!\operatorname{-mod},\\
eA\otimes_{A}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\!\operatorname{-mod}\to eAe\!\operatorname{-mod}.\end{split}

and

(2.2.18)

\begin{split}A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to A_{\mathbb{F}}\!\operatorname{-mod},\\
e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{A_{\mathbb{F}}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A_{\mathbb{F}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}\!\operatorname{-mod}.\end{split}

Note that
eAe
is an
\mathcal{O}
-direct summand of
A
, so we can identify the graded
\mathbb{F}
-superalgebra
(eAe)_{\mathbb{F}}
with the graded
\mathbb{F}
-superalgebra
e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}
. Now, we can also identify as graded bisupermodules
(Ae)_{\mathbb{F}}=A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}
and
