(eA)_{\mathbb{F}}=e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}
. Moreover, for
V\in eAe\!\operatorname{-mod}
and
W\in A\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we have functorial isomorphisms

(2.2.19)

\begin{split}(Ae\otimes_{eAe}V)_{\mathbb{F}}\simeq A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}}V_{\mathbb{F}},\\
(eA\otimes_{A}W)_{\mathbb{F}}\simeq e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}\otimes_{A_{\mathbb{F}}}W_{\mathbb{F}}.\end{split}

Lemma 2.2.20
.

If for all fields
\mathbb{F}\in{\mathscr{F}}=\{\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{m}\mid\text{$\mathfrak{m}$ is a maximal ideal of $\mathcal{O}$}\}
, the functors (
2.2.18
) are mutually quasi-inverse equivalences, then so are the functors (
2.2.17
).

Proof.

We always have an isomorphism of graded
eAe
-superbimodules
eA\otimes_{A}Ae\simeq eAe
, so it suffices to prove that the multiplication map
\mu\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Ae\otimes_{eAe}eA\to A
is an isomorphism. In view of
[
12
, Statement 7]
, it suffices to prove that
\mu
is surjective, i.e.
AeA=A
, given the assumption that
A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}=A_{\mathbb{F}}
for all
\mathbb{F}\in{\mathscr{F}}
.
The short exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}
-modules
0\to AeA\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}A\to A/AeA\to 0
yields an exact sequence of
\mathbb{F}
-vector spaces
(AeA)_{\mathbb{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{\mathbb{F}}}}{{\to}}A_{\mathbb{F}}\to(A/AeA)_{\mathbb{F}}\to 0
. To see that
A/AeA=0
it suffices to prove
(A/AeA)_{\mathbb{F}}=0
for all
\mathbb{F}\in{\mathscr{F}}
, or, equivalently, that
\iota_{\mathbb{F}}
is surjective for all
\mathbb{F}\in{\mathscr{F}}
. But it is easy to see that
\operatorname{Im}\iota_{\mathbb{F}}=A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}
for all
\mathbb{F}
, and
A_{\mathbb{F}}e_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}=A_{\mathbb{F}}
by assumption.
∎

Example 2.2.21
.

Let
A
be a graded superalgebra and
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
be (not necessarily distinct) bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
idempotents of
A
.
We have a graded superalgebra
\bigoplus_{1\leq i,j\leq n}e_{i}Ae_{j}
with multiplication inherited from
A
. In fact, setting

P\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}Ae_{i},

there the isomorphism of graded superalgebras

(2.2.22)

\bigoplus_{1\leq i,j\leq n}e_{i}Ae_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{End}_{A}(P)^{\mathrm{sop}},\ a\mapsto{\varphi}_{a},

where for (a homogeneous)
a\in e_{i}Ae_{j}
, the endomorphism
{\varphi}_{a}
sends the summand
Ae_{k}
of
P
to
0
if
k\neq i
and the summand
Ae_{i}
to the summand
Ae_{j}
as follows:

{\varphi}_{a}(be_{i})=(-1)^{|a||b|}be_{i}a

(for a homogeneous
b\in A
). If
P
is a projective generator for
A
(equivalently, if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}Ae_{i}A=A
) then
A
is graded Morita superequivalent to
\operatorname{End}_{A}(P)^{\mathrm{sop}}
using the graded
(A,\operatorname{End}_{A}(P)^{\mathrm{sop}})
-superbimodule
M
being
P
considered as a graded
(A,\operatorname{End}_{A}(P)^{\mathrm{sop}})
-superbimodule as in Example
2.2.7
.

Example 2.2.23
.

An easy special case of a graded Morita superequivalence comes from a ‘regrading’ of a graded superalgebra in the following sense. Suppose that
A
is a graded superalgebra and we are given an orthogonal idempotent decomposition
1_{A}=\sum_{r=1}^{n}e_{r}
with the idempotents
e_{r}
of bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
. Then as graded superspaces
A=\bigoplus_{1\leq r,s\leq n}e_{s}Ae_{r}
. Given
grading supershift parameters

(2.2.24)

t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad{\varepsilon}_{1},\dots,{\varepsilon}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}/2,

we can consider the new graded superalgebra

{\mathsf{A}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{1\leq r,s\leq n}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{t_{r}-t_{s}}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}_{r}-{\varepsilon}_{s}}e_{s}Ae_{r},

which equals
A
as an algebra but has different grading and parity. To distinguish between the elements of
A
and
{\mathsf{A}}
, we denote by
{\mathsf{a}}
the element of
{\mathsf{A}}
corresponding to
a\in A
. In particular, we have idempotents
{\mathsf{e}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{e}}_{n}\in{\mathsf{A}}
.
We have the graded
({\mathsf{A}},A)
-bisupermodule

X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{r=1}^{n}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{-t_{r}}\Uppi^{-{\varepsilon}_{r}}e_{r}A=\bigoplus_{s=1}^{n}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{-t_{s}}\Uppi^{-{\varepsilon}_{s}}{\mathsf{A}}{\mathsf{e}}_{s},

and the graded
(A,{\mathsf{A}})
-bisupermodule

Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{s=1}^{n}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{t_{s}}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}_{s}}Ae_{s}=\bigoplus_{r=1}^{n}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{t_{r}}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}_{r}}{\mathsf{e}}_{r}{\mathsf{A}},

inducing a graded Morita superequivalence between
A
and
{\mathsf{A}}
. Under this graded Morita superequivalence, a graded
A
-supermodule
V
, corresponds to the graded
{\mathsf{A}}
-supermodule

(2.2.25)

{\mathsf{V}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\otimes_{A}V\simeq\bigoplus_{r=1}^{n}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{-t_{r}}\Uppi^{-{\varepsilon}_{r}}e_{r}V.

2.2e.
Schur functors

Here we adapt the material of
[
4
, Section 3.1]
to the graded superalgebra setting. Let
A
be a finite dimensional graded
\mathbb{F}
-superalgebra and
P\in A\!\operatorname{-mod}
be a projective graded
A
-supermodule, i.e. a direct summand of a finite direct sum of modules of the form
{\mathpzc{Q}}^{m}\Uppi^{\delta}A
.
Let
H=\operatorname{End}_{A}(P)^{\mathrm{sop}}
, so

P
can be considered as a graded
(A,P)
-bisupermodule with the right action
p\cdot h=(-1)^{|p||h|}h(p)
for all
p\in P
and
h\in H
. (In all applications later on we will always have
H
purely even, so the issue of signs will not arise, but we do not need to make this assupmtion in this subsection.)

Define the functors:

\displaystyle{\mathpzc{f}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P,?)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\!\operatorname{-mod}\to H\!\operatorname{-mod},

\displaystyle{\mathpzc{g}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=P\otimes_{H}?\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}H\!\operatorname{-mod}\to A\!\operatorname{-mod}.

Then
{\mathpzc{f}}
is exact, and
{\mathpzc{g}}
is left adjoint to
{\mathpzc{f}}
, the adjunction isomorphism being

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P\otimes_{H}W,V)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(W,\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P,V)),

\displaystyle{\varphi}

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(w\mapsto(p\mapsto(-1)^{|p||w|}{\varphi}(p\otimes w))\big).

Given an
A
-module
V
, let
O_{P}(V)
denote the largest graded subsupermodule
V^{\prime}
of
V
such that
\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P,V^{\prime})=0
. Let
O^{P}(V)
denote the graded subsupermodule of
V
generated by the images of all
A
-homomorphisms from
P
to
V
.
Any graded
A
-supermodule homomorphism
V\to W
sends
O_{P}(V)
into
O_{P}(W)
and
O^{P}(V)
into
O^{P}(W)
, so we can view
O_{P}
and
O^{P}
as functors
A\!\operatorname{-mod}\to A\!\operatorname{-mod}
. Finally, any graded
A
-supermodule homomorphism
V\to W
induces a well-defined graded
A
-supermodule homomorphism
V/O_{P}(V)\to W/O_{P}(W)
. We thus obtain an exact functor

{\mathpzc{q}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A\!\operatorname{-mod}\to A\!\operatorname{-mod}

defined on objects by
V\mapsto V/O_{P}(V)
.

Lemma 2.2.26
.

The functors
{\mathpzc{f}}\circ{\mathpzc{g}}
and
{\mathpzc{f}}\circ{\mathpzc{q}}\circ{\mathpzc{g}}
are both isomorphic to the identity.

Proof.

The proof given in
[
4
, 3.1a]
goes through using the modified version of
[
1
, Proposition 20.10]
, in which the isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P,P)\otimes_{H}W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P,P\otimes_{H}W)

involves a sign, namely
h\otimes w\mapsto(p\mapsto(-1)^{|w||p|}h(p)\otimes w)
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.27
.

If
V,V^{\prime}\in A\!\operatorname{-mod}
satisfy
O^{P}(V)=V
and
O_{P}(V^{\prime})=0
, then

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(V,V^{\prime})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{H}({\mathpzc{f}}(V),{\mathpzc{f}}(V^{\prime})).

Proof.

The proof given in
[
4
, 3.1c]
goes through using the properties of the modified version of the map

\omega\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\mathpzc{g}}\circ{\mathpzc{f}}(V)=P\otimes_{H}\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(P,V)\to V,\ p\otimes{\varphi}\mapsto(-1)^{|{\varphi}||p|}{\varphi}(p),

cf.
[
4
, 3.1b]
.
∎

Theorem 2.2.28
.

The functors
{\mathpzc{f}}
and
{\mathpzc{q}}\circ{\mathpzc{g}}
induce mutually quasi-inverse graded superequivalences between
H\!\operatorname{-mod}
and the full graded subsupercategory of
A\!\operatorname{-mod}
consisting of all
V\in A\!\operatorname{-mod}
such that
O_{P}(V)=0
and
O^{P}(V)=V
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
4
, 3.1d]
goes through.
∎

As a consequence we have the following relation between the irreducible modules, see
[
4
, 3.1e]
:

Lemma 2.2.29
.

Let
\{L_{r}\mid r\in R\}
be a complete set of non-isomorphic irreducible graded
A
-supermodules appearing in the head of
P
. For all
r\in R
, set
D_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathpzc{f}}(L_{r})
. Then,
\{D_{r}\mid r\in R\}={\mathrm{Irr}}(H)
, and
{\mathpzc{q}}\circ{\mathpzc{g}}(D_{r})\cong L_{r}
for all
r
.

Finally, we have the following explicit description of the effect of the composite functor
{\mathpzc{q}}\circ{\mathpzc{g}}
on left ideals of
H
, see
[
4
, 3.1f]
:

Lemma 2.2.30
.

If every composition factor of the socle of
P
also appears in its head then for any graded left superideal
J
of
H
, we have
{\mathpzc{q}}\circ{\mathpzc{g}}(J)\simeq PJ
.

Chapter 3
Some important (super)algebras

3.1.
Classical Schur algebras

Algebras and modules appearing in this section will be considered as graded superalgebras and graded supermodules in the trivial way, i.e. concentrated in bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
.

3.1a.
Permutation modules over symmetric groups

The trivial
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
-module is denoted
\Bbbk_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}
, and the sign
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
-module is denoted
\mathtt{sgn}_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}
. Sometimes we use the
right
trivial
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
-module, which is again denoted
\Bbbk_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}
.
We have the
permutation module
and the
sign permutation module

(3.1.1)

M^{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathrm{Ind}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\Bbbk_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\simeq\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda}\quad\text{and}\quad N^{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathrm{Ind}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\mathtt{sgn}_{\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\simeq\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}

with generators
m^{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\otimes 1_{\Bbbk}
and
n^{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\otimes 1_{\Bbbk}
and bases

\{gm^{\lambda}\mid g\in{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}\}
and
\{gn^{\lambda}\mid g\in{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}\}
, respectively.

For
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
and
g\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, define the elements

(3.1.2)

X_{\mu,\lambda}^{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap\,{}^{\mu}{\mathscr{D}}}w\qquad\text{and}\qquad Y_{\mu,\lambda}^{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap\,{}^{\mu}{\mathscr{D}}}\mathtt{sgn}(w)w

of
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
. Then

(3.1.3)

\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}w={\mathtt{x}}_{\mu}\cdot X_{\mu,\lambda}^{g}=\Big(\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap{\mathscr{D}}^{\lambda}}w\Big)\cdot{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda},

(3.1.4)

\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}}\mathtt{sgn}(w)w={\mathtt{y}}_{\mu}Y_{\mu,\lambda}^{g}=\Big(\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap{\mathscr{D}}^{\lambda}}\mathtt{sgn}(w)w\Big)\cdot{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}.

So the right multiplication with the element

X_{\mu,\lambda}^{g}

defines a homomorphism

(3.1.5)

\xi_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M^{\mu}\simeq\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}{\mathtt{x}}_{\mu}\to\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda}\simeq M^{\lambda},\ m^{\mu}\mapsto\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap{\mathscr{D}}^{\lambda}}wm^{\lambda},

and the right multiplication with the element

Y_{\mu,\lambda}^{g}

defines a homomorphism

(3.1.6)

\eta_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}N^{\mu}\simeq\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}{\mathtt{y}}_{\mu}\to\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}{\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda}\simeq N^{\lambda},\ n^{\mu}\mapsto\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap{\mathscr{D}}^{\lambda}}\mathtt{sgn}(w)wn^{\lambda}

The following is a well-known consequence of the Mackey Theorem:

Lemma 3.1.7
.

We have that

\{\xi_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}\mid g\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}\}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}}(M^{\mu},M^{\lambda})
and
\{\eta_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}\mid g\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}\}
is a basis of
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}}(N^{\mu},N^{\lambda})
.

3.1b.
Classical Schur algebras

For general information on Schur algebras we refer the reader to
[
19
]
. The facts which are needed here are also collected in a compact form (and properly referenced) in
[
4
, §1]
. We recall some facts for reader’s convenience.
Let
d,n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
The corresponding
classical Schur algebra
is

S(n,d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\big(\textstyle\bigoplus_{\nu\in\Uplambda(n,d)}M^{\nu}\big).

(
S(n,0)
is interpreted as
\Bbbk
.)
Recalling the homomorphism
\xi_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}M^{\mu}\to M^{\lambda}
from (
3.1.5
), we denote its trivial extension to an endomorphism of
\bigoplus_{\nu\in\Uplambda(n,d)}M^{\nu}
again by
\xi_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}
. Then Lemma
3.1.7
implies:

Lemma 3.1.8
.

The algebra
S(n,d)
has basis
\{\xi^{g}_{\lambda,\mu}\mid\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d),\,g\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}\}
.

We can also use the right permutation modules
\Bbbk_{\mathfrak{S}_{\nu}}\otimes_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{\nu}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}\simeq{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}(\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d})
instead of the left permutation modules
M^{\lambda}
. Considering the anti-automorphism

i\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}\to\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d},\ \sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}}c_{w}w\mapsto\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}}c_{w}w^{-1},

note that
i({\mathtt{x}}_{\nu})={\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}
for all
\nu
. So there is an algebra isomorphism

(3.1.9)

S(n,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\Big(\bigoplus_{\nu\in\Uplambda(n,d)}{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}(\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d})\Big),

where
\xi^{g}_{\mu,\nu}\in S(n,d)
maps onto the endomorphism which is
0
on the summands
{\mathtt{x}}_{\nu}(\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d})
with
\nu\neq\lambda
and the summand
{\mathtt{x}}_{\lambda}(\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d})
is mapped to the summand
{\mathtt{x}}_{\mu}(\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d})
by the right multiplication with

i(X^{g}_{\mu,\lambda})=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}g\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}\cap\,{}^{\mu}{\mathscr{D}}}w^{-1}=\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}g^{-1}\mathfrak{S}_{\mu}\cap{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu}}w,

where we have used (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
).

We can also use the sign permutation modules
N^{\nu}
instead of the permutation modules
M^{\nu}
. Recalling the homomorphism
\eta_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}N^{\mu}\to N^{\lambda}
from (
3.1.6
), we denote its trivial extension to an endomorphism of
\bigoplus_{\nu\in\Uplambda(n,d)}N^{\nu}
again by
\eta_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}
.

Lemma 3.1.10
.

We have an isomorphism

S(n,d)\simeq\operatorname{End}_{\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}}\big(\textstyle\bigoplus_{\nu\in\Uplambda(n,d)}N^{\nu}\big)

under which each basis element
\xi_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}
of
S(n,d)
with
g\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
corresponds to
\eta_{\lambda,\mu}^{g}
.

The case
q=1
of
[
11
, Theorem 1.11]
yields (see also
[
19
, §2.7]
):

Lemma 3.1.11
.

There is an anti-automorphism
\tau
of
S(n,d)
which maps each
\xi^{g}_{\lambda,\mu}
to
\xi^{g^{-1}}_{\mu,\lambda}
.

Let
W
be a (left)
S(n,d)
-module.
Using the anti-automorphism
\tau
from the lemma, we define the
contravariant dual

W^{\tau}
of
W
to be the (left)
S(n,d)
-module
W^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(W,\Bbbk)
with action defined by
(s\cdot f)(w)=f(\tau(s)w)
for all
s\in S(n,d),\,w\in W,\,f\in W^{*}
.
We also define the right
S(n,d)
-module
\tilde{W}
to be
W
as a
\Bbbk
-module, with right action defined by
ws=\tau(s)w
for
w\in M,\,s\in S(n,d)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
(d_{1},\dots,d_{k})\in\Uplambda(k,d)
. As in
[
4
, (1.3.1)]
, there is an explicit algebra map

S(n,d)\to S(n,d_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes S(n,d_{k}).

So, given
S(n,d_{t})
-modules
W_{t}
for
t=1,\dots,k
, we can view the tensor product

(3.1.12)

W_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{k}

as an
S(n,d)
-module.

We have the orthogonal idempotents

(3.1.13)

\xi_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\xi^{1}_{\lambda,\lambda}\in S(n,d)\qquad(\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d))

summing to the identity in
S(n,d)
.
So for any
S(n,d)
-module
W
,
we have the
weight space decomposition

W=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)}\xi_{\lambda}W.

Recall
\omega_{d}
from (
2.1.10
).

Lemma 3.1.14
.

[
19
, (6.1d)]

If
n\geq d
then there is an algebra isomorphism

\kappa\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}\to\xi_{\omega_{d}}S(n,d)\xi_{\omega_{d}},\ g\mapsto\xi^{g}_{\omega_{d},\omega_{d}}.

For
N\geq n
we define

\Uplambda(N,n;d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\lambda\in\Uplambda(N,d)\mid\lambda_{n+1}=\dots=\lambda_{N}=0\}

and consider the idempotent

\xi(N,n;d)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Uplambda(N,n;d)}\xi_{\lambda}\in S(N,d).

Then there is a natural isomorphism

(3.1.15)

S(n,d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\xi(N,n;d)S(N,d)\xi(N,n;d),

see
[
19
, §6.5]
.
As a special case of (
2.2.8
) and (
2.2.9
), we get the functors

(3.1.16)

{\mathrm{Res}}^{S(N,d)}_{S(n,d)}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathrm{Ind}}^{S(N,d)}_{S(n,d)}.

Let
{\underline{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{k})\in\Uplambda(k,d)
and
{\underline{n}}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\Uplambda(k,n)
. We denote

S({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=S(n_{1},d_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes S(n_{k},d_{k}).

For
t=1,\dots,k+1
, letting
m_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}n_{s}
,
denote

(3.1.17)

\Uplambda({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)\mid\sum_{s=m_{t}+1}^{m_{t+1}}\lambda_{s}=d_{t}\ \text{for all}\ t=1,\dots,k\}.

and define the idempotent

(3.1.18)

\xi({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\Uplambda({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})}\xi_{\lambda}\in S(n,d).

Then we can consider
S({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})
as an explicit (unital) subalgebra of the idempotent truncation
\xi({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})S(n,d)\xi({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})
, referred to as a
Levi subalgebra
, see
[
4
, (1.3g)]
:

(3.1.19)

S({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})\subseteq\xi({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})S(n,d)\xi({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}}).

As a special case of (
2.2.8
) and (
2.2.9
), we get the functors

(3.1.20)

{\mathrm{Res}}^{S(n,d)}_{S({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathrm{Ind}}^{S(n,d)}_{S({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})}.

3.1c.
Representation theory of Schur algebras

Throughout this subsection we assume that
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
.

The irreducible
S(n,d)
-modules are parametrized by the elements of
\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
. We write
L_{n,d}(\lambda)
for the irreducible
S(n,d)
-module corresponding to
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
. In particular,
L_{n,d}(\lambda)
has highest weight
\lambda
, i.e.
\xi_{\lambda}L_{n,d}(\lambda)\neq 0
and
\xi_{\mu}L_{n,d}(\lambda)=0
for
\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d)
unless
\mu\unlhd\lambda
. We have for the contravariant duals
L_{n,d}(\lambda)^{\tau}\simeq L_{n,d}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
.

It is well-known that
S(n,d)
is a quasi-hereditary algebra with weight poset
(\Uplambda_{+}(n,d),\unlhd)
.
In particular, associated to
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
we have the standard module
\Delta_{n,d}(\lambda)
(resp. costandard module
\nabla_{n,d}(\lambda)
) such that
\Delta_{n,d}(\lambda)
(resp.
\nabla_{n,d}(\lambda)
) has simple head (resp. socle) isomorphic to
L_{n,d}(\lambda)
, and all other composition factors are of the form
L_{n,d}(\mu)
with
\mu\lhd\lambda
. We have
\Delta_{n,d}(\lambda)^{\tau}\simeq\nabla_{n,d}(\lambda)

for all
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
.

Recalling the notation
\tilde{W}
from the previous subsection, we also have the right modules
\tilde{L}_{n,d}(\lambda),\tilde{\Delta}_{n,d}(\lambda)
and
\tilde{\nabla}_{n,d}(\lambda)
for each
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
. The following general fact on quasi-hereditary algebras is well-known, cf.
[
20
, Theorem 7.1(iii)]
:

Lemma 3.1.21
.

As an
(S(n,d),S(n,d))
-bimodule,

S(n,d)
has a filtration with factors isomorphic to
\Delta_{n,d}(\lambda)\otimes\tilde{\Delta}_{n,d}(\lambda)
, each appearing once for each
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
and ordered in any way refining the dominance order on partitions so that factors corresponding to more dominant
\lambda
appear lower in the filtration.

A finite dimensional
S(n,d)
-module
W
has a
standard
(resp.
costandard
) filtration if
W
has a filtration
0=W_{0}\subset W_{1}\subset\dots\subset W_{r}=W
such that each sub-quotient
W_{s}/W_{s-1}
is isomorphic to a standard (resp. costandard) module.
The following is well-known:

Lemma 3.1.22
.

Let
(d_{1},\dots,d_{k})\in\Uplambda(d)
, and let
W_{t}
be a finite dimensional
S(n,d_{t})
-module for
t=1,\dots,k
.
If each module
W_{t}
has a standard (resp. costandard) filtration
then so does the
S(n,d)
-module
W_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes W_{k}
.

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
. We define the
S(n,d)
-modules

\displaystyle S^{\lambda}_{n,d}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\nabla_{n,d}((\lambda_{1}))\otimes\dots\otimes\nabla_{n,d}((\lambda_{n})),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}_{n,d}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Delta_{n,d}((\lambda_{1}))\otimes\dots\otimes\Delta_{n,d}((\lambda_{n})).

Assuming
d\leq n
and recalling the notation (
2.1.10
), we also define the
S(n,d)
-module

(3.1.23)

\Lambda^{\lambda}_{n,d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=L_{n,d}(\omega_{\lambda_{1}})\otimes\dots\otimes L_{n,d}(\omega_{\lambda_{n}}).

By Lemma
3.1.22
, the module
S^{\lambda}_{n,d}
has a costandard filtration, and the module
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{n,d}
has a standard filtration. Moreover, since for any
l\leq n
we have
L_{n,l}(\omega_{l})=\Delta_{n,l}(\omega_{l})=\nabla_{n,l}(\omega_{l})
, the module
\Lambda^{\lambda}_{n,d}
has both a standard and a costandard filtration, i.e. is a
tilting module
. Also, using the formal characters it is easy to establish the following well-known fact:

Lemma 3.1.24
.

Let
d\leq n
and
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
. Then, interpreting the transposed partition
\lambda^{\prime}
as an element of
\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
, we have that
L_{n,d}(\lambda^{\prime})
appears as a composition factor of
\Lambda^{\lambda}_{n,d}
with multiplicity
1
, and
L_{n,d}(\mu)
is a composition factor of
\Lambda^{\lambda}_{n,d}
only if
\mu\unlhd\lambda^{\prime}
.

Recall the isomorphism
\kappa
from Lemma
3.1.14
. The following is well-known, see for example
[
4
, (1.3b)]
.

Lemma 3.1.25
.

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
. We have isomorphisms of
S(n,d)
-modules:

(i)

S(n,d)\xi_{\lambda}\simeq\Gamma^{\lambda}_{n,d}
.

(ii)

If
n\geq d
, then
S(n,d)\kappa({\mathtt{y}}_{\lambda})\simeq\Lambda^{\lambda}_{n,d}
.

Lemma 3.1.26
.

[
4
, (1.3d)]
Let
n\geq d
,
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
, and consider the transposed partition
\lambda^{\prime}
as an element of
\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
. Then

\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{S(n,d)}(\Gamma^{\lambda}_{n,d},\Lambda^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{n,d})=1,

and the image of any non-zero homomorphism
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{n,d}\to\Lambda^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{n,d}
is isomorphic to
\Delta_{n,d}(\lambda)
.

Lemma 3.1.27
.

[
19
, §6.5]

Let
N\geq n\geq d
. Then the functors (
3.1.16
) yield a Morita equivalence between
S(N,d)
and
S(n,d)
. Moreover, considering any
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
as an element of
\Uplambda_{+}(N,d)
, we have

{\mathrm{Res}}^{S(N,d)}_{S(n,d)}L_{N,d}(\lambda)\simeq L_{n,d}(\lambda),

{\mathrm{Res}}^{S(N,d)}_{S(n,d)}\Delta_{N,d}(\lambda)\simeq\Delta_{n,d}(\lambda)

and

{\mathrm{Res}}^{S(N,d)}_{S(n,d)}\nabla_{N,d}(\lambda)\simeq\nabla_{n,d}(\lambda).

Lemma 3.1.28
.

[
5
, Theorem 2.7]

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
{\underline{n}}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{k})\in\Uplambda(k,n)
and
{\underline{d}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{k})\in\Uplambda(k,d)
, with
d_{t}\leq n_{t}
for all
t
. Then for the modules
W_{1}\in S(n_{1},d_{1})\!\operatorname{-mod},\dots,W_{k}\in S(n_{k},d_{k})\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we have a functorial isomorphism

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{S({\underline{n}},{\underline{d}})}^{S(n,d)}(W_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\,W_{k})\simeq({\mathrm{Ind}}_{S(n_{1},d_{1})}^{S(n,d_{1})}W_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes({\mathrm{Ind}}_{S(n_{k},d_{k})}^{S(n,d_{k})}W_{k}).

3.1d.
Tilting modules and Ringel duality

Throughout the subsection we again assume that
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
.
We will use the theory of tilting modules and Ringel duality for quasi-hereditary algebras, see for example
[
13
, § A4]
for details. For a quasi-hereditary (graded super)algebra
A
with weight poset
(X,\leq)
, a finite dimensional graded
A
-supermodule is called
tilting
if it has both a standard and a costandard filtrations. For each
\lambda\in X
, there exists a unique indecomposable tilting module
T(\lambda)
such that
[T(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Delta(\lambda)]=1
and
[T(\lambda)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Delta(\mu)]=0
unless
\mu\leq\lambda
. Furthermore, every tilting module is isomorphic to a direct sum of indecomposable tilting modules
T(\lambda)
. A
full tilting
module is a tilting module that contains every
T(\lambda),\ \lambda\in X,
as a summand.

Given a full tilting module
T
, the
Ringel dual
of
S
relative to
T
is the graded superalgebra
S^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{End}_{S}(T)^{\mathrm{sop}}
.
Then
S^{\prime}
is also a quasi-hereditary graded superalgebra with weight poset
X
, but ordered with the opposite order. The right tilting modules are defined similarly to the left tilting modules.

Lemma 3.1.29
.

[
4
, Lemma 4.5a]

Regarded as a right graded
S^{\prime}
-supermodule,
T
is a full tilting module for
S^{\prime}
. Moreover,
\operatorname{End}_{S^{\prime}}(T)\cong S
.

Applying the above theory to the Schur algebra
S(n,d)
, we obtain the indecomposable tilting modules
\{T_{n,d}(\lambda)\mid\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)\}
over
S(n,d)
.

Lemma 3.1.30
.

Let
d\leq n
and
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{+}(n,d)
. Then
\Lambda_{n,d}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
is a tilting module, the indecomposable module

T_{n,d}(\lambda)
occurs exactly once as a summand of
\Lambda_{n,d}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
, and if
T_{n,d}(\mu)
is a summand of
\Lambda_{n,d}^{\lambda^{\prime}}
then
\mu\unlhd\lambda
.

Proof.

This is well-known, see for example
[
13
, Section 3.3(1)]
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.31
.

Let
d\leq n
. Then every composition factor of both the socle and the head of
T_{n,d}(\lambda)
belongs to the head of the projective
S(n,d)
-module
S(n,d)\xi_{\omega_{d}}
.

Proof.

This is well-known. For example, one can use that
S(n,d)\xi_{\omega_{d}}\cong V_{n,1}^{\otimes d}
is contravariantly self-dual, and the known fact that
T_{n,d}(\lambda)
is both a submodule and a quotient of
S(n,d)\xi_{\omega_{d}}
, which can be seen using Lemmas
3.1.30
and
3.1.25
(ii).
∎

3.2.
Affine Brauer tree algebras

Recall the notation (
2.1.1
).

3.2a.
Brauer tree superalgebras
A_{\ell}
and
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}

We will need two Morita eqivalent versions of a graded Brauer tree superalgebra. Let
A_{\ell}
be the path algebra of the quiver

0
1
2
\cdots
\ell-2
\ell-1
u
a^{[1,0]}
a^{[2,1]}
a^{[3,2]}
a^{[\ell-3,\ell-2]}
a^{[\ell-1,\ell-2]}
a^{[0,1]}
a^{[1,2]}
a^{[2,3]}
a^{[\ell-3,\ell-2]}
a^{[\ell-2,\ell-1]}

generated by the length
0
paths
\{e^{[j]}\mid j\in J\}
, and the length
1
paths

\big\{a^{[k,k+1]},a^{[k+1,k]}\mid k\in\{0,1,\dots,\ell-2\}\big\}\quad\text{and}\quad u,

modulo the following relations:

(3.2.1)

\displaystyle\text{all paths of length three or greater are zero};

(3.2.2)

\displaystyle\text{all paths of length two that are not cycles are zero};

(3.2.3)

\displaystyle\text{the length-two cycles based at the vertex $i\in\{1,\dots,\ell-1\}$ are equal};

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle u^{2}=a^{[0,1]}a^{[1,0]}.

Let

c^{[0]}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=u^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^{[i]}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=a^{[i,i-1]}a^{[i-1,i]}\quad\text{for $i=1,\dots,\ell-1$}.

Then

\displaystyle B_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{e^{[j]},c^{[j]}\mid j\in J\}\cup\big\{a^{[k,k+1]},a^{[k+1,k]}\mid k\in\{0,1,\dots,\ell-2\}\big\}\cup\{u\}

is a basis of
A_{\ell}
.
We consider
A_{\ell}
as a graded superalgebra by setting

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}(e^{[j]})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(0,{\bar{0}}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(2,{\bar{1}}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}(a^{[k+1,k]})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(4,{\bar{0}}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}(a^{[k,k+1]})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(0,{\bar{0}}).

We will also need a
regrading

{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
of
A_{\ell}
, see Example
2.2.23
. By definition,

(3.2.5)

{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{i,j\in J}(\Uppi{\mathpzc{Q}}^{2})^{j-i}e^{[i]}A_{\ell}e^{[j]},

i.e.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
is obtained from
A_{\ell}
by regrading, using the grading supershift parameters

(3.2.6)

t_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=2j+1-2\ell\quad\text{and}\quad{\varepsilon}_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bar{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=j\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }2)\,\qquad(j\in J),

see (
2.2.24
). In particular,
A_{\ell}
and
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
are graded Morita superequivalent, see Example
2.2.23
.

The elements of
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
corresponding to
e^{[j]},c^{[j]},a^{[i,j]},u\in A_{\ell}
will be denoted
{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]},{\mathsf{c}}^{[j]},{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]},{\mathsf{u}}
, respectively. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}({\mathsf{e}}^{[j]})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(0,{\bar{0}}),\ \operatorname{bideg}({\mathsf{u}})=\operatorname{bideg}({\mathsf{a}}^{[k+1,k]})=\operatorname{bideg}({\mathsf{a}}^{[k,k+1]})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(2,{\bar{1}}).

We have a basis of
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
corresponding to the basis
B_{\ell}
of
A_{\ell}
above:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]},{\mathsf{c}}^{[j]}\mid j\in J\}\cup\big\{{\mathsf{a}}^{[k,k+1]},{\mathsf{a}}^{[k+1,k]}\mid k\in\{0,1,\dots,\ell-2\}\big\}\cup\{{\mathsf{u}}\}

Note that all
c^{[j]}\in A_{\ell}
and
{\mathsf{c}}^{[j]}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
have bidegree
(4,{\bar{0}})
.

Let
j\in J
. We let
{\mathsf{L}}_{j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Bbbk\cdot{\mathsf{v}}_{j}
be the rank
1
free graded
\Bbbk
-supermodule with

\operatorname{bideg}({\mathsf{v}}_{j})=(0,{\bar{0}}).

We make
{\mathsf{L}}_{j}
into a graded
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
-supermodule via
{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]}{\mathsf{v}}_{j}={\mathsf{v}}_{j}
and
{\mathsf{b}}{\mathsf{v}}_{j}=0
for all
{\mathsf{b}}\in{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell}\setminus\{{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]}\}
.
Taking into account (
2.2.25
), under the graded Morita superequivalence between
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
and
A_{\ell}
, the graded supermodule
{\mathsf{L}}_{j}
corresponds to the graded
A_{\ell}
-supermodule
L_{j}=\Bbbk\cdot v_{j}
with

(3.2.7)

\operatorname{bideg}(v_{j})=(2j+1-2\ell,j\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }2)\,)

and the action
e^{[j]}v_{j}=v_{j}
and
bv_{j}=0
for all
b\in B_{\ell}\setminus\{e^{[j]}\}
. Recalling the notation (
2.2.1
), note that

(3.2.8)

{\mathsf{L}}_{j}\simeq{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]}/({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]})^{>0},

but it is not true that
L_{j}\simeq A_{\ell}e^{[j]}/(A_{\ell}e^{[j]})^{>0}

(which is the first indication that the degree shifts we chose to switch from
A_{\ell}
to
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
are convenient).

3.2b.
Affine Brauer tree superalgebras
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
and
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})

Now, fix
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. We now review the (graded) affine (super)algebra
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
and its counterpart
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
, referring the reader to
[
33
, §2.2f]
for more details.
For
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}\cdots i_{d}\in J^{d}
, denote

(3.2.9)

\begin{split}e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=e^{[i_{1}]}\otimes\dots\otimes e^{[i_{d}]}\in A_{\ell}^{\otimes d}\quad(\text{resp.}\ {\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathsf{e}}^{[i_{1}]}\otimes\dots\otimes{\mathsf{e}}^{[i_{d}]}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}^{\otimes d}).\end{split}

Let
z,{\mathsf{z}}
be variables of bidegree
(4,{\bar{0}})
and consider the polynomial algebras
\Bbbk[z]
and
\Bbbk[{\mathsf{z}}]
. We denote by
A_{\ell}[z]
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]
) the free product
\Bbbk[z]\star A_{\ell}
(resp.
\Bbbk[{\mathsf{z}}]\star{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
) subject to the relations

(3.2.10)

uz=-zu\quad\text{and}\quad bz=zb\ \ \text{for all even $b\in B_{\ell}$}

(3.2.11)

(\text{resp.}\ \ {\mathsf{b}}{\mathsf{z}}=-{\mathsf{z}}{\mathsf{b}}\ \ \text{for all odd ${\mathsf{b}}\in{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell}$}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{b}}{\mathsf{z}}={\mathsf{z}}{\mathsf{b}}\ \ \text{for all even ${\mathsf{b}}\in{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell}$}).

Then

A_{\ell}[z]
(resp.

{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]
) has basis

\{z^{n}b\mid n\in\mathbb{N},\,b\in B_{\ell}\}

(resp.

\{{\mathsf{z}}^{n}{\mathsf{b}}\mid n\in\mathbb{N},\,{\mathsf{b}}\in{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell}\}
).

Note that
A_{\ell}
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
) can be considered both as a subalgebra and as a quotient algebra of
A_{\ell}[z]
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]
). Considering
A_{\ell}
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
) as a quotient of
A_{\ell}[z]
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]
) by the ideal generated by
z
(resp.
{\mathsf{z}}
), we can inflate the graded

A_{\ell}
-supermodule
L_{j}
(resp.

{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
-supermodule
{\mathsf{L}}_{j}
) to a graded
A_{\ell}[z]
-supermodule
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]
-supermodule), which we denote again by
L_{j}
(resp.
{\mathsf{L}}_{j}
). Similarly to (
3.2.8
), we have

(3.2.12)

{\mathsf{L}}_{j}\simeq{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]}/({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]})^{>0}.

Consider the graded superalgebra

A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]^{\otimes d}
). Using the notation (
2.2.4
), this algebra has basis

\displaystyle\{z_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots z_{d}^{n_{d}}b^{(1)}_{1}\cdots b^{(d)}_{d}\mid n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{N},\ b^{(1)},\dots,b^{(d)}\in B_{\ell}\}

\displaystyle(\text{resp.}\ \{{\mathsf{z}}_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots{\mathsf{z}}_{d}^{n_{d}}{\mathsf{b}}^{(1)}_{1}\cdots{\mathsf{b}}^{(d)}_{d}\mid n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{N},\ {\mathsf{b}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathsf{b}}^{(d)}\in{\mathsf{B}}_{\ell}\}).

Recall that we always consider the group algebra
\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
as a graded superalgebra concentrated in bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
. We consider the free product
A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\star\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]^{\otimes d}\star\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
). For any
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in J^{d}
, we have that
e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\in A_{\ell}^{\otimes d}\subseteq A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\subseteq A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\star\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
and similarly we consider
{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
as an element of
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]^{\otimes d}\star\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
.

Recalling the notation (
2.2.3
), (
2.2.4
), we define the
affine Brauer tree (graded super)algebra
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
to be the free product
A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\star\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
subject to the following relations:

(3.2.13)

w\,(a^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes a^{(d)})={}^{w}(a^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes a^{(d)})\,w

for all
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d},\ a^{(1)},\dots,a^{(d)}\in A_{\ell}
, and

(3.2.14)

(s_{r}z_{t}-z_{s_{r}(t)}s_{r})e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=\begin{cases}\big((\delta_{r,t}-\delta_{r+1,t})(c_{r}^{[i_{r}]}+c_{r+1}^{[i_{r+1}]})&\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta_{i_{r},0}u_{r}u_{r+1}\big)e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\text{if}\ i_{r}=i_{r+1},\\
(\delta_{r,t}-\delta_{r+1,t})a_{r}^{[i_{r+1},i_{r}]}a_{r+1}^{[i_{r},i_{r+1}]}e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\text{if}\ |i_{r}-i_{r+1}|=1,\\
0&\textup{otherwise},\end{cases}

for all
1\leq r<d
,
1\leq t\leq d
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in J^{d}
.

Similarly, the
affine Brauer tree (graded super)algebra
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
is defined to be the free product
A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\star\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
subject to the following relations:

(3.2.15)

w\,({\mathsf{a}}^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{(d)})={}^{w}({\mathsf{a}}^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{(d)})\,w

for all
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d},\ {\mathsf{a}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathsf{a}}^{(d)}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
, and

(3.2.16)

(s_{r}{\mathsf{z}}_{t}-{\mathsf{z}}_{s_{r}(t)}s_{r}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=\begin{cases}\big((\delta_{r,t}-\delta_{r+1,t})({\mathsf{c}}_{r}^{[i_{r}]}+{\mathsf{c}}_{r+1}^{[i_{r+1}]})&\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta_{i_{r},0}{\mathsf{u}}_{r}{\mathsf{u}}_{r+1}\big){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\text{if}\ i_{r}=i_{r+1},\\
(\delta_{r,t}+\delta_{r+1,t}){\mathsf{a}}_{r}^{[i_{r+1},i_{r}]}{\mathsf{a}}_{r+1}^{[i_{r},i_{r+1}]}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\text{if}\ |i_{r}-i_{r+1}|=1,\\
0&\textup{otherwise}\end{cases}

for all
1\leq r<d
,
1\leq t\leq d
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in J^{d}
. (A sign change in (
3.2.16
) compared to (
3.2.14
) is related to the fact that the
{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}
are odd, while the
a^{[i,j]}
are even).

There are natural graded superalgebra homomorphisms (of bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
):

(3.2.17)

\displaystyle\iota_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\to H_{d}(A_{\ell})\quad\text{and}\quad\iota_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}\to H_{d}(A_{\ell}),

and similarly for
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
.
By the following theorem, these maps are in fact embeddings.
We will use the same symbols for elements of the domain of these maps as for their images in
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
, and similarly for
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
.

Theorem 3.2.18
.

[
33
, Theorem 3.9]

The map

A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\otimes\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}\to H_{d}(A_{\ell}),\ x\otimes y\mapsto\iota_{1}(x)\iota_{2}(y)

is an isomorphism of graded superspaces. In particular,

\displaystyle\{w\,z_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots z_{d}^{n_{d}}b^{(1)}_{1}\cdots b^{(d)}_{d}\mid n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{N},\ b^{(1)},\dots,b^{(d)}\in B_{\ell},\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}\},

\displaystyle\{z_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots z_{d}^{n_{d}}b^{(1)}_{1}\cdots b^{(d)}_{d}\,w\mid n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{N},\ b^{(1)},\dots,b^{(d)}\in B_{\ell},\,w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}\}.

are bases of
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
. The similar statements hold for
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
.

By the theorem and relations,
A_{\ell}^{\otimes d}\otimes\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}
is a graded subsuperalgebra of
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
isomorphic to the wreath superproduct
A_{\ell}\wr_{\mathop{\tt s}\nolimits}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
. The quotient of
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
by the graded superideal generated by
z_{1},\dots,z_{d}
is also isomorphic to
A_{\ell}\wr_{\mathop{\tt s}\nolimits}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
. The similar statements hold for
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
. We identify
A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}
(resp.
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]^{\otimes d}
) with a graded subsuperalgebra of
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
(resp.
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
). Since
\deg(w)=0
for all
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
, we deduce from the theorem:

Corollary 3.2.19
.

We have:

(i)

The algebras
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
and
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
are non-negatively graded.

(ii)

H_{d}(A_{\ell})^{>0}=H_{d}(A_{\ell})(A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d})^{>0}
and
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})^{>0}=H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]^{\otimes d})^{>0}.

(iii)

The degree zero component
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})^{0}
of
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
is generated by all
{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$j$}}
with
\text{\boldmath$j$}\in J^{d}
and
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
.

3.2c.
Comparing
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
and
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})

Recall that the graded superalgebra
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
is obtained from the graded superalgebra
A_{\ell}
by regrading. Already for the graded superalgebras
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}\wr_{\mathop{\tt s}\nolimits}\mathfrak{S}_{d}
and
A_{\ell}\wr_{\mathop{\tt s}\nolimits}\mathfrak{S}_{d}

the similar statement is far from clear (in fact, it is even far from obvious that they are isomorphic as algebras). Even less obvious is the same claim on the affine level, namely, that the graded superalgebra
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
is obtained from the graded superalgebra
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
by regrading.
Proposition
3.2.28
below, inspired by
[
17
, Lemma 5.27]
, shows that this is the case.

We note that
\sum_{\text{\boldmath$i$}\in J^{n}}e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
is an orthogonal idempotent decomposition of the identity in
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
, and similarly for
\sum_{\text{\boldmath$i$}\in J^{n}}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$j$}}
in
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
. Recalling (
3.2.6
), for
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}\cdots i_{d}\in J^{d}
, we denote

(3.2.20)

\begin{split}t_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=t_{i_{1}}+\dots+t_{i_{d}}=2(i_{1}+\dots+i_{d})+d(1-2\ell),\\
{\varepsilon}_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\varepsilon}_{i_{1}}+\dots+{\varepsilon}_{i_{d}}=(i_{1}+\dots+i_{d})\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }2)\,.\end{split}

We will also need the following elements:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{j\in J,\,\,j\,\text{odd}}{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{j\in J,\,\,j\,\text{even}}{\mathsf{e}}^{[j]}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{h}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}-{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell},

\displaystyle{\mathsf{g}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}\otimes 1+{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime\prime}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}\otimes{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}.

Recalling the notation (
2.2.4
), we have the elements

{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}\in{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}^{\otimes d}\subseteq H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})

for
1\leq r<d
. The following properties are easy to check:

Lemma 3.2.21
.

(i)

In
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
, we have:

(3.2.22)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{h}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,

(3.2.23)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}{\mathsf{h}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\mathsf{h}}{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}\qquad(\text{for all admissible $i,j$}),

(3.2.24)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{u}}{\mathsf{h}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\mathsf{u}}\ \,=\ \,{\mathsf{h}}{\mathsf{u}}.

(ii)

In
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}[{\mathsf{z}}]
, we have

(3.2.25)

{\mathsf{z}}{\mathsf{h}}={\mathsf{h}}{\mathsf{z}},

(iii)

In
{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}\otimes{\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}
, we have:

(3.2.26)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{g}}({\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{u}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}){\mathsf{g}},

(3.2.27)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{g}}(1\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}){\mathsf{g}}\qquad(\text{for all admissible $i,j$}).

We now prove the main result of this subsection:

Proposition 3.2.28
.

There is an isomorphism of graded superalgebras

\bigoplus_{\text{\boldmath$i$},\text{\boldmath$j$}\in J^{n}}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{t_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}-t_{\text{\boldmath$j$}}}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}-{\varepsilon}_{\text{\boldmath$j$}}}e^{\text{\boldmath$j$}}H_{d}(A_{\ell})e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\to H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})

which maps (for all admissible indices
r,i,j
):

\displaystyle e^{[i]}_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mathsf{e}}^{[i]}_{r}

\displaystyle u_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r)}

\displaystyle a^{[i,j]}_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}\otimes 1^{\otimes(d-r)}

\displaystyle s_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}

\displaystyle z_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto({\mathsf{z}}{\mathsf{h}})_{r}.

Proof.

We denote by
X\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{e^{[i]},u,a^{[i,j]}\}
the set of the standard generators of
A_{\ell}
, by
Y\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=X\sqcup\{z\}
the set of the standard generators of
A_{\ell}[z]
and by
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{s_{r},x_{t}\mid 1\leq r<d,\,1\leq t\leq d,\,x\in Y\}
the set of the standard generators of
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
.
For every
x\in Z
, we denote by
{\varphi}(x)
the element of
H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
which corresponds to it under the assignments in the statement of the proposition, so the proposition claims that there exists an isomorphism
{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\bigoplus_{\text{\boldmath$i$},\text{\boldmath$j$}\in J^{n}}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{t_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}-t_{\text{\boldmath$j$}}}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}-{\varepsilon}_{\text{\boldmath$j$}}}e^{\text{\boldmath$j$}}H_{d}(A_{\ell})e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\to H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
of graded superalgebras with the prescribed
{\varphi}(x)
on the generators
x
. In fact, it suffices to prove that there is such a homomorphism since then it is an isomorphism by bases, see Theorem
3.2.18
.
Noting that
\operatorname{bideg}({\varphi}(x))=\operatorname{bideg}(x)
for al
x\in Z
,
it suffices to check that
{\varphi}(x)
’s satisfy the defining relations of
H_{d}(A_{\ell})
.

Claim 1.
For each
1\leq r\leq d
, there is a graded superalgebra homomorphism
{\varphi}_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A_{\ell}[z]\to H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell})
with

{\varphi}_{r}(x)={\varphi}(x_{r})
for all
x\in Y
.

We check that the elements
{\varphi}(x_{r})
satisfy the defining relations of
A_{\ell}[z]
. The
relations (
3.2.1
), (
3.2.2
), (
3.2.3
) are clear. To check the relation (
3.2.4
), we need to verify that

{\varphi}(a^{[0,1]}_{r}){\varphi}_{r}(a^{[1,0]})={\varphi}(u_{r})^{2}

which is easy to see using (
3.2.22
).
Finally, to check (
3.2.10
), it is immediate that

{\varphi}(e^{[i]}_{r}){\varphi}_{r}(z)={\varphi}(z_{r}){\varphi}(e^{[i]}_{r}).

Moreover,

{\varphi}(a^{[i,j]}_{r}){\varphi}_{r}(z)={\varphi}(z_{r}){\varphi}(a^{[i,j]}_{r})

thanks to (
3.2.11
) and (
3.2.23
), and

{\varphi}(u_{r}){\varphi}(z_{r})=-{\varphi}(z_{r}){\varphi}(u_{r})

thanks to (
3.2.11
) and (
3.2.24
).

Claim 2.
Let
1\leq r<d
and
x,y\in Y
. Then
{\varphi}_{r}(x){\varphi}_{r+1}(y)={\varphi}_{r+1}(y){\varphi}_{r}(x)
unless
x=y=u
, in which case
{\varphi}_{r}(u){\varphi}_{r+1}(u)=-{\varphi}_{r+1}(u){\varphi}_{r}(u)
.

Note, using the notation (
2.2.4
), the equalities (
3.2.22
),(
3.2.24
) and the fact that
{\mathsf{u}}
is odd,

\displaystyle{\varphi}_{r}(u){\varphi}_{r+1}(u)=({\mathsf{u}}{\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}{\mathsf{u}})_{r,r+1}=({\mathsf{h}}{\mathsf{u}}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}{\mathsf{h}})_{r,r+1}=-{\varphi}_{r+1}(u){\varphi}_{r}(u).

Moreover, using (
3.2.23
) and the fact that
{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}
and
{\mathsf{a}}^{[k,l]}
are odd, we get

\displaystyle{\varphi}_{r}(a^{[i,j]}){\varphi}_{r+1}(a^{[k,l]})

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}{\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[k,l]})_{r,r+1}=-({\mathsf{h}}{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[k,l]})_{r,r+1}

\displaystyle={\varphi}_{r+1}(a^{[k,l]}){\varphi}_{r}(a^{[i,j]}).

Similarly we get

\displaystyle{\varphi}_{r}(a^{[i,j]}){\varphi}_{r+1}(u)

\displaystyle=1^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}{\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r-1)}

\displaystyle=-1^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r-1)}

\displaystyle={\varphi}_{r+1}(u){\varphi}_{r}(a^{[i,j]})

and

{\varphi}_{r}(u){\varphi}_{r+1}(a^{[i,j]})={\varphi}_{r+1}(a^{[i,j]}){\varphi}_{r}(u)
.
The remaining cases are easy to check.

Claim 3.
There is a graded superalgebra homomorphism

{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}A_{\ell}[z]^{\otimes d}\to H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}),\ x^{(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes x^{(d)}\mapsto{\varphi}_{1}(x^{(1)})\cdots{\varphi}_{d}(x^{(d)}).

This follows from Claim 2 since in the generating set
Y

the element
u
is odd and all other elements are even.

Claim 4.
There is a graded superalgebra homomorphism

{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Bbbk\mathfrak{S}_{d}\to H_{d}({\mathsf{A}}_{\ell}),\ s_{r}\mapsto s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}.

Indeed, the elements
s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}
are checked to satisfy the Coxeter relations, for example,

(s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1})^{2}=s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}=s_{r}s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}=1

and

\displaystyle s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}s_{r+1}{\mathsf{g}}_{r+1,r+2}s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}

\displaystyle=s_{r}s_{r+1}s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r+1,r+2}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+2}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}

\displaystyle=s_{r+1}s_{r}s_{r+1}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+2}{\mathsf{g}}_{r+1,r+2}

\displaystyle=s_{r+1}{\mathsf{g}}_{r+1,r+2}s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}s_{r+1}{\mathsf{g}}_{r+1,r+2}.

Claim 5.
For any
1\leq r<d
and
x\in X
, we have
{\varphi}(s_{r}){\varphi}(x_{r})={\varphi}(x_{r+1}){\varphi}(s_{r})
.

Indeed, for
x=u
, using
s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}={\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}s_{r}
and (
3.2.26
), we have

\displaystyle{\varphi}(s_{r}){\varphi}(u_{r})

\displaystyle=(s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1})({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r)})

\displaystyle=s_{r}({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes({\mathsf{g}}({\mathsf{u}}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}))\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r-1)})

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes({\mathsf{g}}({\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}))\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r-1)})s_{r}

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes(({\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}){\mathsf{g}})\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r-1)})s_{r}

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes({\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{u}})\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r-1)}){\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}s_{r}

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes r}\otimes{\mathsf{u}}\otimes{\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(d-r-1)})s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}

\displaystyle={\varphi}(u_{r+1}){\varphi}(s_{r}).

For
x=a^{[i,j]}
,
we have similarly, using (
3.2.27
),

\displaystyle{\varphi}(s_{r}){\varphi}(a^{[i,j]}_{r})

\displaystyle=(s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1})({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}\otimes 1^{\otimes(d-r)})

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes({\mathsf{g}}(1\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}))\otimes 1^{\otimes(d-r-1)})s_{r}

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes(r-1)}\otimes(({\mathsf{h}}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}){\mathsf{g}})\otimes 1^{\otimes(d-r-1)})s_{r}

\displaystyle=({\mathsf{h}}^{\otimes r}\otimes{\mathsf{a}}^{[i,j]}\otimes 1^{\otimes(d-r-1)})s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}

\displaystyle={\varphi}(a^{[i,j]}_{r+1}){\varphi}(s_{r}).

For
x=e^{[i]}
the argument is similar (but easier).

From Claims 3,4,5 we have all the required relations except (
3.2.13
). This final relation for the case
t\not\in\{r,r+1\}
is easy to check, and in the presence of other relations, the case
t=r+1
follows from the case
t=r
(by multiplying with
s_{r}
on both sides). So we may assume that
t=r
. In this case, we have using (
3.2.14
),

\displaystyle({\varphi}(s_{r}){\varphi}(z_{r})-{\varphi}(z_{r+1}){\varphi}(s_{r})){\varphi}(e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}})

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}({\mathsf{z}}{\mathsf{h}})_{r}-({\mathsf{z}}{\mathsf{h}})_{r+1}s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{z}}_{r}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}-{\mathsf{z}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{h}}_{r+1}s_{r}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(s_{r}{\mathsf{z}}_{r}-{\mathsf{z}}_{r+1}s_{r}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}({\mathsf{c}}_{r}^{[i_{r}]}+{\mathsf{c}}_{r+1}^{[i_{r+1}]}+\delta_{i_{r},0}{\mathsf{u}}_{r}{\mathsf{u}}_{r+1}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}&\text{if}\ i_{r}=i_{r+1},\\
{\mathsf{a}}_{r}^{[i_{r+1},i_{r}]}{\mathsf{a}}_{r+1}^{[i_{r},i_{r+1}]}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}&\text{if}\ |i_{r}-i_{r+1}|=1,\\
0&\textup{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
i_{r}=i_{r+1}=i
then
{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}={\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
; if, in addition
i=0
, then

{\mathsf{u}}_{r}{\mathsf{u}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}={\mathsf{u}}_{r}{\mathsf{u}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}{\mathsf{h}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}={\mathsf{u}}_{r}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}{\mathsf{h}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{u}}_{r+1}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}={\varphi}(u_{r}){\varphi}(u_{r+1}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}.

So in this case we are getting

({\mathsf{c}}_{r}^{[i]}+{\mathsf{c}}_{r+1}^{[i]}+\delta_{i,0}{\mathsf{u}}_{r}{\mathsf{u}}_{r+1}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=({\varphi}(c_{r}^{[i]})+{\varphi}(c_{r+1}^{[i]})+\delta_{i,0}{\varphi}(u_{r}){\varphi}(u_{r+1})){\varphi}(e^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}),

as required. If
|i_{r}-i_{r+1}|=1
then, considering two cases when
i_{r}
is even or odd, we get

\displaystyle{\mathsf{a}}_{r}^{[i_{r+1},i_{r}]}{\mathsf{a}}_{r+1}^{[i_{r},i_{r+1}]}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}{\mathsf{g}}_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{a}}_{r}^{[i_{r+1},i_{r}]}{\mathsf{a}}_{r+1}^{[i_{r},i_{r+1}]}({\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}\otimes 1-{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime\prime}\otimes{\mathsf{h}})_{r,r+1}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{a}}_{r}^{[i_{r+1},i_{r}]}{\mathsf{h}}_{r}{\mathsf{a}}_{r+1}^{[i_{r},i_{r+1}]}{\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}}

\displaystyle={\varphi}(a_{r}^{[i_{r+1},i_{r}]}){\varphi}(a_{r+1}^{[i_{r},i_{r+1}]}){\mathsf{e}}^{\text{\boldmath$i$}},

as required. The remaining case
|i_{r}-i_{r+1}|>1
is clear.
∎

3.3.
Quiver Hecke superalgebras

We will work with the quiver Hecke superalgebra of type
A_{2\ell}^{(2)}
, as defined in
[
26
]
.

3.3a.
Definition of the quiver Hecke superalgebra

For
i,j,k\in I
, we define polynomials
Q_{i,j}(u,v),B_{i,j,k}(u,v)\in\Bbbk[u,v]
as follows:

\displaystyle Q_{i,j}(u,v)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\hbox{if $i=j$,}\\
1&\hbox{if $|i-j|>1$,}\\
(j-i)(u-v)&\hbox{if $|i-j|=1$ and $1\leq i,j<\ell$,}\\
(j-i)(u^{2}-v)&\hbox{if $\{i,j\}=\{0,1\}$ or $\{\ell-1,\ell\}$, and $\ell>1$,}\\
(j-i)(u^{4}-v)&\hbox{if $\{i,j\}=\{0,1\}$ and $\ell=1$,}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle B_{i,j,k}(u,v)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-1&\hbox{if $i=k=j+1$,}\\
1&\hbox{if $i=k=j-1\not\in\{0,\ell-1\}$,}\\
(u+v)&\hbox{if $i=k=j-1=\ell-1>0$,}\\
(v-u)&\hbox{if $i=k=j-1=0$ and $\ell>1$,}\\
(u^{2}+v^{2})(v-u)&\hbox{if $i=k=j-1=0$ and $\ell=1$,}\\
0&\hbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Let
\theta\in Q_{+}
be of height
n
. The
quiver Hecke superalgebra

R_{\theta}
is the unital graded
\Bbbk
-superalgebra generated by the elements

\{1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mid\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\theta}\}\cup\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}\cup\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{n-1}\}

and the following defining relations (for all admissible
r,s,\text{\boldmath$i$}
, etc.)

(3.3.3)

1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}}=\delta_{\text{\boldmath$i$},\text{\boldmath$j$}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}},

(3.3.4)

\sum_{\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\theta}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=1,

(3.3.5)

y_{r}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}y_{r},

(3.3.6)

\psi_{r}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=1_{s_{r}\cdot\text{\boldmath$i$}}\psi_{r},

(3.3.7)

y_{r}y_{s}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-y_{s}y_{r}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\hbox{if $r\neq s$ and $\|i_{r}\|=\|i_{s}\|={\bar{1}}$,}\\
y_{s}y_{r}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\hbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

(3.3.8)

\psi_{r}y_{s}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=(-1)^{\|i_{r}\|\,\|i_{r+1}\|\,\|i_{s}\|}y_{s}\psi_{r}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\qquad(s\neq r,r+1),

(3.3.9)

\begin{split}(\psi_{r}y_{r+1}-(-1)^{\|i_{r}\|\,\|i_{r+1}\|}y_{r}\psi_{r})1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&=(y_{r+1}\psi_{r}-(-1)^{\|i_{r}\|\,\|i_{r+1}\|}\psi_{r}y_{r})1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\\
&=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}&\hbox{if $i_{r}=i_{r+1}$,}\\
0&\hbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.\end{split}

(3.3.10)

\psi_{r}^{2}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=Q_{i_{r},r_{r+1}}(y_{r},y_{r+1}),

(3.3.11)

\psi_{r}\psi_{s}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=(-1)^{\|i_{r}\|\,\|i_{r+1}\|\,\|i_{s}\|\,\|i_{s+1}\|}\psi_{s}\psi_{r}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\qquad(|r-s|>1),

(3.3.12)

(\psi_{r+1}\psi_{r}\psi_{r+1}-\psi_{r}\psi_{r+1}\psi_{r})1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=B_{i_{r},i_{r+1},i_{r+2}}(y_{r},y_{r+2})1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}

The structure of a graded superalgebra on
R_{\theta}
is defined by setting

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}(\text{\boldmath$i$})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(0,{\bar{0}}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}(y_{s}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=((\alpha_{i_{s}}|\alpha_{i_{s}}),\|i_{s}\|),

\displaystyle\operatorname{bideg}(\psi_{r}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=-((\alpha_{i_{r}}|\alpha_{i_{r+1}}),\|i_{r}\|\,\|i_{r+1}\|).

Sometimes we denote the identity in
R_{\theta}
by
1_{\theta}
.

We will use Khovanov-Lauda diagrams
[
29
]
to represent the elements of
R_{\theta}
. In particular, if
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}\cdots i_{n}\in I^{\theta}
,
1\leq r<n
and
1\leq s\leq n
, we denote

\displaystyle 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=\hbox to60.6pt{\vbox to42.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.29863pt\lower-15.11127pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}

{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{1.2pt,4.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 98.27 36.54 L 125.99 36.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 128.51 0 L 128.51 37.8}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{88.42766pt}{-10.27826pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
