\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

For every
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we choose a reduced decomposition
w=s_{r_{1}}\dots s_{r_{l}}
and define
\psi_{w}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\psi_{r_{1}}\cdots\psi_{r_{l}}
. In general
\psi_{w}
depends on the choice of a reduced decomposition.

Theorem 3.3.13
.

[
26
, Corollary 3.15]

Let
\theta\in Q_{+}
and
n={\operatorname{ht}}(\theta)
. Then

\displaystyle\{\psi_{w}y_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots y_{n}^{k_{n}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mid w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},\ k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{N},\ \text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\theta}\},

\displaystyle\{y_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots y_{n}^{k_{n}}\psi_{w}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mid w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},\ k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{N},\ \text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\theta}\}

are bases of
R_{\theta}
.

Lemma 3.3.14
.

[
8
, Proposition 2.5]

Let
\theta\in Q_{+}
with
{\operatorname{ht}}(\theta)=n
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\theta}
.

(i)

If
w
is an element of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with reduced decompositions
w=s_{t_{1}}\dots s_{t_{m}}=s_{r_{1}}\dots s_{r_{m}}
. Then in
R_{\theta}
we have

\psi_{t_{1}}\dots\psi_{t_{m}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=\psi_{r_{1}}\dots\psi_{r_{m}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}+(*),

where
(*)
is a linear combination of elements of the form
\psi_{u}f(y)1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
such that
u<w
and
f(y)
is a polynomial in
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
.

(ii)

If the decomposition
w=s_{t_{1}}\dots s_{t_{m}}
is not reduced, then the element
\psi_{t_{1}}\dots\psi_{t_{m}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
can be written as a linear combination of elements of the form

\psi_{t_{a_{1}}}\dots\psi_{t_{a_{b}}}f(y)1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}

such that
1\leq a_{1}<\dots<a_{b}\leq m
,
b<m
,
s_{t_{a_{1}}}\dots s_{t_{a_{b}}}
is a reduced word, and
f(y)
is a polynomial in
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
.

We have a graded superalgebra antiautomorphism which is identity on the generators:

(3.3.15)

\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{\theta}\to R_{\theta}

By definition (of superalgebra antiautomorphism), we have
\tau(xy)=\tau(y)\tau(x)
for all
x,y\in R_{\theta}
.

If
V=\bigoplus_{n,{\varepsilon}}V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
is a graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodule, then the
graded dual

V^{\circledast}
is the graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodule
which is the graded dual superspace
V^{*}
with the
R_{\theta}
-action given by
(xf)(v)=f(\tau(x)v)
, for all
f\in V^{\circledast},v\in V,x\in R_{\theta}
.
Given an
even
homomorphism
{\varphi}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}V\to W
of graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodules, we have a homomorphism

(3.3.16)

{\varphi}^{\circledast}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}W^{\circledast}\to V^{\circledast}

of graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodules so that
{\varphi}^{\circledast}(f)=f\circ{\varphi}
for all
f\in W^{\circledast}
.

Note that the graded supercomponents
(R_{\theta})^{n}
are free of finite rank over
\Bbbk
and
(R_{\theta})^{n}=0
for
n\ll 0
.
So, if
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
then the same is true for a finitely generated graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodule
V
and its
word spaces

1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}V
for all
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\theta}
.
At any rate, suppose that
V
is a graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodule such that the graded supercomponents
V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
are free of finite rank over
\Bbbk
and
V_{n}=0
for
n\ll 0
. Then we define the
formal character of
V

\operatorname{ch}_{q,\pi}V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{\theta}}(\dim_{q,\pi}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}V)\,\text{\boldmath$i$}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))\cdot I^{\theta},

where
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))\cdot I^{\theta}
denotes the set of all formal
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))
-linear combinations of the words in
I^{\theta}
.
We say that a graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodule
V

has a well-defined formal character
if all
V^{n}_{\varepsilon}
are free of finite rank over
\Bbbk
and
V^{n}=0
for
n\ll 0
. In particular, if
V
is free of finite rank as a
\Bbbk
-module, then it has a well-defined formal character
\operatorname{ch}_{q,\pi}V\in\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}]\cdot I^{\theta}

and, using (
2.2.2
), we have

(3.3.17)

\operatorname{ch}_{q,\pi}(V^{\circledast})=\overline{\operatorname{ch}_{q,\pi}V},

where we have extended the bar-involution (
2.1.3
) to
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}[q,q^{-1}]\cdot I^{\theta}
in the obvious way.

In fact,

\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))\langle I\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bigoplus_{\theta\in Q_{+}}\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))\cdot I^{\theta}

is a
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))
-algebra with respect to the
quantum shuffle product
defined on words as follows:

(3.3.18)

\text{\boldmath$i$}^{1}\circ\text{\boldmath$i$}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum f_{w}(\pi,q)i_{w(1)}\cdots i_{w(k+l)},

where
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{1}=i_{1}\cdots i_{k}
,
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{2}=i_{k+1}\cdots i_{k+l}
, the sum is over all
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{k+l}
such that
w^{-1}(1)<\cdots<w^{-1}(k)
,
w^{-1}(k+1)<\cdots<w^{-1}(k+l)
, and

f_{w}(\pi,q)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq r\leq k<s\leq k+l\\
w^{-1}(r)>w^{-1}(s)\end{subarray}}\pi^{\|i_{w(r)}\|\,\|i_{w(s)}\|}q^{-(\alpha_{i_{w(r)}}|\alpha_{i_{w(s)}})}

The shuffle product on formal characters corresponds to the parabolic induction on modules, see Corollary
3.3.30
.

3.3b.
Irreducible modules of quiver Hecke superalgebras

By
[
21
, §5.1, Proposition 6.15]
,
[
28
, Theorems 8.6, 8.7(ii)]
(and the argument of
[
29
, Corollary 3.19]
for absolute irreducibility), we have:

Lemma 3.3.19
.

Let
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
.
Every irreducible graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodule
L
is finite dimensional and absolutely irreducible as an
R_{\theta}
-module. Moreover, there exists
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
({\mathpzc{Q}}^{m}L)^{\circledast}\simeq\Uppi^{\varepsilon}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{m}L
for some
{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Recall the set
{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)
of the root partitions of
\theta
from §
2.1d
.

Lemma 3.3.20
.

[
33
, Lemma 3.1.18]

Let
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
. Then
|{\mathrm{Irr}}(R_{\theta})|=|{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)|
.

3.3c.
Induction and restriction for quiver Hecke superalgebras

Let
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}\in Q_{+}
and
\theta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}
. Denote

\underline{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in Q_{+}^{n}
,

R_{\underline{\theta}}=R_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R_{\theta_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes R_{\theta_{n}}

and

(3.3.21)

1_{\underline{\theta}}=1_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{1}\in I^{\theta_{1}},\dots,\text{\boldmath$i$}^{n}\in I^{\theta_{n}}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{1}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{n}}\in R_{\theta}.

By
[
27
, §4.1]
, there is a natural graded superalgebra embedding

(3.3.22)

\iota_{\underline{\theta}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{\underline{\theta}}\to 1_{\underline{\theta}}R_{\theta}1_{\underline{\theta}},

and we identify
R_{\underline{\theta}}
with a subalgebra of
1_{\underline{\theta}}R_{\theta}1_{\underline{\theta}}
via this embedding. We refer to this subalgebra as a
parabolic subalgebra
. Note that
\iota_{\underline{\theta}}(1_{\theta_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes 1_{\theta_{n}})=1_{\underline{\theta}}
.

We have the exact functors:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Res}}^{\theta}_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}}={\mathrm{Res}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\underline{\theta}}R_{\theta}\otimes_{R_{\theta}}?\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{\theta}\!\operatorname{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod},

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ind}}^{\theta}_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}}={\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R_{\theta}1_{\underline{\theta}}\otimes_{R_{\underline{\theta}}}?\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}\!\operatorname{-mod},

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Coind}}^{\theta}_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}}={\mathrm{Coind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{\underline{\theta}}}(1_{\underline{\theta}}R_{\theta},?)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\rightarrow R_{\theta}\!\operatorname{-mod}.

The functor
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}
is left adjoint to
{\mathrm{Res}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}
, and
{\mathrm{Coind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}
is right adjoint to
{\mathrm{Res}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}
.

The following follows immediately from Theorem
3.3.13
.

Lemma 3.3.23
.

Let
\underline{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in Q_{+}^{n}
,

\lambda_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\operatorname{ht}}(\theta_{r})
for
r=1,\dots,n
, and

V\in R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
.
Let
\lambda\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
be a composition of
d\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\lambda_{1}+\dots+\lambda_{n}
. Then, as graded
\Bbbk
-supermodules,

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}V=\bigoplus_{w\in{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}}\psi_{w}\otimes V,

and for each
w\in{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\lambda}
, the map
V\to\psi_{w}\otimes V,\ v\mapsto\psi_{w}\otimes v
is an isomorphism.

The functors of (co)induction and restriction have obvious parabolic analogues. Given a family
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{a,b})_{1\leq a\leq m,\,1\leq b\leq n}
of elements of
Q_{+}
such that
\sum_{a=1}^{m}\gamma_{a,b}=\theta_{b}
for all
1\leq b\leq n
, we have functors

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,1}\,;\,\dots\,;\,\gamma_{1,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}^{\,\theta_{1};\,\dots\,;\,\theta_{n}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,1}\,,\,\dots\,,\,\gamma_{1,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to R_{\,\theta_{1},\,\dots\,,\,\theta_{n}}\!\operatorname{-mod}

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Res}}_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,1}\,;\,\dots\,;\,\gamma_{1,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}^{\,\theta_{1};\,\dots\,;\,\theta_{n}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{\,\theta_{1},\,\dots\,,\,\theta_{n}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to R_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,1}\,,\,\dots\,,\,\gamma_{1,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}\!\operatorname{-mod}.

For
g\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
\underline{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in Q_{+}^{n}
, recalling (
2.1.23
), let

g\underline{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\theta_{g^{-1}(1)},\dots,\theta_{g^{-1}(n)}),

\displaystyle s(g,\underline{\theta})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m<k\leq n,\\
g(m)>g(k)\end{subarray}}(\theta_{m}\mid\theta_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad{\varepsilon}(g,\underline{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq m<k\leq n,\\
g(m)>g(k)\end{subarray}}\|\theta_{m}\|\,\|\theta_{k}\|\in\mathbb{Z}/2.

There is an isomorphism of graded superalgebras

{\varphi}^{g}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}R_{g\underline{\theta}}\to R_{\underline{\theta}},\ x_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{n}\mapsto(-1)^{\sum_{1\leq a<c\leq n,g(a)>g(c)}|x_{a}||x_{c}|}x_{g(1)}\otimes\dots\otimes x_{g(n)}.

Note that this makes sense even when some of
\theta_{k}^{\prime}s
are
0
(with
R_{0}
being interpreted as the trivial
\Bbbk
-algebra
\Bbbk
).
Composing with the isomorphism
{\varphi}^{g}
, we get a functor

R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to R_{g\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ V\mapsto V^{{\varphi}^{g}}.

Making in addition degree and parity shifts, we get a functor

(3.3.24)

R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to R_{g\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ V\mapsto{}^{\{g\}}V\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\Uppi^{-{\varepsilon}(g,\underline{\theta})}{\mathpzc{Q}}^{-s(g,\underline{\theta})}V^{{\varphi}^{g}}.

Let
\underline{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in Q_{+}^{n}
and
\underline{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{m})\in Q_{+}^{m}
with

\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}=\eta_{1}+\dots+\eta_{m}=\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\alpha.
We denote by
{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})
the set of all tuples
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{a,b})_{1\leq a\leq m,\,1\leq b\leq n}
of elements of
Q_{+}
such that
\sum_{a=1}^{m}\gamma_{a,b}=\theta_{b}
for all
1\leq b\leq n
and
\sum_{b=1}^{n}\gamma_{a,b}=\eta_{a}
for all
1\leq a\leq m
. For each
\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})
, we define permutations

w_{m,n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{mn}
(not depending on
\underline{\gamma}
)
and

x(\underline{\gamma})\in\mathfrak{S}_{{\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)}
(depending only on the heights
{\operatorname{ht}}(\gamma_{a,b})
)
as follows. The permutation
w_{m,n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{mn}
maps the ordered
mn
-tuple of symbols

(\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,1},\gamma_{1,2},\dots,\gamma_{m,2},\dots,\gamma_{1,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,n})

onto the ordered
mn
-tuple of symbols

(\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{1,n},\gamma_{2,1},\dots,\gamma_{2,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,n})

while
x(\underline{\gamma})
is the corresponding permutation of integral segments of sizes
{\operatorname{ht}}(\gamma_{a,b})
. Denoting
h_{a,b}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\operatorname{ht}}(\gamma_{a,b})
and considering the composition

\chi=(h_{1,1},\dots,h_{m,1},h_{1,2},\dots,h_{m,2},\dots,h_{1,n},\dots,h_{m,n})\in\Uplambda(mn,{\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)),

we have
x(\underline{\gamma})=w_{\chi,w_{m,n}}
, using the notation introduced in (
2.1.18
).

Example 3.3.25
.

Let
\ell=2
so we have simple roots
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
.
Take

\underline{\eta}=(\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\in Q_{+}^{2}
and
\underline{\theta}=(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1})\in Q_{+}^{3}
. Then
{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})=\{\underline{\gamma},\underline{\beta}\}
, where

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\gamma_{1,1}&\gamma_{1,2}&\gamma_{1,3}\\
\gamma_{2,1}&\gamma_{2,2}&\gamma_{2,3}\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}&0\\
\alpha_{2}&0&\alpha_{1}\end{matrix}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\beta_{1,1}&\beta_{1,2}&\beta_{1,3}\\
\beta_{2,1}&\beta_{2,2}&\beta_{2,3}\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}0&\alpha_{0}&\alpha_{1}\\
\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{1}&0\end{matrix}\right).

Now

\displaystyle w_{2,3}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}1\mapsto 1,\ 2\mapsto 4,\ 3\mapsto 2,\ 4\mapsto 5,\ 5\mapsto 3,\ 6\mapsto 6,

\displaystyle x(\underline{\gamma})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}1\mapsto 3,\ 2\mapsto 1,\ 3\mapsto 2,\ 4\mapsto 4,

\displaystyle x(\underline{\beta})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}1\mapsto 3,\ 2\mapsto 1,\ 3\mapsto 4,\ 4\mapsto 2.

Let
V\in R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
. Note that
for any
\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})
, we have

1_{\underline{\eta}}\psi_{x(\underline{\gamma})}1_{\underline{\theta}}\otimes V\subseteq{\mathrm{Res}}_{\underline{\eta}}^{\alpha}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\alpha}V.

Let
\leq
be a total order refining the Bruhat order on
\mathfrak{S}_{{\operatorname{ht}}(\alpha)}
.
For
\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})
, we consider the submodules

(3.3.26)

\displaystyle F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}(V)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\underline{\kappa}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})\,\text{with}\,x(\underline{\kappa})\leq x(\underline{\gamma})}R_{\underline{\eta}}1_{\underline{\eta}}\psi_{x(\underline{\kappa})}1_{\underline{\theta}}\otimes V\subseteq{\mathrm{Res}}_{\underline{\eta}}^{\alpha}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\alpha}V,

(3.3.27)

\displaystyle F_{<\underline{\gamma}}(V)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{\underline{\kappa}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})\,\text{with}\,x(\underline{\kappa})<x(\underline{\gamma})}R_{\underline{\eta}}1_{\underline{\eta}}\psi_{x(\underline{\kappa})}1_{\underline{\theta}}\otimes V\subseteq{\mathrm{Res}}_{\underline{\eta}}^{\alpha}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\alpha}V.

The Mackey Theorem below for
m=n=2
follows from
[
27
, Proposition 4.5]
. The general case can be deduced from the case
m=n=2
by induction. See also
[
16
, Proposition 3.7]
or the proof of
[
29
, Proposition 2.18]
.

Theorem 3.3.28
.

(Mackey Theorem)

Let
\underline{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in Q_{+}^{n}
and
\underline{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{m})\in Q_{+}^{m}
with

\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}=\eta_{1}+\dots+\eta_{m}
, and

V\in R_{\underline{\theta}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
. Then we have the filtration

(F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}(V))_{\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\underline{\eta},\underline{\theta})}

of

{\mathrm{Res}}_{\underline{\eta}}^{\alpha}\,{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\alpha}V
. Moreover, for the sub-quotients
S_{\gamma}(V)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}(V)/F_{<\underline{\gamma}}(V)
of the filtration we have

S_{\gamma}(V)\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{1,n}\,;\,\dots\,;\,\gamma_{m,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}^{\,\eta_{1};\,\dots\,;\,\eta_{m}}{}^{\{w_{m,n}\}}\big({\mathrm{Res}}_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,1}\,;\,\dots\,;\,\gamma_{1,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}^{\,\theta_{1};\,\dots\,;\,\theta_{n}}\,V\big).

Example 3.3.29
.

Continuing with Example
3.3.25
, we can take

x(\underline{\gamma})<x(\underline{\beta})
and so, according to Theorem
3.3.28
,
{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}^{\alpha_{0}+2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\,{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}}^{\alpha_{0}+2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}V
has a submodule

F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}(V)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}1_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\psi_{x(\underline{\gamma})}1_{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}}\otimes V

isomorphic to

{\mathrm{Ind}}^{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}}\,\,{}^{w_{3,2}}\big({\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{2};\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{\alpha_{2};\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{1}}\,V\big)={\mathrm{Ind}}^{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}}\,\,{}^{w_{3,2}}V

and the quotient

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}^{\alpha_{0}+2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}\,{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\alpha_{2},\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}}^{\alpha_{0}+2\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}V\,/\,F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}(V)

\displaystyle\simeq\,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ind}}^{\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}_{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1};\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}}\,\,{}^{w_{3,2}}\big({\mathrm{Res}}_{\alpha_{2};\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1};\alpha_{1}}^{\alpha_{2};\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1};\alpha_{1}}\,V\big).

Recalling the shuffle product in
\mathbb{Z}^{\pi}((q))
from (
3.3.18
), a special case of the Mackey Theorem where each
\eta_{k}
is a simple root yields:

Corollary 3.3.30
.

Let
V_{1}\in R_{\theta_{1}}\!\operatorname{-mod},\dots,V_{n}\in R_{\theta_{n}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
have well-defined formal characters. Then
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})
has a well-defined formal character, and

\operatorname{ch}_{\pi,q}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})=(\operatorname{ch}_{\pi,q}V_{1})\circ\dots\circ(\operatorname{ch}_{\pi,q}V_{n}).

Corollary 3.3.31
.

Suppose
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
, and
V\in R_{\theta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
,
W\in R_{\eta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
be finite dimensional. Then for any
L\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(R_{\theta+\eta})
, the multiplicities of
L
in

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta,\eta}^{\theta}(V\boxtimes W)

and in

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\eta,\theta}^{\theta}(W\boxtimes V)

are the same.

Proof.

It follows from Corollary
3.3.30
that at
q=1
and
\pi=1
, the characters of
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta,\eta}^{\theta}(V\boxtimes W)

and

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\eta,\theta}^{\theta}(W\boxtimes V)

are the same. Now the result follows from the linear independence of the usual formal characters of the irreducible
R_{\theta+\eta}
modules, see for example
[
28
, Corollary 8.16]
.
∎

For
\underline{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})
as above, recalling (
2.1.23
), we denote

\displaystyle s(\underline{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=-\sum_{1\leq m<k\leq n}(\theta_{m}\mid\theta_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}\quad{\varepsilon}(\underline{\theta})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{1\leq m<k\leq n}\|\theta_{m}\|\,\|\theta_{k}\|\in\mathbb{Z}/2.

Note that
s(\underline{\theta})=s(w_{n},\underline{\theta})
and

{\varepsilon}(\underline{\theta})={\varepsilon}(w_{n},\underline{\theta})
.
The following result is essentially proved in
[
37
, Theorem 2.2]
for quiver Hecke algebras (under some unnecessary additional assumptions on finite dimensionality):

Lemma 3.3.32
.

Let
\underline{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in Q_{+}^{n}
, and

V_{k}\in R_{\theta_{k}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
for
k=1,\dots,n
.
Then there is a natural isomorphism

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}}^{\theta}(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})\simeq{\mathpzc{Q}}^{s(\underline{\theta})}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}(\underline{\theta})}\,{\mathrm{Coind}}_{\theta_{n},\dots,\theta_{1}}^{\theta}(V_{n}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{1}).

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case
n=2
, i.e. prove that

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta,\eta}^{\theta+\eta}(V\boxtimes W)\simeq{\mathpzc{Q}}^{(\theta\mid\eta)}\Uppi^{\|\theta\|\,\|\eta\|}\,{\mathrm{Coind}}_{\eta,\theta}^{\theta+\eta}(W\boxtimes V).

To prove this, we check the assumptions of Lemma
2.2.11
(and Example
2.2.14
).
Let
m={\operatorname{ht}}(\theta)
and
n={\operatorname{ht}}(\eta)
.
For the assumption (1), the right
R_{\theta,\eta}
-module
R_{\theta+\eta}1_{\theta,\eta}
has basis
\{\psi_{x}\mid x\in{\mathscr{D}}_{m+n}^{(m,n)}\}
and
the left
R_{\eta,\theta}
-module
1_{\eta,\theta}R_{\theta+\eta}
has basis
\{\psi_{y}\mid y\in{}^{(n,m)}{\mathscr{D}}_{m+n}\}
. For the assumption (2), see Example
2.2.14
. For the assumption (3), the Mackey Theorem yields a filtration of
1_{\eta,\theta}R_{\theta+\eta}1_{\theta,\eta}={\mathrm{Res}}_{\eta,\theta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta,\eta}R_{\theta,\eta}
by bimodules with the top quotient

1_{\eta,\theta}R_{\theta+\eta}1_{\theta,\eta}/Y
such that
R_{\theta,\eta}\to 1_{\eta,\theta}R_{\theta+\eta}1_{\theta,\eta}/Y,\ r\mapsto r\psi_{x_{0}}
is an isomorphism of left modules, where
x_{0}
is the longest element of
{}^{(n,m)}{\mathscr{D}}_{m+n}^{(m,n)}
given by

1
n+1
m
n+m
m+1
1
m+n
n

For the remaining part of the condition (3), taking
\xi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\eta,\theta}\psi_{x_{0}}1_{\theta,\eta}
, one needs to check that for
r\otimes s\in R_{\theta}\otimes R_{\eta}=R_{\theta,\eta}
we have

\xi(r\otimes s)\equiv(-1)^{|s||r|+|s||\xi|+|r||\xi|}(s\otimes r)\xi\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }Y)\,,

which is checked using Lemma
3.3.14
, cf. the proof of
[
37
, Theorem 2.2]
. Finally to check the condition (4) of Lemma
2.2.11
, we need to observe that

{}^{(n,m)}{\mathscr{D}}_{m+n}=\{x_{0}x^{-1}\mid{\mathscr{D}}_{m+n}^{(m,n)}\},

and use Lemma
3.3.14
to see that

1_{\eta,\theta}\psi_{y}\psi_{x}1_{\theta,\eta}\equiv\delta_{x_{0}x^{-1},y}1_{\eta,\theta}\psi_{x_{0}}1_{\theta,\eta}\text{ }(\text{\rm mod }Y)\,,

completing the proof.
∎

The anti-automorphism
\tau
from (
3.3.15
) restricts to an anti-automorphism of the parabolic subalgebra
R_{\underline{\theta}}
. So, just like for
R_{\theta}
, for a graded
R_{\underline{\theta}}
-supermodule, we can define its
graded dual

V^{\circledast}
. By definition, for the graded supermodules
V_{1}\in R_{\theta_{1}}\!\operatorname{-mod},\dots,V_{n}\in R_{\theta_{n}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we have

(3.3.33)

(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})^{\circledast}\simeq V_{1}^{\circledast}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n}^{\circledast}.

Lemma 3.3.34
.

Let
\underline{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\in Q_{+}^{n}
.

(i)

The functor
{\mathrm{Res}}^{\theta}_{\underline{\theta}}
commutes with duality
\circledast
.

(ii)

If
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
, and
V_{k}\in R_{\theta_{k}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
for
k=1,\dots,n
are finite dimensional, then

\big({\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}}^{\theta}(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})\big)^{\circledast}\simeq{\mathpzc{Q}}^{s(\underline{\theta})}\Uppi^{{\varepsilon}(\underline{\theta})}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\theta_{n},\dots,\theta_{1}}(V_{n}^{\circledast}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{1}^{\circledast}).

Proof.

(i) is clear.

(ii) follows from (i) and Lemma
3.3.32
by uniqueness of adjoint functors as in
[
30
, Theorem 3.7.5]

∎

3.3d.
Divided power idempotents

Let
i\in I
and
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. Recall that we denote by
w_{m}
the longest element of
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
. If
i\neq 0
, the algebra
R_{m\alpha_{i}}
is known to be the (affine)
nil-Hecke algebra

and has an idempotent

(3.3.35)

1_{i^{(m)}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\psi_{w_{m}}\prod_{s=1}^{m}y_{s}^{s-1},

cf.
[
29
]
. If
i=0
, the algebra
R_{m\alpha_{0}}
is known to be the (affine)
odd nil-Hecke algebra
and has an idempotent

1_{0^{(m)}}
of the form
\psi_{w_{m}}\prod_{s=1}^{m}y_{s}^{s-1}
, cf.
[
15
]
(the choice of a reduced decomposition for
w_{m}
is important here to get the right sign).
Diagrammatically we will denote

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The fact that
1_{i^{(m)}}
is an idempotent is equivalent to

(3.3.37)

1_{i^{m}}\psi_{w_{m}}=\psi_{w_{m}}.

Lemma 3.3.38
.

For any
i\in I
and
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, in
R_{m\alpha_{i}}
we have

1_{i^{(m)}}R_{m\alpha_{i}}=\psi_{w_{m}}R_{m\alpha_{i}}=\psi_{w_{m}}\Bbbk[y_{1},\dots,y_{m}].

Proof.

The first equality follows from (
3.3.35
) and (
3.3.37
). The second equality follows from Theorem
3.3.13
since in
R_{m\alpha_{i}}
we have
\psi_{w_{m}}\psi_{y}=0
for any non-trivial
y\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
.
∎

Recalling the notation (
2.2.5
) and (
2.1.3
), we have the following well-known fact (see for example
[
29
, p. 330]
for
i\neq 0
and
[
7
, Lemma 5.8]
for
i=0
):

Lemma 3.3.39
.

Let
i\in I
. Then, as graded left supermodules,

R_{m\alpha_{i}}\simeq(R_{m\alpha_{i}}1_{i^{(m)}})^{\bigoplus q_{i}^{-m(m-1)/2}[m]^{!}_{i}}\simeq(1_{i^{(m)}}R_{m\alpha_{i}})^{\bigoplus q_{i}^{m(m-1)/2}\overline{[m]^{!}_{i}}}.

Let
\theta\in Q_{+}
. Recalling the notation
I^{\theta}_{{\operatorname{div}}}
from §
2.1d
, for a divided power word
\text{\boldmath$i$}=(i_{1}^{(m_{1})},\ldots,i_{r}^{(m_{r})})\in I^{\theta}_{{\operatorname{div}}}
, we have the corresponding
divided power idempotent

(3.3.40)

1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\iota_{m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}}(1_{i_{1}^{(m_{1})}}\otimes\dots\otimes 1_{i_{r}^{(m_{r})}})\in R_{\theta}.

Lemma 3.3.41
.

Let
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\in I^{\theta_{1}}_{\operatorname{div}},\dots,\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}\in I^{\theta_{k}}_{\operatorname{div}}
and
\theta=\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{k}
. Then there is an isomorphism of graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodules

\displaystyle R_{\theta}

\displaystyle 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}(R_{\theta_{1}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes R_{\theta_{k}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}}),

\displaystyle 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}}\mapsto 1_{\underline{\theta}}\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}}.

Proof.

The homomorphism of graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodules as in the statement exists because in
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}(R_{\theta_{1}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes R_{\theta_{k}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}})
we have

1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}}\cdot(1_{\underline{\theta}}\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}})=1_{\underline{\theta}}\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}}.

The inverse homomorphism is constructed using the adjointness of
{\mathrm{Ind}}
and
{\mathrm{Res}}
.
∎

Let
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}^{(m_{1})}\cdots i_{r}^{(m_{r})}\in I^{\theta}_{\operatorname{div}}
.
Recalling the notation (
2.1.25
) and (
2.1.6
), note that
1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}=1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}=1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
,
and define

(3.3.42)

\text{\boldmath$i$}!\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=[m_{1}]_{i_{1}}^{!}\cdots[m_{r}]_{i_{r}}^{!}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\langle\text{\boldmath$i$}\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}(\alpha_{i_{k}}\mid\alpha_{i_{k}})m_{k}(m_{k}-1)/4.

Lemma 3.3.43
.

Let
\text{\boldmath$i$}=i_{1}^{(m_{1})}\cdots i_{r}^{(m_{r})}\in I^{\theta}_{{\operatorname{div}}}
, and
U
(resp.
V
) be a left (resp. right) graded
R_{\theta}
-supermodule.
Then

1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}U\simeq(1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}U)^{\oplus q^{\langle\text{\boldmath$i$}\rangle}\text{\boldmath$i$}!}\quad\text{and}\quad V1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}\simeq(V1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}})^{\oplus q^{-\langle\text{\boldmath$i$}\rangle}\overline{\text{\boldmath$i$}!}}

In particular, if
1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}U
is a free
\Bbbk
-module of rank
(m_{1})!\cdots(m_{r})!
, then
1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}U
is the lowest degree component of
1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}U
and
1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}U
is a free
\Bbbk
-module of rank
1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.3.41
and adjointness of induction and restriction,

\displaystyle 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}U

\displaystyle\simeq\,\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{\theta}}(R_{\theta}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}},U)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}}}(R_{m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}}}1_{i_{1}^{(m_{1})}}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes R_{m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}}1_{i_{r}^{(m_{r})}},{\mathrm{Res}}^{\theta}_{m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}}U)

and

\displaystyle 1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}U

\displaystyle\simeq\,\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{\theta}}(R_{\theta}1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}},U)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{R_{m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}}}(R_{m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}}}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes R_{m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}},{\mathrm{Res}}^{\theta}_{m_{1}\alpha_{i_{1}},\dots,m_{r}\alpha_{i_{r}}}U)

So the isomorphism
1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$i$}}}U\simeq(1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}U)^{\oplus q^{\langle\text{\boldmath$i$}\rangle}\text{\boldmath$i$}!}
follows from Lemma
3.3.39
.
The second isomorphism is proved similarly. The final statement follows easily from the first isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 3.3.44
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
\eta\in Q_{+}
with
{\operatorname{ht}}(\eta)=m
,
i\in I
,
\text{\boldmath$j$}\in I^{\eta}
, and
\theta=\eta+n\alpha_{i}
.

(i)

Let
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
be the permutation mapping
k\mapsto k+m
for
k=1,\dots,n
and
n+l\mapsto l
for
l=1,\dots,m
. Then in
R_{\theta}
we have
\psi_{x}1_{i^{(n)}\text{\boldmath$j$}}=1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{(n)}}\psi_{x}1_{i^{(n)}\text{\boldmath$j$}}
(for any choice of a reduced decomposition for
x
used to define
\psi_{x}
).

(ii)

Let
y\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
be the permutation mapping
k\mapsto k+n
for
k=1,\dots,m
and
m+l\mapsto l
for
l=1,\dots,n
. Then in
R_{\theta}
we have
\psi_{y}1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{(n)}}=1_{i^{(n)}\text{\boldmath$j$}}\psi_{y}1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{(n)}}
(for any choice of a reduced decomposition for
y
used to define
\psi_{y}
).

Proof.

We prove (i), the proof of (ii) being similar. Recall that
1_{i^{(n)}}=\psi_{w_{n}}f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
, where
f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=\prod_{s=1}^{n}y_{s}^{s-1}
. We consider
w_{n}
as an element of
S_{m+n}
via the natural embedding
S_{n}\to S_{n}\times S_{m}\leq S_{m+n}
. When we use the embedding
S_{n}\to S_{m}\times S_{n}\leq S_{m+n}
instead, we denote the image of
w_{n}
by
w_{n}^{\prime}
(it permutes the last
n
letters).

Now, we have, using the relations in
R_{\theta}
,

\displaystyle\psi_{x}1_{i^{(n)}\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle=1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{n}}\psi_{x}1_{i^{(n)}\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle=1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{n}}\psi_{x}\psi_{w_{n}}f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})1_{i^{n}\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle=1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{n}}\psi_{w_{n}^{\prime}}\psi_{x}f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})1_{i^{n}\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle=1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{(n)}}\psi_{w_{n}^{\prime}}\psi_{x}f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})1_{i^{n}\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle=1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{(n)}}\psi_{x}\psi_{w_{n}}f(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})1_{i^{n}\text{\boldmath$j$}}

\displaystyle=1_{\text{\boldmath$j$}i^{(n)}}\psi_{x}1_{i^{(n)}\text{\boldmath$j$}},

where we have used (
3.3.37
) for the fourth equality.
∎

Chapter 4
Cuspidal algebras

4.1.
Cuspidal modules and cuspidal algebras

4.1a.
Cuspidal systems

We describe a classification of the graded irreducible
R_{\theta}
-supermodules in terms of cuspidal systems. Recall the terminology of §
2.1d
.
For now, fix an
arbitrary
convex preorder
\preceq
on
\Phi_{+}
.

Let
\beta\in\Psi
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
V\in R_{m\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
. Then
V
is called
cuspidal
if
{\mathrm{Res}}_{\theta,\eta}V\neq 0
for
\theta,\eta\in Q_{+}
implies that
\theta
is a sum of positive roots
\preceq\beta
and
\eta
is a sum of positive roots
\succeq\beta
(this and other definitions below depend on the choice of
\preceq
).

A word
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{m\beta}
is called
cuspidal
if
\text{\boldmath$i$}=\text{\boldmath$j$}\text{\boldmath$k$}
for words
\text{\boldmath$j$},\text{\boldmath$k$}
implies that
{\operatorname{wt}}(\text{\boldmath$j$})
is a sum of positive roots
\preceq\beta
and
{\operatorname{wt}}(\text{\boldmath$k$})
is a sum of positive roots
\succeq\beta
.
We denote by

I^{m\beta}_{\operatorname{cus}}
the set of all cuspidal words in
I^{m\beta}
.

Let
(1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mid\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{m\beta}\setminus I^{m\beta}_{\operatorname{cus}})
be the two-sided ideal of
R_{m\beta}
generated by all
1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
with
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{m\beta}\setminus I^{m\beta}_{\operatorname{cus}}
.
Then
V\in R_{m\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
is cuspidal if and only if every word of
V
is cuspidal if and only if
1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}V=0
for all
\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{m\beta}\setminus I^{m\beta}_{\operatorname{cus}}
if and only if
V

factors through a module over the
cuspidal algebra

(4.1.1)

\bar{R}_{m\beta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=R_{m\beta}/(1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}\mid\text{\boldmath$i$}\in I^{m\beta}\setminus I^{m\beta}_{\operatorname{cus}}).

Thus
\bar{R}_{m\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
can be identified with the full subcategory of
R_{m\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
which consists of the (finitely generated) cuspidal (graded super)modules.

For an element
u\in R_{m\beta}
we denote its image under the natural projection
R_{m\beta}{\twoheadrightarrow}\bar{R}_{m\beta}
again by
u
. So we have elements
1_{m\beta},\,y_{1}\dots,y_{mh},\,\psi_{1}\dots,\psi_{mh-1}
, etc. in
\bar{R}_{m\beta}
where
h={\operatorname{ht}}(\beta)
.

A
cuspidal system
(for the fixed convex preorder
\preceq
) is the following data:

(Cus1)

An irreducible cuspidal
L_{\beta}\in R_{\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
for every
\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}
;

(Cus2)

An irreducible cuspidal
L_{\text{\boldmath$\mu$}}\in R_{n\delta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and every multipartition
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(n)
, such that
L_{\text{\boldmath$\mu$}}\not\cong L_{\text{\boldmath$\nu$}}
if
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\neq\text{\boldmath$\nu$}
.

We call the (graded super)modules
L_{\beta}
from (Cus1)
real irreducible cuspidal modules
, and
the modules
L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
from (Cus2)
imaginary irreducible cuspidal modules
.

Let
({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\in{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)
for some
\theta\in Q+
. As almost all
m_{\beta}
are zero, we can choose a finite subset

\beta_{1}\succ\dots\succ\beta_{s}\succ\delta\succ\beta_{-t}\succ\dots\succ\beta_{-1}

of
\Psi
such that
m_{\beta}=0
for
\beta
’s outside of this subset. Then, denoting
m_{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=m_{\beta_{u}}
, we can write
({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
in the form

(4.1.2)

({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})=(\beta_{1}^{m_{1}},\dots,\beta_{s}^{m_{s}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$},\beta_{-t}^{m_{-t}},\dots,\beta_{-1}^{m_{-1}}).

Denote also

\langle{\underline{m}}\rangle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(m_{1}\beta_{1},\dots,m_{s}\beta_{s},m_{\delta}\delta,m_{-t}\beta_{-t},\dots,m_{-1}\beta_{-1})\in Q_{+}^{s+t+1},

so we have a parabolic subalgebra
R_{\langle{\underline{m}}\rangle}\subseteq 1_{\langle{\underline{m}}\rangle}R_{\theta}1_{\langle{\underline{m}}\rangle}
, cf. §
3.3c
.

We define a partial order on
{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)
.
First, there are left and write lexicographic orders
\leq_{l}
and
\leq_{r}
on the finitary tuples
{\underline{m}}=(m_{\beta})_{\beta\in\Psi}
, and for two such tuples
{\underline{m}}
and
{\underline{n}}
we set

{\underline{m}}\leq{\underline{n}}
if
{\underline{m}}\leq_{l}{\underline{n}}
and
{\underline{m}}\geq_{r}{\underline{n}}
.
As in
[
38
,
39
]
, for
({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$}),({\underline{n}},\text{\boldmath$\nu$})\in{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)
, we now set

(4.1.3)

({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\leq({\underline{n}},\text{\boldmath$\nu$})\ \iff\ {\underline{m}}<{\underline{n}},\ \text{or ${\underline{m}}={\underline{n}}$ and}\ \text{\boldmath$\mu$}=\text{\boldmath$\nu$}.

In this subsection, it will be convenient to use the following notation: for graded supermodules
V_{1}\in R_{\theta_{1}}\!\operatorname{-mod},\,\dots,\,V_{n}\in R_{\theta_{n}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we denote

V_{1}\circ\dots\circ V_{n}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={\mathrm{Ind}}_{\underline{\theta}}^{\theta}(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n}).

Now suppose that
\{L_{\beta},L_{\text{\boldmath$\mu$}}\mid\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}\}
is a cuspidal system for our fixed convex preorder. For a root partition

({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\in\Uppi(\theta)

written in the form (
4.1.2
), we define
the
(proper) standard (graded super)module
over
R_{\theta}
:

(4.1.4)

\operatorname{Stand}({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=L_{\beta_{1}}^{\circ m_{1}}\circ\dots\circ L_{\beta_{s}}^{\circ m_{s}}\circ L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\circ L_{\beta_{-t}}^{\circ m_{-t}}\circ\dots\circ L_{\beta_{-1}}^{\circ m_{-1}}.

Theorem 4.1.5
.

[
33
, Theorem 3.3.13]

Let
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
.
For a given convex preorder there exists a cuspidal system
\{L_{\beta},L_{\text{\boldmath$\mu$}}\mid\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}},\,\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}\}
. Moreover:

(i)

For every root partition
({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
, the standard module

\operatorname{Stand}({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
has an irreducible head; denote this irreducible module
L({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
.

(ii)

\{L({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mid({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\in{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)\}={\mathrm{Irr}}(R_{\theta})
.

(iii)

[\operatorname{Stand}({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})]=1
, and
[\operatorname{Stand}({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}L({\underline{n}},\text{\boldmath$\nu$})]\neq 0
implies
({\underline{n}},\text{\boldmath$\nu$})\leq({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
.

(iv)

{\mathrm{Res}}_{\langle{\underline{n}}\rangle}L({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\neq 0
implies
{\underline{n}}\leq{\underline{m}}
and

{\mathrm{Res}}_{\langle{\underline{m}}\rangle}L({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\cong L_{\beta_{1}}^{\circ m_{1}}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes L_{\beta_{s}}^{\circ m_{s}}\boxtimes L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\boxtimes L_{\beta_{-t}}^{\circ m_{-t}}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes L_{\beta_{-1}}^{\circ m_{-1}}.

(v)

Let
\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. Then
\operatorname{Stand}(\beta^{n})=L_{\beta}^{\circ n}
is irreducible, i.e.
L(\beta^{n})=L_{\beta}^{\circ n}
.

(vi)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. Then
\{L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mid\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(n)\}={\mathrm{Irr}}(\bar{R}_{n\delta})
and
\{L(\beta^{n})\}={\mathrm{Irr}}(\bar{R}_{n\beta})
for any
\beta\in\Phi_{+}^{\mathrm{re}}
.

Let
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
. It is clear from Theorem
4.1.5
that for a real root
\beta
, the irreducible real cuspidal (graded super)module
L_{\beta}
is
the only irreducible
R_{\beta}
-module that is cuspidal, and so the choice of
L_{\beta}
is canonical. On the other hand, the irreducible imaginary cuspidal
R_{n\delta}
-modules
\{L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})\mid\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(n)\}
are determined up to permutation of the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(n)
of their labels, and so in this sense the definition of
L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
(and of
\operatorname{Stand}({\underline{m}},\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
) is
not canonical
. For now
L(\text{\boldmath$\mu$})
is just one of the irreducible imaginary cuspidal modules over
R_{n\delta}
. One of the goals of this paper is to label the irreducible imaginary cuspidal modules over each
R_{n\delta}
canonically by the multipartitions
\text{\boldmath$\mu$}\in{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(n)
.

4.1b.
Induction and restriction for cuspidal modules

We continue to work with a fixed arbitrary convex preorder
\preceq
.
Let
\beta\in\Psi
,
d,n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\Uplambda(n,d)
. Denote

\lambda\beta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\lambda_{1}\beta,\dots,\lambda_{n}\beta)\in Q_{+}^{n}.

Recalling (
3.3.21
), we have the idempotent
1_{\lambda\beta}\in R_{d\beta}
and the parabolic subalgebra

R_{\lambda\beta}\subseteq 1_{\lambda\beta}R_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}
identified with
R_{\lambda_{1}\beta}\otimes\dots\otimes R_{\lambda_{n}\beta}
via the embedding
\iota_{\lambda\beta}
of (
3.3.22
). So we can consider
\bar{R}_{\lambda_{1}\beta}\otimes\dots\otimes\bar{R}_{\lambda_{n}\beta}
as a quotient of
R_{\lambda\beta}
.
Define the
cuspidal parabolic subalgebra

\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}\subseteq 1_{\lambda\beta}\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}
to be the image of
R_{\lambda\beta}
under the natural projection
1_{\lambda\beta}R_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}{\twoheadrightarrow}1_{\lambda\beta}\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}
.

Lemma 4.1.6
.

[
33
, Lemma 3.3.21]

Let
h={\operatorname{ht}}(\beta)
and
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
.

(i)

The natural map

R_{\lambda_{1}\beta}\otimes\dots\otimes R_{\lambda_{n}\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{\lambda\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}1_{\lambda\beta}R_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}{\twoheadrightarrow}1_{\lambda\beta}\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}

factors through
\bar{R}_{\lambda_{1}\beta}\otimes\dots\otimes\bar{R}_{\lambda_{n}\beta}
and induces an isomorphism

\bar{R}_{\lambda_{1}\beta}\otimes\dots\otimes\bar{R}_{\lambda_{n}\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}.

(ii)

\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}
is a free right
\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}
-module with basis

\{\psi_{w}1_{\lambda\beta}\mid w\in{\mathscr{D}}_{hd}^{h\lambda}\}
.

(iii)

1_{\lambda\beta}\bar{R}_{d\beta}
is a free left
\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}
-module with basis

\{1_{\lambda\beta}\psi_{w}\mid w\in{}^{h\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{hd}\}
.

In view of the lemma we identify

(4.1.7)

\bar{R}_{\lambda_{1}\beta}\otimes\dots\otimes\bar{R}_{\lambda_{n}\beta}=\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}.

We call a
R_{\lambda\beta}
-module cuspidal if it factors through
\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}
. (This agrees with the definition of a cuspidal
R_{d\delta}
-modules for the case
\lambda=(d)
.) The following two lemmas show, among other things, that cuspidality is preserved under parabolic induction and restriction.

Lemma 4.1.8
.

[
33
, Lemma 3.3.18]

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
.
If
V\in R_{d\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
is cuspidal then so is

{\mathrm{Res}}^{d\beta}_{\lambda\beta}V\in R_{\lambda\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
.

Lemma 4.1.9
.

[
33
, Lemmas 3.3.18, 3.3.20, Corollary 3.3.22]

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
.

(i)

If
V\in R_{\lambda\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
is cuspidal then so is the
R_{d\beta}
-module
{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\beta}^{d\beta}V
and there is a natural bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
isomorphism of graded
R_{d\beta}
-supermodules

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\beta}^{d\beta}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes_{\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}}V,\ 1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes v\mapsto 1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes v.

(ii)

If
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(r)}\in I^{\lambda_{r}\beta}_{\operatorname{cus}}
for
r=1,\dots,n
, then there is a bidegree
(0,{\bar{0}})
isomorphism of graded
R_{d\beta}
-supermodules

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\beta}^{d\beta}\big(\bar{R}_{\lambda_{1}\beta}1_{{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes\bar{R}_{\lambda_{n}\beta}1_{{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}}\big),

\displaystyle 1_{{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto 1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes 1_{{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}}\otimes\dots\otimes 1_{{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}}.

From now on, for cuspidal
V\in R_{\lambda\beta}\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we identify the induced (graded super)modules

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\beta}^{d\beta}V=R_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes_{R_{\lambda\beta}}V
and
\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes_{\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}}V

as in Lemma
4.1.9
(i):

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\beta}^{d\beta}V=R_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes_{R_{\lambda\beta}}V=\bar{R}_{d\beta}1_{\lambda\beta}\otimes_{\bar{R}_{\lambda\beta}}V.

4.1c.
Imaginary cuspidal algebra

Fix
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
.
From now on, for the case
\beta=\delta
, we redenote the cuspidal algebra
\bar{R}_{d\beta}
of (
4.1.1
) by
\hat{C}_{d}
:

(4.1.10)

\hat{C}_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bar{R}_{d\delta}.

and refer to it as the
imaginary cuspidal (graded super)algebra
.
Similarly, for
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
, we denote

(4.1.11)

\hat{C}_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\bar{R}_{\lambda\delta},

so in view of (
4.1.7
), we have
\hat{C}_{\lambda}\cong\hat{C}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\hat{C}_{\lambda_{n}}
. According to (
4.1.7
), we always identify:

(4.1.12)

\hat{C}_{\lambda}=\hat{C}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\hat{C}_{\lambda_{n}}

Lemma 4.1.13
.

Let
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
. We have
|{\mathrm{Irr}}(\hat{C}_{d})|=|{\mathcal{P}}^{J}(d)|
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
4.1.5
(vi).
∎

4.1d.
Imaginary Mackey Theorem

Let

(4.1.14)

\rho_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathfrak{S}_{d}\to\mathfrak{S}_{dp},\ s_{r}\mapsto t_{r}

be the group embedding
where
t_{r}
is defined as the product of transpositions

t_{r}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\prod_{k=rp-p+1}^{rp}(k,k+p)\qquad(1\leq r<n).

In other words, for
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{d}
,
\rho_{d}(w)
permutes
d
blocks of size
p
according to
w
.

Recall that the element
\psi_{\rho_{d}(w)}
in general depends on the choice of a reduced decomposition for
\rho_{d}(w)
. We will always use a reduced decomposition consistent with a reduced decomposition for
w
, i.e. for a reduced decomposition
w=s_{r_{1}}\dots s_{r_{k}}
, we take
\psi_{\rho_{d}(w)}=\psi_{t_{r_{1}}}\dots\psi_{t_{r_{k}}}
(now
\psi_{\rho_{d}(w)}
only depends on the choice of a reduced decomposition of
w
).

Recall the notation from Section
2.1c
. In particular, given two compositions
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
and
x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
we have compositions
\lambda\cap x\mu
and
x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu
in
\Uplambda(d)
. Moreover, the corresponding parabolic subalgebras
\hat{C}_{\lambda\cap x\mu}
and
\hat{C}_{x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu}
are naturally isomorphic via an isomorphism

\hat{C}_{\lambda\cap x\mu}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\hat{C}_{x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu}

which permutes the components. Composing with this isomorphism we get a functor

(4.1.15)

\hat{C}_{x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to\hat{C}_{\lambda\cap x\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ M\mapsto{}^{x}M.

Note that this functor is a special case of the functor (
3.3.24
)
i.e.
{}^{x}M={}^{\{h(x)\}}M
for a permutation
h(x)
of the parts of
x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu
which corresponds to the conjugation by
x
.
Moreover, we do not need any grading and parity shifts here since
(\delta,\delta)=0
.

Let
V\in\hat{C}_{\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}
. Note that
for any
x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
, we have

1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}1_{\mu\delta}\otimes V\subseteq{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\mu\delta}^{d\delta}V.

Let
\leq
be a total order refining the Bruhat order on
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
.
For
x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
, we consider the submodules

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{\leq x}(V)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{y\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}\,\text{with}\,y\leq x}R_{\lambda\delta}1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(y)}1_{\mu\delta}\otimes V\subseteq{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\mu\delta}^{d\delta}V,

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{<x}(V)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{y\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}\,\text{with}\,y<x}R_{\lambda\delta}1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(y)}1_{\mu\delta}\otimes V\subseteq{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\mu\delta}^{d\delta}V.

Theorem 4.1.16
.

(Imaginary Mackey Theorem)

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
, and
V\in\hat{C}_{\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}
. Then we have the filtration

(\bar{F}_{\leq x}(V))_{x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}}

of

{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\mu\delta}^{d\delta}V
.
Moreover, the sub-quotients of the filtration are

\bar{F}_{\leq x}(V)/\bar{F}_{<x}(V)\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{(\lambda\cap x\mu)\delta}^{\lambda\delta}\,{}^{x}({\mathrm{Res}}^{\mu\delta}_{(x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu)\delta}V).

Proof.

This follows from the Mackey Theorem
3.3.28
and the definition of cuspidal modules, cf.
[
34
, Theorem 4.5.4]
.
Indeed, by Theorem
3.3.28
, we have the explicit filtration

(F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}(V))_{\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\lambda\delta,\mu\delta)}

of

{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\mu\delta}^{d\delta}V
with
F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}
given by
(
3.3.26
) and the sub-quotients

S_{\gamma}(V)\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{1,n}\,;\,\dots\,;\,\gamma_{m,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}^{\,\lambda_{1}\delta;\,\dots\,;\,\lambda_{m}\delta}{}^{\{w_{m,n}\}}\big({\mathrm{Res}}_{\gamma_{1,1},\dots,\gamma_{m,1}\,;\,\dots\,;\,\gamma_{1,n},\dots,\gamma_{m,n}}^{\,\mu_{1}\delta;\,\dots\,;\,\mu_{n}\delta}\,V\big),

where
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m})
and
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})
.

Suppose
S_{\gamma}(V)\neq 0
.
By the definition of cuspidal modules, we have for
b=1,\dots,n
that
\gamma_{1,b}
is a sum of positive roots
\preceq\delta
. Since
\lambda_{1}\delta=\sum_{b=1}^{n}\gamma_{1,b}
, it follows from the definition of a convex preorder that all
\gamma_{1,b}
must be multiples of
\delta
. Now, by Lemma
4.1.8
, the module

{\mathrm{Res}}_{\gamma_{1,1},\gamma_{2,1}+\dots+\gamma_{m,1}\,;\,\dots\,;\,\gamma_{1,n},\gamma_{2,n}+\dots+\gamma_{m,n}}^{\,\mu_{1}\delta;\,\dots\,;\,\mu_{n}\delta}\,V

is cuspidal, so for
b=1,\dots,n
we have that
\gamma_{2,b}
is a sum of positive roots
\preceq\delta
. Since
\lambda_{2}\delta=\sum_{b=1}^{n}\gamma_{2,b}
, it follows from the definition of a convex preorder that all
\gamma_{2,b}
must be multiples of
\delta
.
Continuing this way we deduce that all
\gamma_{a,b}
are multiples of
\delta
. Thus,
S_{\gamma}(V)\neq 0
only if

\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\lambda\delta,\mu\delta)_{\operatorname{cus}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\underline{\gamma}\in{\mathscr{D}}(\lambda\delta,\mu\delta)\mid\text{all $\gamma_{a,b}$ are multiples of $\delta$}\}.

We now get a bijection

\zeta\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\mathscr{D}}(\lambda\delta,\mu\delta)_{\operatorname{cus}}\to{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}

with
\rho_{d}(\zeta(\underline{\gamma}))=x(\underline{\gamma})
, so

F_{\leq\underline{\gamma}}(V)=\bar{F}_{\leq\zeta(\underline{\gamma})}(V)
, and the result follows.
∎

Example 4.1.17
.

Let
d=3
,
\lambda=(1,2)
and
\mu=(2,1)
.
Then

{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}^{\mu}_{3}=\{1,s_{1}s_{2}\},

and
\lambda\cap\mu=(1,1,1)
,
\lambda\cap s_{1}s_{2}\mu=\lambda
,
s_{2}s_{1}\lambda\cap\mu=\mu
.
Now, according to Theorem
4.1.16
,
{\mathrm{Res}}_{\delta,2\delta}^{3\delta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{2\delta,\delta}^{3\delta}V
has a submodule

\bar{F}_{\leq 1}=R_{\delta,2\delta}1_{\delta,2\delta}1_{2\delta,\delta}\otimes V\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\delta;\delta,\delta}^{\delta;2\delta}{\mathrm{Res}}_{\delta,\delta;\delta}^{2\delta;\delta}V

and

{\mathrm{Res}}_{\delta,2\delta}^{3\delta}{\mathrm{Ind}}_{2\delta,\delta}^{3\delta}V/F_{\leq 1}\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\delta,2\delta}^{\delta,2\delta}\,{}^{s_{1}s_{2}}({\mathrm{Res}}^{2\delta,\delta}_{2\delta,\delta}V)={}^{s_{1}s_{2}}V.

4.2.
Gelfand-Graev truncation of imaginary cuspidal algebra

From now on, we fix a convex preorder
\preceq
on
\Phi_{+}
as in Example
2.1.33
, in particular (
2.1.34
) holds. When we speak of cuspidality from now on, we mean cuspidality with respect to this specially chosen
\preceq
.

Recall from (
4.1.1
) and (
4.1.10
) that for
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, the imaginary cuspidal algebra
\hat{C}_{d}
is an explicit quotient of
R_{d\delta}
. The algebra
R_{d\delta}
is generated by the elements
\psi_{r},y_{s},1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}}
, and we use the same notation for the corresponding elements of
\hat{C}_{d}
.

4.2a.
Gelfand-Graev words

For
j\in J
, in
[
33
, §4.2a]
, we have defined (divided power) words

\begin{split}\text{\boldmath$l$}^{j}&\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ell\,(\ell-1)^{(2)}\,\cdots\,(j+1)^{(2)}\,j\,\cdots\,1\,0^{(2)}\,1\,\cdots\,j\,\in\,I^{\delta}_{\operatorname{div}}.\end{split}

For
j\in J
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we will consider more general divided power words in
I^{m\delta}_{\operatorname{div}}
:

(4.2.1)

\begin{split}\text{\boldmath$l$}^{m,j}&\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\ell^{(m)}(\ell-1)^{(2m)}\,\cdots\,(j+1)^{(2m)}j^{(m)}\cdots\,1^{(m)}0^{(2m)}1^{(m)}\cdots\,j^{(m)}.\end{split}

Using the notation from (
2.1.25
), we also have the words

\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{m,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\widehat{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{m,j}}=\ell^{m}\,(\ell-1)^{2m}\,\cdots\,(j+1)^{2m}\,j^{m}\,\cdots\,1^{m}\,0^{2m}\,1^{m}\,\cdots\,j^{m}\,\in\,I^{m\delta}.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
recall the colored compositions
\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
from §
2.1b
.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\in I^{d_{1}\delta},\dots,\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}\in I^{d_{k}\delta}
for
d_{1},\dots,d_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. If
\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{m,j}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}
then
k=1
.

We refer to the words
\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{m,j}
as
indecomposable Gelfand-Graev words
and to the divided power words
\text{\boldmath$l$}^{m,j}
as
indecomposable divided power Gelfand-Graev words
. From the definition:

Given
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
, we define more general
Gelfand-Graev words

(4.2.3)

\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu_{n},j_{n}}\in I^{d\delta}_{\operatorname{div}}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\widehat{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}}=\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu_{n},j_{n}}\in I^{d\delta}.

Recalling the set
\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)
of colored
essential
compositions from §
2.1b
, note that the words
\{\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}
are distinct and exhaust all non-divided power Gelfand-Graev words in
I^{d\delta}
. Similalrly, the divided power words
\{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}
are distinct and exhaust all divided power Gelfand-Graev words in
I^{d\delta}
. We introduce the sets of
rank
d
Gelfand-Graev words
:

{\mathsf{GGW}}^{(d)}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathsf{GGW}}^{(d)}_{\operatorname{div}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}

It is clear from the definitions that
{\mathsf{GGW}}^{(d)}\subseteq I^{d\delta}_{\operatorname{cus}}
. Moreover, from the definitions:

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Let
\mu\in\Uplambda(n,d)
and
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}\in I^{\mu_{k}\delta}
for all
k=1,\dots,n
. Then

\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(k)}\in{\mathsf{GGW}}^{(\mu_{k})}
for all
k=1,\dots,n
if and only if
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}\in{\mathsf{GGW}}^{(d)}
.

Recall the notation (
4.1.14
) and (
2.1.18
).

Lemma 4.2.5
.

Let
(\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
,
\lambda\in\Uplambda_{0}(m,d)
and
w\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\nu}
. If
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(r)}\in I^{\lambda_{r}\delta}
for
r=1,\dots,m
. Suppose that

\rho_{d}(w)\cdot\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(m)}
. Then
m=n
,
w=w_{\nu,\sigma}
and
\lambda=\sigma\mu
for some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and

\rho_{d}(w)\cdot\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}=\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\nu_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},j_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}}\cdots\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\nu_{\sigma^{-1}(n)},j_{\sigma^{-1}(n)}},

in particular,
\rho_{d}(w)\cdot\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\in{\mathsf{GGW}}^{(d)}
.

Proof.

We may assume that all parts of
\nu
and all parts of
\lambda
are non-zero.
We use the notation of §
2.1c
. In particular,
w=w_{A}
for some
A\in M(\lambda,\nu)
and
w_{A}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{r,s}\nearrow{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{r,s}
for all
1\leq r\leq m
and
1\leq s\leq n
. Moreover,

[1,dp]
is an increasing disjoint union of the segments

\hat{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{1,1},\dots,\hat{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{m,1},\dots,\hat{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{1,n},\dots,\hat{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{m,n},

of sizes
|\hat{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{r,s}|=a_{r,s}p
as well as
an increasing disjoint union of the segments

\hat{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{1,1},\dots,\hat{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{1,n},\dots,\hat{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{m,1},\dots,\hat{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{m,n},

of sizes
\hat{|}{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{r,s}|=a_{r,s}p
,
and
\rho_{d}(w_{A})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\hat{\mathtt{B}}^{A}_{r,s}\nearrow\hat{\mathtt{T}}^{A}_{r,s}
for all
1\leq r\leq m
and
1\leq s\leq n
.

For
s=1,\dots,n
, we can write

\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\nu_{s},j_{s}}=\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(1,s)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(m,s)}
for
\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(r,s)}\in I^{a_{r,s}p}
. Then
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(r)}=\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(r,1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(r,n)}
.
Since
\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\nu_{s},j_{s}}
is cuspidal, we have
\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(1,s)}\in I^{\theta_{1,s}}
for
\theta_{s}
a sum of positive roots
\preceq\delta
.
Since
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}=\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(1,1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$k$}^{(1,n)}\in I^{\lambda_{1}\delta}
, we deduce using by Lemma
2.1.29
that each
\theta_{1,s}
is a multiple of
\delta
. Now, we deduce similarly that each
\theta_{2,s}
is a multiple of
\delta
, …, each
\theta_{m,s}
is a multiple of
\delta
.

Using Lemma
4.2.2
, we now deduce that
m=n
and there is
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that

\lambda_{r}=a_{r,\sigma^{-1}(r)}=\mu_{\sigma^{-1}(r)}
for all
r=1,\dots,n
. So
w=w_{\mu,\sigma}
, and the rest follows.
∎

In some frequently occurring special cases it will be convenient to use a special notation.
In the special case where
\text{\boldmath$j$}=j^{n}
, we denote

(4.2.6)

(\mu,j)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\mu,j^{n}).

In particular, we have

(4.2.7)

\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,j^{n}}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu,j^{n}}.

In the other special case where
\mu=\omega_{d}
and
\text{\boldmath$j$}=j_{1}\cdots j_{d}
, we denote

(4.2.8)

\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\omega_{d},\text{\boldmath$j$}}=\text{\boldmath$l$}^{j_{1}}\cdots\text{\boldmath$l$}^{j_{d}}.

4.2b.
Gelfand-Graev idempotents

For
(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda^{\operatorname{col}}(n,d)
, the corresponding
Gelfand-Graev idempotent
is

(4.2.9)

{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}}\in\hat{C}_{d}.

This is a divided power idempotent, cf. (
3.3.40
).
Occasionally, we will also need the non-divided power version:

(4.2.10)

\hat{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}}\in\hat{C}_{d}.

Recalling the special cases (
4.2.1
), (
4.2.7
) and (
4.2.8
), we also denote

(4.2.11)

{f}^{d,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}^{(d),j}=1_{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{d,j}},\quad{f}^{\mu,j}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\mu,j}}\quad\text{and}\quad{f}^{\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=1_{\text{\boldmath$l$}^{\text{\boldmath$j$}}}.

4.2c.
Gelfand-Graev idempotent truncation
C_{d}
of
\hat{C}_{d}

We have observed in §
4.2a

that the words
\{\hat{\text{\boldmath$l$}}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}
are distinct, so the
idempotents
\{\hat{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}
in
\hat{C}_{d}
are orthogonal. Hence the
idempotents
\{{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\mid(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)\}
in
\hat{C}_{d}
are orthogonal. Define the idempotent

(4.2.12)

{f}_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\sum_{(\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$})\in\Uplambda_{0}^{\operatorname{col}}(d)}{f}^{\mu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}\in\hat{C}_{d},

and the
Gelfand-Graev truncated cuspidal algebra

C_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}_{d}\hat{C}_{d}{f}_{d}.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\Uplambda(n,d)
.
Recalling (
4.1.12
),
we have the cuspidal parabolic subalgebra

\hat{C}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\hat{C}_{\lambda_{n}}=\hat{C}_{\lambda}\subseteq 1_{\lambda\delta}\hat{C}_{d}1_{\lambda\delta}.

Define the idempotent

{f}_{\lambda}={f}_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes{f}_{\lambda_{n}}\in\hat{C}_{\lambda}.

Truncating with this idempotent we get the parabolic subalgebra

(4.2.13)

\displaystyle C_{\lambda}\,\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\,\,{f}_{\lambda}\hat{C}_{\lambda}{f}_{\lambda}\,\,\subseteq\,\,{f}_{\lambda}C_{d}{f}_{\lambda}.

The isomorphism
\hat{C}_{\lambda}\cong\hat{C}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\hat{C}_{\lambda_{n}}
yields an isomorphism

C_{\lambda}\cong C_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes C_{\lambda_{n}}
, which we use to identify

(4.2.14)

C_{\lambda}=C_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes C_{\lambda_{n}}.

4.2d.
Functors relating
\hat{C}_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}
and
C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}

As a special case of (
2.2.8
) and (
2.2.9
), we have functors

(4.2.15)

\begin{split}{\mathpzc F}_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}_{d}\hat{C}_{d}\otimes_{\hat{C}_{d}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\hat{C}_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod},\\
{\mathpzc G}_{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\hat{C}_{d}{f}_{d}\otimes_{C_{d}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to\hat{C}_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}.\end{split}

More generally, for
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
, we have functors

(4.2.16)

\begin{split}{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\hat{C}_{\lambda}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to\hat{C}_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod},\\
{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}_{\lambda}\hat{C}_{\lambda}\otimes_{\hat{C}_{\lambda}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\hat{C}_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}\end{split}

with

(4.2.17)

\begin{split}{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}(W_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes W_{n})\simeq{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{1}}(W_{1})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{n}}(W_{n}),\\
{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})\simeq{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda_{1}}(V_{1})\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda_{n}}(V_{n})\end{split}

for
V_{k}\in\hat{C}_{\lambda_{k}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
and
W_{k}\in C_{\lambda_{k}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
,
k=1,\dots,n
. Note that

(4.2.18)

{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}}.

We will eventually prove that the functors
{\mathpzc F}_{d}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{d}
are quasi-inverse equivalences, in particular,
C_{d}
is graded Morita superequivalent to
\hat{C}_{d}
. If
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
and
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{F}>d
or
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{F}=0
, recalling the notation (
2.1.10
), this is known to be true even for the smaller idempotent truncation
{f}_{\omega_{d}}C_{d}{f}_{\omega_{d}}
(denoted
B_{d}
in
[
33
]
):

Theorem 4.2.19
.

[
33
, Theorems 4.2.55, 4.5.9]

Let
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
.
If
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{F}>d
or
\operatorname{char}\mathbb{F}=0
then the graded superalgebras
{f}_{\omega_{d}}C_{d}{f}_{\omega_{d}}
and
C_{d}
are graded Morita superequivalent to
\hat{C}_{d}
. In particular, the left module
C_{d}{f}_{\omega_{d}}
is a projective generator for
C_{d}
.

Corollary 4.2.20
.

The functors
{\mathpzc F}_{1}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{1}
are quasi-inverse equivalences.

Proof.

Note that
f_{1}=f_{\omega_{1}}
. By Theorem
4.2.19
, for
\Bbbk=\mathbb{F}
, the functors
{\mathpzc F}_{1}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{1}
are quasi-inverse equivalences. Now, the result for
\Bbbk=\mathcal{O}
follows using Lemma
2.2.20
.
∎

In Corollary
6.2.9
(ii), we will prove that the functors
{\mathpzc F}_{d}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{d}
are quasi-inverse equivalences by induction on
d
using Corollary
4.2.20
as the induction base. Until then, while working with a fixed
d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we make the inductive assumption:

Inductive Assumption 4.2.21
.

For all
c<d
, the functors
{\mathpzc F}_{c}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{c}
are quasi-inverse equivalences.

Lemma 4.2.22
.

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
have more than one non-zero part. In the presence of Inductive Assumption
4.2.21
, the functors
{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}
are quasi-inverse equivalences.

Proof.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
. By the assumption on
\lambda
, we have
\lambda_{k}<d
and
{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda_{k}}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{k}}
are quasi-inverse equivalences for all
k=1,\dots,n
by the Inductive Assumption. In particular, we have
\hat{C}_{\lambda_{k}}=\hat{C}_{\lambda_{k}}{f}_{\lambda_{k}}\hat{C}_{\lambda_{k}}
for all
k
.

Now, we always have
{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}W\simeq W
for
W\in C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}
, while
{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}V\simeq V
for
V\in\hat{C}_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}
is equivalent to
\hat{C}_{\lambda}=\hat{C}_{\lambda}{f}_{\lambda}\hat{C}_{\lambda}
. But

\hat{C}_{\lambda}{f}_{\lambda}\hat{C}_{\lambda}=\hat{C}_{\lambda_{1}}{f}_{\lambda_{1}}\hat{C}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\hat{C}_{\lambda_{n}}{f}_{\lambda_{n}}\hat{C}_{\lambda_{n}}=\hat{C}_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\hat{C}_{\lambda_{n}}=\hat{C}_{\lambda}

by the previous paragraph.
∎

4.2e.
Gelfand-Graev induction and restriction

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
.
As a special case of (
2.2.8
) and (
2.2.9
),
we have the functors of
Gelfand-Graev restriction and induction
:

(4.2.23)

\begin{split}{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}={f}_{\lambda}C_{d}\otimes_{C_{d}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod},\\
{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=C_{d}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}.\end{split}

If
\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
and
\lambda
is a refinement of
\mu
, we have more generally

\displaystyle{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{\mu}

\displaystyle={f}_{\lambda}C_{\mu}\otimes_{C_{\mu}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod},

\displaystyle{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=C_{\mu}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}-\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}.

We have transitivity of Gelfand-Graev induction:

Lemma 4.2.24
.

If
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\Uplambda(d)
such that
\lambda
is a refinement of
\mu
and
\mu
is a refinement of
\nu
then
{\mathrm{GGI}}^{\nu}_{\mu}\circ{\mathrm{GGI}}^{\mu}_{\lambda}\simeq{\mathrm{GGI}}^{\nu}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Using
{f}_{\mu}{f}_{\lambda}={f}_{\lambda}
, we have

C_{\nu}{f}_{\mu}\otimes_{C_{\mu}}C_{\mu}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V\simeq C_{\nu}{f}_{\mu}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V=C_{\nu}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

for
V\in C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}
.
∎

Recalling the functors (
4.2.15
) and (
4.2.16
), note that

(4.2.25)

{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}\simeq{\mathpzc F}_{d}\circ{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{d}\simeq{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}\circ{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{d}.

Indeed, for the first isomorphism, let
V\in C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}
. Using Lemma
4.1.9
(i) and the equality
1_{\lambda\delta}{f}_{\lambda}={f}_{\lambda}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathpzc F}_{d}\big({\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}\,{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}(V)\big)

\displaystyle={f}_{d}\hat{C}_{d}\otimes_{\hat{C}_{d}}\hat{C}_{d}1_{\lambda\delta}\otimes_{\hat{C}_{\lambda}}\otimes\hat{C}_{\lambda}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle\simeq{f}_{d}\hat{C}_{d}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle=C_{d}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle={\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}V.

The second isomorphism is proved similarly, but using Lemma
4.1.8
instead of Lemma
4.1.9
.

Even more generally,
for
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
such that
\lambda
is a refinement of
\mu
we have by a similar argument:

(4.2.26)

{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{\mu}\simeq{\mathpzc F}_{\mu}\circ{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{\mu\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{\mu}\simeq{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}\circ{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda\delta}^{\mu\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{\mu}.

Lemma 4.2.27
.

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
.
The functors
{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}
and
{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{d}
are exact.

Proof.

This is clear for
{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{d}
. For
{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}
, if
\lambda
has only one non-zero part, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, we have

{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}\simeq{\mathpzc F}_{d}\circ{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}
by (
4.2.25
), and the exactness of
{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}
follows from that of
{\mathpzc F}_{d}
,

{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}
and
{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}
, the last one coming from Lemma
4.2.22
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.28
.

For
\lambda\in\Uplambda(d)
and
V\in C_{\lambda}\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we have a functorial isomorphism
{\mathpzc G}_{d}\,{\mathrm{GGI}}^{d}_{\lambda}(V)\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}({\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}\,V)
of graded
\hat{C}_{d}
-supermodules.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathpzc G}_{d}\,{\mathrm{GGI}}^{d}_{\lambda}(V)

\displaystyle\simeq\hat{C}_{d}{f}_{d}\otimes_{C_{d}}C_{d}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle\simeq\hat{C}_{d}{f}_{d}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle=\hat{C}_{d}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle=\hat{C}_{d}1_{\lambda\delta}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle\simeq\hat{C}_{d}1_{\lambda\delta}\otimes_{\hat{C}_{\lambda}}\hat{C}_{\lambda}{f}_{\lambda}\otimes_{C_{\lambda}}V

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathrm{Ind}}_{\lambda\delta}^{d\delta}({\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}V),

as required.
∎

We have that
{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}
is left adjoint to
{\mathrm{GGR}}_{\lambda}^{d}
.
The following lemma partially describes the right adjoint:

Lemma 4.2.29
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\Uplambda(n,d)
and define
\lambda^{\mathrm{op}}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=(\lambda_{n},\dots,\lambda_{1})\in\Uplambda(n,d)
. Then for
W\in C_{d}\!\operatorname{-mod}
and
V_{k}\in C_{\lambda_{k}}\!\operatorname{-mod}
for
k=1,\dots,n
, there is a functorial isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{C_{\lambda}}({\mathrm{GGR}}^{d}_{\lambda}W\,,\,V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{C_{d}}(W\,,\,{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda^{\mathrm{op}}}^{d}(V_{n}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{1})).

Proof.

If
\lambda
has only one non-zero part, there is nothing to prove. Otherwise,
{\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}
is an equivalence with quasi-inverse
{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}
by Lemma
4.2.22
. Moreover,
{\mathpzc G}_{d}
is always left adjoint to
{\mathpzc F}_{d}
. So we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{C_{\lambda}}({\mathrm{GGR}}^{d}_{\lambda}W,V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{C_{\lambda}}(({\mathpzc F}_{\lambda}\circ{\mathrm{Res}}^{d\delta}_{\lambda\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{d})(W),V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{C}_{\lambda}}(({\mathrm{Res}}^{d\delta}_{\lambda\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{d})(W),{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda}(V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{n}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{C}_{\lambda}}(({\mathrm{Res}}^{d\delta}_{\lambda\delta}\circ{\mathpzc G}_{d})(W),{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{1}}V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{n}}V_{n})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{C}_{d}}({\mathpzc G}_{d}W,{\mathrm{Coind}}^{d\delta}_{\lambda\delta}({\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{1}}V_{1}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{n}}V_{n}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\hat{C}_{d}}({\mathpzc G}_{d}W,{\mathrm{Ind}}^{d\delta}_{\lambda^{\mathrm{op}}\delta}({\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{n}}V_{n}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{1}}V_{1}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{C_{d}}(W,{\mathpzc F}_{d}{\mathrm{Ind}}^{d\delta}_{\lambda^{\mathrm{op}}\delta}({\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{n}}V_{n}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda_{1}}V_{1}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{C_{d}}(W,{\mathpzc F}_{d}{\mathrm{Ind}}^{d\delta}_{\lambda^{\mathrm{op}}\delta}{\mathpzc G}_{\lambda^{\mathrm{op}}}(V_{n}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{1}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{C_{d}}(W,{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda^{\mathrm{op}}}^{d}(V_{n}\boxtimes\dots\boxtimes V_{1})),

where we have used Lemma
3.3.32
for the fifth isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 4.2.30
.

Let
\lambda\in\Uplambda(n,d)
and

\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\in I^{\lambda_{1}\delta}_{\operatorname{div}},\dots,\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}\in I^{\lambda_{n}\delta}_{\operatorname{div}}
be Gelfand-Graev words. Then there is an isomorphism of graded
C_{d}
-supermodules

\displaystyle C_{d}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathrm{GGI}}_{\lambda}^{d}\big(C_{\lambda_{1}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}\boxtimes\,\cdots\,\boxtimes\,C_{\lambda_{n}}1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}\big),

\displaystyle 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}

\displaystyle\mapsto{f}_{\lambda}\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}}\otimes\cdots\,\otimes 1_{\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}}.

Proof.

This follows easily from (
4.2.25
), Lemma
4.1.9
(ii), and the fact that the concatenation
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(1)}\cdots\text{\boldmath$i$}^{(n)}
is a Gelfand-Graev word.
∎

4.2f.
Gelfand-Graev Mackey

Here we establish a version of the Mackey Theorem for Gelfand-Graev induction and restriction.
First, we need the following lemma. Recall the embedding
\rho_{d}
from (
4.1.14
).

Lemma 4.2.31
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
and
x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
. Then in
\hat{C}_{d}
we have

1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}_{\mu}={f}_{\lambda}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}_{\mu}.

Proof.

Since
{f}_{\mu}
is a sum of Gelfand-Graev idempotents of the form
{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
for a composition
\nu
being a refinement of a composition
\mu
, it suffices to show that for any refinement
\nu\in\Uplambda(n,d)
of
\mu
we have
1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}={f}_{\lambda}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}.

Since
\nu
is a refinement of
\mu
, the assumption
x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
implies
x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\nu}
. Now Lemma
4.2.5
implies that for some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we have
x=w_{\nu,\sigma}
and

1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}=1_{\lambda\delta}\hat{f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}.

By Lemma
4.2.4
, if
\sigma\nu
is not a refinement of
\lambda
then
1_{\lambda\delta}\hat{f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}=0
and so both
1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
and
{f}_{\lambda}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}={f}_{\lambda}1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}
are zero.

So we may assume that
\sigma\nu
is a refinement of
\lambda
. Then
1_{\lambda\delta}\hat{f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}=\hat{f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}
, so

1_{\lambda\delta}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}=\hat{f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}.

Now, repeatedly using Lemma
3.3.44
, we deduce that

\hat{f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}={f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}={f}_{\lambda}{f}^{\sigma\nu,\sigma\text{\boldmath$j$}}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}}={f}_{\lambda}\psi_{\rho_{d}(x)}{f}^{\nu,\text{\boldmath$j$}},

as required.
∎

Recall the notation of §
2.1c
. In particular, given
\lambda,\mu\in\Uplambda(d)
and
x\in{}^{\lambda}{\mathscr{D}}_{d}^{\mu}
we have the compositions
\lambda\cap x\mu
and
x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu

in
\Uplambda(d)
. Moreover, the corresponding parabolic algebras
C_{\lambda\cap x\mu,\delta}
and
C_{x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu,\delta}
are naturally isomorphic via an isomorphism

C_{\lambda\cap x\mu,\delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu,\delta}

which permutes the components. Composing with this isomorphism we get a functor

C_{x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to C_{\lambda\cap x\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ V\mapsto{}^{x}V.

Recalling from (
3.3.24
) the similar functor
\hat{C}_{x^{-1}\lambda\cap\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}\to\hat{C}_{\lambda\cap x\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod},\ W\mapsto{}^{x}W
, note that for
V\in C_{\mu}\!\operatorname{-mod}
, we have

(4.2.32)
